NOTES BY " MUST."

Jack Frost played Warrington a scurvy trick last Saturday when, by means of a special effort between 12 noon and 2 p.m. he made the ground so bard that the match with Wakefield Trinity had to be postponed. There had been a keen frost in the night, but during Saturday forenoon the ground was playable, only to go harder as the sun declined in the early afternoon. The teams and most of the spectators made their way to the ground, only to be disappointed. There was a goodly contingent cf Warringt,niaus from Wakefield, and they naturally felt particularly aggrieved.

The match has been re-arranged for Monday after- noon at 2-15. Warrington are sure to lose heavily through the loss of a Saturday gate.

It is to be hoped that bttter luck attends the fixture to-day, for there is no more attractive match on the card than the one with Huddersfield. The champions are still undefeated in the League, but I have no faith in them going through the season without the loss of a point. Neither have I any faith in the Wirepullers as the team likely to make the first breach in that proud 100 per cent. Time was when they could be relied upon for a superlative effort against the mighty, but the team of to-day is not so powerful as the side which contained Jack Fish. I remember Oldham coming to Wilderspool with a clean record of ten or eleven successive victories, but they left the certificate at Warrington. The unexpected , may happen—that is one of the joys of the game— but I shall be surprised and delighted if Warrington prove themselves equal to putting a spoke in the , Huddersfield wheel.

The Warrington men should be both fresh and keen. They have had plenty of rest lately, and as training has been rigorously atteoded to they should be able to stay the pace alright. Where they are likely to be left is by the pace and cleverness of the Huddersfield rear division, than which there has probably oelosr been a finer combination. Given a fine day, and the match should be worth missing a wedding to see.

Unfortunately Warrington will again have to take the field without their Skipper. Renwick is reported " fit " and in training, but his shoulder still gives some trouble and it is thought unwise to risk him in such a big engagement. The team well, otherwise, be at full strength.

The selection is: Jolley, full tack ; Brooks. Dickin- son, Tranter and Mclntyre, three-rivarters ; Nicholas and Rushton, half backs; G Thomas, R Thomas, Cheaters, Skelhorne, Cos and Fearnley forwards.

[he Huddersfie Id team nil] be : Bollard, full back ; Rosenfeld, Gleason, Wagstaffe, and Moorhouse, or Kitchen, threequarters ; Grey and Davies, half backs ; Clarke, Chilcott, Gronow, Higsan, Langstaff, Swinden or linsworth, forwards.

Huddersfield possess at least two men whi may bp considered perfect in their respective positions, and I should like Warrington supporters to take note of their play. I refer to Wagstaffe, the brainiest centre who has ever played the N.U. game, and Clarke, the Cumberland forward. The latter is a slashing player and a certain scorer near the line.

The "A "team make their first visit to Yorkshire in combination football to-day. They are due at Fartown to meet Huddersfield "A," and should he fully equal to giving the home lot a good game. If our local lads wruld only play the same game that they did against Wigan Bighfield, they might spring a surprise at Fartown.

The "A " team will be : Brown, full back ; Clare, Hudson, Jordan and Davies, three-quarters: A Brown and Daintith, half backs; Naylor. Critchley, Bevan, Arnold, Harmer and Gibbs,f forwards.

LANCASHIRE v. YORKSHIRE.

The Lancashire County Northern Union authorities Lave maintained the principle which they adopted some little time ago of selecting county-born players for county championship games, and for the match with Yorkshire at Oldham on December 16th the following side has been chosen : J Sharrock (Wigan) ; G Sharpies (Dewsbury), H Taylor (Widnes), J Dobson (Barrow), H Curwen (Wigan) ; F Gleave (Wigan), S Watton (Runcorn) ; R Ramsdale (Wigan), J Dearden (Rochdale Hornets), A Lee (Leigh), NV Longworth (Oldham), J Bailey (Swinton), W Reid (Widnes). Referee, R Robinson (Bradford).

Compared with the side selected to meet Cumber- ' land in September, when Lanerbhire lost, only Sharrock, Taylor, Gleave, Ramadale, Lee and Long- worth maintain their places. Tom Johnson, Harris, and Flannagan are left out of the three-quarter line, and Sharpies, Dobson and Curwen gain county honours for the first time. Gleave was selected for the last match, but owing to an injury i.e did not play, Trenwith, of St. Helens, deputizing. Thus it can be said tkat both the half-back positions are changes, Ganley giving way to the veteran Walton, of Runcorn. In the forwards Winstanley, Parr and Preacott are omitted, and Reid, who has previously gained county honours as a three-quarter back, Dearden aud Bailey come in. The latter played his first League game of the season on Saturday last. It is not a formidable aide, but the mode of selection is to be commended.

The Yorkshire team (which defeated Cumberland on Thursday at Hull by 19 points to 5) will be : Carmichael (Hull Kingston Hovers); Jenkinson, W Batten (Hunslet), Wagstaffe, Moorhouse, (Hudde Ts- fie Id) ; Smith (Hunslet), Slater (Wakefield Trinity) ; Mann, Moore, Haskins (Hull Kingston Rovers), =sinks, Smalea (Hunslet), and Burrall (Bradford).

LANCAbHIRE CUP FINAL. It was decided to play the postponed Lancashire Cup final between Rochdale Hornets, the holders, and Wigan at Salford on Wednesday, December 11, the kick-off to take place at 2.39. The Mayor of Salford is to be asked to present the cup and medals.

NORTH-WESTERN LEAGUE. Barrow A ..... 1 2 8 v Barrow Holker at. Old Boss RUNCORN v. BARROW: HOME SIDE'S IMPROVED SECOND HALF. On a hard and treacherous ground these two learns tried conclusions at Irwell-lane. Runcorn, though doing most of the pressing in the first half, were six points behind at the interval as the result of a couple of easy tries obtained by Gifford and Barrett. In the first instance the home defence was caught napping badly, and in the second case Stevenson, the home full back, was utterly deceived by the bounces of a lively ball. Neither try was improved upon. In the second half both teams appeared to have become more accus- tomed to the ground, and some good play was seen. The home team, however, held the upper band, and tries were run in by Hughes, Hoxworth, and Thornton, the last of which was a capital finish to a brilliant round of passing. Result :—Runcorn 1 goal 3 tries al points), Barrow 2 tries (6 points). Teams and officials:— Runcorn—Stevenson, back ; Moran, Creevey. Hughes and Thornton, three-quarter backs ; Hoxworth and Walton, half backs ; Padbury, Peacock, Richardson, Jones, Donohue and Williams, forwards. Barrow—Morgan, back; Bernier, Gifford, Hill and Williams, three-quarter backs ; Barrett and Baines, half backs; Knowles, Brocklebank, Parr, Tomlinson, Gould, and Fletcher, forwards. Referee, W McCutcheon, Oldham. Touch judges, E Bennett, Liverpool, and E J Hannaford, St Helens. LANCASHIRE JUNIOR CUP. RTLANDS' BEATEN IN FOURTH ROUND. Rylands' Recreation met their Waterloo on Saturday, when they were knocked out of the Lancashire Junior Cup '4th Round) by Pemberton. The match took place at the Gorsey-lane ground before a large crowd, and the defeat of the homsters by 2 tries to 1 was a disappoint- ment. Rylands were short of Hennessey, who has been signed on by Salford. The opening phases of the game were fought out at half-way, but eventually the Recrea- tion forced matters and raided the Pemberton goal. The defence held out, however, and a spell of exchange kicking resulted in the visitors attacking, but like the home lot the Pemberton hacks lacked finish Bramhall was sorely tried on several occasions, and twice had to take advantage of the saving clause. Lancaster came near scoring after a dashing run. Bramhall relieved with a ponderous sick. End-to-end play was witnessed and though Rylands' again as,umed the aggressive they were net able to break through and at the interval no tangible score had been recorded. In the second half Pemberton's weight told a tale, and the forwards overplayed Rylands, though the latter made a fine tight. Final : Pemberton, two tries (6 points) ; Wands 1 try (3 points).

BYAIM v BROADIIEATH.

At Lymm, on Saturday. Both teams had several changes from the previous week, when they met at Navigation-road, Broadheath, and considering the state of the ground, a good game was witnessed. Wilson, for Lymm, scored two good goals in the first half, Preston being helpless, Hinde also defending well at full-back. Broadheath were awarded a penalty for an infringeme- in the area, from which the centre-half scored. The visitors were on the defence during all the second half, but Pearson, in goal, and the full backs were not to be beaten, and the result was : Lymm, 2 goals ; Broadheath 1 goal.

STOCKTON HEATH V CADISHEAD WESLEYANS.

At Stockton Heath on Saturday, and after a lively opening the Heathens opened the score from a penalty. The visitors retaliated, and after several efforts Taylor equalized for them. Gradually the home team assumed the aggressive, and before the interval increased their score with a couple of goals, the last one being a particularly fine effort by Lockhart. Half-time : Stockton Heath 3, Cadishead Wesleyans I. In the second half Stockton Heath had by far the best of matters and increased their lead. Result : Stockton Heath 5, Cadishead Wesleyans 1. WARRINOTON C.L.B. V WEAVER REFINUFG Co.

At Greppenhall. The Brigade Lads went off with great dash and in the early stages bad the hest of matters. From a penalty they opened the score (their first goal in the competition.) After this reverse. the Refiners settled down to their game and overplayed the plucky Juniors : Result : Weaver Refining Co., 6 goals ; Warrington C.L.8., 2 goals.

MOORE AND DARESBERY V CROSFIELDS.

Owing to some mistake in the arrangements made for a ground on Saturday last Crosfields again visited Moore. The memory of the severe beating they sus- tained three weeks previoint served to put great spirit into the visitors ; with this and a slightly altered team they easily ay. nged their defeat. From a corner near half-time Crosflelds scored a goal. In the second half two further goals were registered for the visitors and one for the home team. Score : Crosfields, 3 goals; Mc ore and Daresbury, 1 goal.

CROSFIELD'S CLUB. Crosfleld's met Heaton Park at home last Saturday. The game commenced with Crostield's playing only eight men, the other three missing the train and only arriving at half-time. The ground was partly flooded and frozen and really unfit for hockey. Handicapped though they were, Crosfleld's played a good game, but their defence was overwhelmed. _

" At half-time the three missing players arrived and took part in the game. This put the teams on a more equal footing and strenuous efforts were now made to reduce the lead, but only with partial success, the game ending in a win for Heaton Park by 7 goals to 1, To-day Crosfleld's meet Clayton at Clayton. CROSS COUNTRY RUNNING.

(BY PF.Do.) On Saturday last Warrington A.O met Salford Harriers at Bentley. A pack of 35, including 15 from Warrington, followed a 42 miles trail, laid by three of Salford assisted by F Price, Warrington, which owing to the frost bound nature of the country was principally on the roads. Turton, Smith, Gregson, Vose, Scott, Shadwell and Fitton wealth() order at the first obstacle where the field strung out, and over the first portion of the course Salford appeared content for Warrington to make the running relying on their pa3e to tell at the finish, but two miles from home Vose let out a reef and was followed by Shadwell, Gregson end Turton, right to the end where Vose had an aavantage of 150 yards with Shadwell, a similar distance in front of Gregson and Turton, who finished close together. About 100 yards away Smith and Scott had only a few yards the beat of the argu- ment with Nurse, who ran splendidly for a novice, and Salford got the next 4 home before Jeffrey Watson who ran into tbe Warrington count and earned his club novice medal in meritorious style. Vose's time was 26min 30seca and the placings were as follows: -Warrington : C Vose 1, F Shadwell 2, A Gregson 3, T F Turton 4, J Nurse 7, J Watson 12 ; total 29 points. Salford : P Smith 5. W Scott 6, W F Robinson 8' J W Fitton 9, J Marshall 10, H Firth 11; total, 49 points- a 20 points win for Warrington. Runcorn Tally Ho had 12 starters for their Junior handicap which resulted in a popular victory for a first season runner after a race over a stiff course of 62 miles. Details :-1, A Radley (6min 40secs start) time 4lmin fOsecs ; 2, N Street (325), 42-30; 3, G Woolley (3min), 4210; W Thompson (smm) 43-0 ; 5, J Poole (4-25), 43-50 ; 6, T Rathbone (5-25), 43-50 1.5 ; 7, J Derbyshire, 8, C E Escott, 9, J Yarwood, 10,11 Helsby, 1 i J Manning, 12, E Pye. Sutton were at home to Leigh who supplied 17 of a pack of 48. Result after a 5 miles race :- Sutton : J Smart 1, D Brown 2, W Barrow 4, A Welding 6, E Harrison 8, S Welding 9, 30 points ; Leigh : J Dickenson 3, \V Elmer 5, J Simm 7, E Rawlinson 15, P Monks 19, 0 Keegan 20. 69 points.

'FORTHCOMING TEAMf RACE.

_,-....-_!._!,•-•_

At Liverpool on Monday last 'the West Lancashire Association scrutinised and passed, subject to two reservations, the following 14 teams for a five miles race at Victoria Park, Latchford, on the 14th inst : Warrington A.C" A " Teato.—J Hanson, J Nurse, W ' Walters J Watson H Woods G Wakefield ; reserves, N Stafford, S Siddria. Warrington A.O " B " Team.—J Hayes, S Siddall, N Stafford, J Thorniley, G Wakefield, H Woods; reserves, H Payton, F Price. St. Helens Sacred Heart, Wallasey, St. Helens Parish Church, Lancaster Primrose, St. Helens Recreation, Runcorn, Ravenhead, Sefton, Sutton, West Cheshire, Liverpool Boundary "A " team, Liverpool Boundary " B " team. No less than eight of these teams sre spoken of as certain winners, and for the honour of first man home, 11 candidates present claims which have evoked declara- tions of confidence by their most intimate friends. As usual, the locals are supposed to be out of the reckoning, but their supporters expect them to come into the picture and occupy a prominent position at the finish, when many thrilling struggles for position should be seen. Three laps inside the Park and three larger laps, including a portion of the land outside will be covered, and Warrington A.C. who have been entrusted with the management, hope to prxsent local sportsmen with a treat. TO-DAY'S FIXTURES. St. Helens League combined run from St. Helena Parish Church headquarters, Warrington A.C. meet at their pavilion at 2.45 p.m., and in view of the race next week and the Christmas handicap the week following, all members are requested to attend. • COLOSSAL POST OFFICE FIGURES.

The figures given by the Postmaster-General in his report for the year 1911-12 can only be described as colossal. The number of postal packages delivered in the United Kingdom during the year was 5,483,000, and the number of tolegrams sent 89,200,000. Postcards have more than doubled in the last ten years.

RIFLE CLUB.

ANGLING.

WARRINGTON WORKINGMEN'S ASSOCIATION N.A. CENTRE.

Saturday last saw the end of the season, so far as marking up specimens was concerned, and on going through the list we cannot say it has been a successful year. Few anglers were out the latter part of last week, and those who ventured far away from home were disappointed with the results, with the exception of P Jolley, who again visited Llangollen, and returned with three dace that stand Ist, 2nd and 3rd, but not at bead- qnarters where they were not weighed. The weights are not to hand so we presume they were not big enough to upset the weight already recorded. We find all the specials offered for Norton length have been captured and Mr T Singleton takes the lion's share, he scores in roach, gudgeon and the monthly prizes. Our delegate will make his report on Monday evening neat and we hope he has some good news for us. We hear that the general meeting of Northern Anglers held at Preston was got through in good time and that there were some very important changes made. However, the report will be down for next week, when we shall all bear more about it.

CRICKET IN SOUTH AUSTRALIA.

A Warringtonian's Experiences.

PLAYING PITCH ON A ROAD.

Mr. H. S. Atkin, formerly of Latchford, who went to Hallett, near Adelaide, South Australia, this year, sends us the following interesting account of a cricket match in the wilds.

Being keenly interested in cricket in the Old Country and more especially in Warrington I thought it would be interesting to my many friends in War- rington and Latchford, to know something cf recent cricket here which I have seen. I don't think it is possible for English people to realise the keen interest taken in village cricket leagues here, and the following facts will be interesting to many. I was spending a few days with my brother, who is a farmer in the district and secretary of a Cricket Club, and on Saturday October 12th my brother asked me to go with him to the ground to prepare the pitch and have a practice. Judge my surprise when I found that we had to drive a distance of five miles, and greater was my surprise when we reached the place where the matches were to be played and found the pitch was to be prepared on the road and what a road it is—quarter mile in width. The position of the playing pitch was decided at one of the committee meetings and is the most central positim they could get. Most of the players travel from 4 to 6 miles. Several turned up and brought with them a plough, spade, broom, &e, We had • taken the cocoa matting, which was to be the playing pitch, and the playing tackle. You may wonder what the various, things were for. The plough was for making the boundary line, and a perfect oval shaped ridge was made. The shovels were for chopping the long grass off and making just sufficient room to put down the- matting. When all was prepared we had two hours practice, returning home at dusk. On October 19th, the first match was played between the team my brother- and his two sons are members of, Willalo (home) and Mount Bryan. I went to see the match as a spectator, but the visiting team being short of an Umpire I was asked te fill that position, which I did. The Mount Bryanites had I driven a distance of 14 miles. You may ask, why not take train. The answer is simple, the nearest station to the playing pitch is 12 miles. Willalo won the toss and decided to bat and the cricket all round was excellent. Don't forget that most, I think all, were farmers and during the morning of the match day I had seen some of them cultivating, stone picking and other jobs equally as heavy and yet these fellows bowled, batted and fielded as keenly as if they had been playing a test match. Willalo hatted splendidly. One hundred and four was scored before the first wicket fell and at 208 for 5 wickets the innings was declared. The Mount Bryanites opened their innings well, scoring nearly 40 before the first wicket fell. Then a collapse followed the whole side being out for 76. On October 26th the Willalo team had to travel a distance by road in conveyances (Buggis, Sulkies &c,) of 25 miles each way to a place called Burra, landing home at 12.30 midnight, the result being another win for the Willaloos,scoring 137 for 7 and Aberdeen, a suburb of Burra, 100 all out. Now, you may ask where do the spectators come from. You ill be susprised when I tell you that although only two small corrugated huts can be seen from the field of play quite a respectable crowd come to watch the match and shout for the favourites. During the match at Willalo I noticed amongst the spectators a slashing of whips and sticks and could tell that a snake had made its appearance, and after the match I found out that two snakes had been killed one 6 feet long and 8 or 10 inches in circumference the other being a bit smaller. This made 6 snakes I had known killed in the district. Wallalo is about 120 miles inland from Adelaide. I wonder what some of the players I know in Warrington and Latchford would think of cricket under these conditions. I have never seen men enjoy the game so much as these splendid fellows or any who played the game more fairly or more gentle- manly.

POISONING THE WELLS.

THE PLEA FOR CONSCRIPTION.

GAPS IN LORD ROBERTS' POINT OF VIEW.

Writing in the "Daily News," Mr. H. W. Massingham, the noted publicist and editor of "The Nation" makes some pertinent criticisms of the dangerous drift towards conscription in this country of men like Lord Roberts. Mr. Massingham opens with a reference to the war in the Balkans, and adds: "Here, then, is no ordinary case of war. It is no conflict between two equally civilized communities, that not only do each other no wrong, but in countless ways support, com- plement and nurture each other, and in their diversity and richness of gifts give hope that Europe and the world may on some distant day grow into the perfect stature of State manhood. That is the conflict that LORD ROBER.TS'S DEVILfSH SPEECHES .invite—speeches that no country but our own would tolerate from a soldier who is practically inciting to mutiny and the dis- Integration of the forces of the Crown. I am told that Germany has its Robertses,

and that particular example of this form of public enemy, General von Bernhardi, has just written a book inspired by the same theory of English policy as Lord Roberts holds of Germany. That is perfectly true, and if the two countries could maroon these two men (and their supporting scribblers) till statesmanship had time and breath to adjust the minute differences that separate the two Empires, all would be well. OUT FOR CONSCRIPTION. But how much better is the reality than the fiction about the relationship of the two countries. A critical situation arises, and Germany and England, in spite of their special attachments, find it possible and easy to work together for peace. No interests intervene; no bad blood arises. The great military Power, the great Naval Empire, can between them assure the tranquillity of Europe. What an excellent and truthful commentary on the true " Anglo-German situation"! How completely it falsifies the malignant attribution of a diabolic destiny, urging each to the ruin of the other! But it is not enough to moralize; the Government and the country must act. Lord Roberts is out for conscription. I do not think that he dares deny this. He has rejected and will reject every scheme for strengthening the military forces of the country because he thinks it may interfere With that cherished end. That intention applies, of course, to his contemptible and unpatriotic attempt to destroy the Terri- torials—an effort which I hear is exciting the deepest indignation among the officers and men of that force. But I believe that he applies the same tactics, with the same avowed purpose, to the vague schemes of the 'Spectator." They fall just a little short of the universal forced service—with a direct view to a continental war—on which Lord Roberts is bent. Therefore they do not suit his book. TO COUNTER-WORK LORD ROBERTS. How far his last speech transgresses even the lax code of propriety that he has hitherto Observed I do not know. But any Govern- trient which wants peace is bound to repudi- ate him, and to take what measures it can to counter-work him. Let the Tory party take' Up conscription if they dare. They are on the eve of that fatal step. We need not move a hair's-breath to stop them, for it ensures them twenty more years of Opposition. But the Government and the Liberal party have Other duties and policies. They know that the preparation of a great continental army means war with Germany. They krow and have proved to the satisfaction of every man with a capacity to reason a simple case of strategy that such an army is useless for defence. They know, too, that it would cripple our finances and banish all hope• of a definite and serious rise in the standard of

our people's life. They must therefore counter- work and destroy this man's crusade for forced service. He has a loose but a plausible tongue, and he has many admirers among the half-baked and the half-taught. For that purpose they had better take the Territorial Force in hand, and give it all it wants to make it a good, serviceable, well- kept, efficient body. The country will not grudge them the money, especially when it realizes that it is spent to save it from slavery and war.

.„ • •

===== 0002601_19121207_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
LORD HALDANE'S REPLY TO LORD ROBERTS.

Speaking at the dinner of the Eighty Club on Friday evening Lord Haldane said : " Hitless you are a strategist you cannot fashion forth plat a of organisation for the defence of the country. What I miss in the Lord Roberts of to-day is just that understanding of the point of view of the seaman and of the statesman which is absolutely vital if you are to make a proper military orguaisation. (Cheers) You can only do that if you know very clearly the purpose for which you want the military organisation.

TIIE FUNDAnENTAL PRINCIPLE OF STRATEGY is the command of the sea. Therefore the navy must come first. Next must come that oversea army for India, for Africa, and for the distant parts of the world which can only be raised on a voluntary basis. No other nation has ever succeeeded in raising such an airily on any basis. Finally, there is our organisation for home defence which, so far as it is military, need not be on the same basis of high readiness for immediate capacity to meet the enemy man for man. For that reason it seems to me treason to spend a great deal of your money or to devote your highest energies upon merely the military part of home defence. You must have something like your Territorial Force. (Cheers.) You have to deal with whatever may get through your doublo naval wall. But to put that first, or to imperil the navy for the sake of that, is to make a most profound strategical blunder. Make your Territorial Force as efficient as you can, spend what money you have .to spend upon it, but first make sure of the cammand of the sea. That is why I condemn the movement of the National Service League. First, because it doesn't mean what it says. Most soldiers hanker for compulsory service which they see at work in other countries woich have a different strategical problem from ours. What they want is not six months' trained men, but a Conti- nental army with three years' training for cavalry and artillery and two years for infantry. That will cost you two thiags—(l) your professional over-sea army, which you cannot recruit at the same rate and basis; (I) an enormous amount of money, ten or fifteen millions addition at the least to the Army Estimates. In the second place, those who want that army scout and laugh at the military programme of the National Service League. lam not now speaking of the colonels who inhabit the bow windows of the clubs and who retired in the time of Queen Victoria, but of the expert soldiers with whom I used to work. But I have

A WORSE OBJECTION to Lord Roberts's scheme than the military one. If you carried out his programme you would find inevitably that you were short of money for both the necessities of the army and navy. You cannot keep up both. I am for spending that money upon the fleet. I con- sider that any policy put forward on any basis that is uncsrtain about that is the policy not of a strategist but of an amateur. Therefore, I record my protest against the strategical principles of the National Sarvice League. What I comp!ain of in this matter of compulsory service is what I complain of in a spore of other things. The Opposition is drifting without any policy. Their newspapers and some of their leaders have drifted into compulsory service, but they have not any idea how they can make a programme or get the country to assent to it. That is also true about insurance, about education, and about much else besides. The party has been inflicted with the incurable weakness of not knowing its own mind ever since it met with a deadly accident some nine years ago. The coachman lost control of the horses, and they bolted with the coach.. The result was an accident, and the coach is still under repair. We must hope they have learned something, and surely from all these mi,fortunes they must have emerged as wiser mon. (Cheers and laughter)

===== 0002601_19121207_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
CONSCRIPTIONISTS CONDEMNED.

General E C Bethune, Inspector-General of the Territorial Force, visited Chester on klondah and inspected the local members of the National Reserve on the Castle-square. Later he reopened the Drill Ilall in the presence of a distinguished compcny. At the outset General Bethune declared the building open formally. General Mackinnon said that the Territorial Force at the present time was going through a very difficult

'rom Reynolds' Newspaper,

moment of its career. It was not for him to talk of the criticisms that were levelled at it. It was their duty more than ever that day to do all they possibly could for the force. And more Van that, it was the duty of all of them, hether of the Regular Army or of the County Associations, or whatever they belonged to that had anything to do with the Territorial Forces, not to relax their efforts to get the force as full as they possibly could. Colonel Bromley-Davenport, D. 5.0., chairman of the Cheshire Territorial Association, in seconding, said this was the time when they required official encourage- ment—at a time which was very critical in the history and development of the Territorill Forces. Just at this juncture, when they were faced with the task of replacing those men Rho had completed their four years' service, the enemies of the force bad taken that opportunity to make an attack which WM ungenerous and unjult. (Loud applau3e).

CRITICS' DILEMMA. There were some critics—he did not include Lord Roberts— nho wanted to destroy the Territorial Forces, and they did not know what they proposed to put in their place. Ile had many friends in the National Service League, and he had often asked them to explain what they were aiming at, and he had never yet received any clear or satisfactory reply. If they wanted Con- scription on the Continental pattern, they were going to creates gigantic force, numbering with is reserves millions, at a cost which was going to be enormous, Lind when they corsidered that that cost bad to be added to tha cost of the Regular Army and the increasing cost of the Navy the cost was going ito be prohibitive. They could not nan island nation like theirs afford to be both supreme at sea and have a standing Army of such a character as Continental countries which had no seaboard. What he would like to ask his friends of the National Service League was did they intend their compulsorily enlisted men to have obligation of service abroad ? If they did, he (Colonel Davenport) rnaintainta'ned that the people of this country a ould never tolerate anything of the kind, and, in that case, it was an obligation absolutely foreign and abhorrent to their national character. (llear,hear.) If they only intended it for service in the British Isles, then he would say they were proceeding to create at an enormous cost a gigantic fighting machine, which, under no conceivable circumstances, could they have any use for. Ile believed the Territorial Force was capable of being developed into such a position, and it would be able to provide everything necessary for home defence, especially when it was supplemented by the !National Reserve, which had made wonderful progress. (Hear, hear). General Bethune' in appealing for more recruits, said he did not object to outdoor games. But, for heaven's sake, let them be men first. Ile would like to make it a law that no one be allowed to amuse himself until lie had done his duty. (Hear, hear).

DWELLING IN UNITY.

POLITICAL OPPONENTS CONDUCT RELIGIOUS SERVICE. Unique Gathering.

Political partisans conducted a unique service at Knutsford on Sunday. The service bad been arranged by the Knutsford Brotherhood, and Conservative M.P. and Liberal candidate shared in conducting it. The meeting place—the local Wesleyan church—was never more crowded. Colonel Alan J Sykes, my., presided and Mr P M Oliver delivered the address. The good offices of the political agents were also utilised. Mr Grimshaw Duckworth, the Conservative ;nd Unionist agent, singing the solos, while Mr H Cliffe, the Liberal agent, read a portion of Scripture. The Chairman said he understood there were many present who in their daily lives professed various denominations of worship. There were men who, outside that service, when dealing with the politics of the day, professed widely different opinions, but they were all assembled that day because they belonged to one Brotherhood. And the Brotherhood to which they belonged was one which professed a belief in God. He was not one of those who took particular notice as to the particular form of worship a person attached himself to. The most important part was that they should worship Him with all sincerity of heart. The outward forms were not so important. Alluding to the war, Colonel Sykes said they must deplore war no matter in what form. But a war was not without its lessons, and the lesson the present war taught him was this: That they ought always to give due respect to tho opinions of their fellow-brothers They might differ, but let them differ in as agreeable. a manner as possible. Mr Oliver, who took for his address the subject of Charles Kingsley, said he supposed that everyone who was interested in social and political questions had before their eyes an ideal city. It was the Church, and the Church only, that could make men fit for an ideal state by preaching the true freedom, the true equality, and the true brotherhood.

COMMERCIAL FAILURES.—According to " Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," the number of commercial failures published in Great Britain and Ireland during the five weeks ending Saturday, Nov. 30th, was 823. The number in the corresponding five weeks of last year was 837, showing a decrease 14, being a net decrease in 1912, of 429.

===== 0002601_19121207_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
A TAME LION.

"KING CARSON" AT WARRINGTON.

HOME RULE GENTLY HANDLED.

King Carson's visit to Warrington was shorn of much of the pomp and circumstance which attend his progresses through his own special dominions in Ulster. There was no guard of honour to give the Royal salute, no playing of the National Anthem, and no wooden cannon. Instead the leader of the Ulsterites made his appearance to the strains of" See the conquering hero comes "—following the nicely chosen musical prophecy of" Let me likes soldier fall"—and the waving of many miniature Union Jacks by the women tariff reformers who, with a patience worthy of a better cause, Lad remained on a stand at the open-air meeting, in a bitterly cold wind, with an occasional fall of snow, for upwards of two hours before Sir Edward made his appearance. The "great open air demonstration" must have been intensely disappointing to those Tories who had been taking it at the valuation suggested by the placards which announced it as the principal gathering, with the Parr Hall meet- ing—in small type—as, apparently, a kind of after- thought. As a matter of fact, the gositions were reversed. The Parr Hall was the scene of the meeting and the outdoor assembly was the overflow at which , those who had not gained admission to the hall had the felicity of enduring a succession of speeches from les er lights with a bald ten minutes' discourse from the man whom they had all come to bear. That open- air meeting was an uninspiring affair. The small open space at the rear of Patten Hall proved more than ample to accommodate the crowd and even the fact that it was Union Jack Day and that the Tories had, according to the bills so freely displayed, Wren the Sivereign under their special protection, failed to awaken more than a flicker of enthusiasm. There was obviously a difficulty in keeping the ball rolling until Sir Edward's advent and many of the audience became so weary of waiting that they disgustedly took tltir departure. It was Union Jack Day, but a stranger might have been pardoned for imagining that, by some strange con- fusion of dates, Warrington was celebrating Guy Fawkes' Day, for, before the demonstration, rockets some appropriately showering green sparks—were sent up to remind the populace of the coming of Sir Edward.

As was to be expected of a meeting representing two counties Conservative contemporary puts the number of delegates 3ad contingents from the various ConservativeWorkingmen's Associations of Lancashire and Cheshire at upwards of nine hundred—the Parr Hall was packed as ith men. To relieve the tedium of waiting, patriotic airs were sung and Walker's Brewery band—unkindly stowed away under the platform—played martial F elections. A few seconds before Sir Edward Carson stepped on to the platform they had been valiantly pouring forth " Let me like a soldier fall " ir.tended no doubt, to conjure up harrowing visions of battlefields in Ulster—but when the ex-Solicitor General was sighted they considerately heralded him with " See the conquering hero comes.' The decoration of the hall was, of course, intended to emphasise the Tory monopolisation of the Union Jack, and above the platform appeared a large device worded thus : "No Surrender! No Home Rule! God Save the King !" Sr Gilbert Greenall, Bart.—whom the Press tickets for the meeting described as "M.P."—was in the chair, and offered Sir Eclaard Carson welcome to the "grand old Tory borough of Warrington." The audience of men chuckled hugely when, by force of habit, Sir Gilbert addressed them as "ladies and gentlemen "—a litCe slip which later gave Mr Harold Smith M.P., an opportunity of declaring humble allegiance to the knight of Walton. There was a tornado of cheering when Sir Edward Carson rose to address the meeting. His strongest enemies will admit that he is a strik- ing and dominating p3rsonality—that prominent chin, so beloved of the caricaturist, plainly denotes the determination of the man and his keen eyes have a magnetism which the Ulsterite finds hard to resist. As an orator, however, he does not 'realize expectations. His style is heavy, entirely lacking in sparkle or originality—his methods are those of the bludgeon rather than of the rapier. At times, when declaiming upon the wickedness of the Government in desiring t " cast out "the Ulsterites, he appeared almost on the verge of tears—th;s type of emotionalism is a familiar feature of Sir Edward's platform style. For the most part, however, the speech failed to stir the listeners, who only waxed over-enthusiastic when, on Sir Edward demanding another appeal to the country, Mr Harold Smith sprang to his feet and led a round of cheering. Sir Edward began his speech with a reference to the second anniversary of Mr Harold Smith's return to Parliament—whereupon some benevolent soul in the gallery generously interjected "Stop and have your supper with us on Mondry night "—a truly alluring invitation. It early become evident that Sir Edward's attention had been drawn t,r Mr A. H CrosfieLl's trenchant letter in the " Examiner" and that he had come to the meeting with the determinat'on to treat it with 1 rfty disdain. "I have a great deal to deal with that is of importance" he observed " and there- fore I shall not deal with Mr Crosfield. After all, he does not matter." (A. Voice : 'Neither do you.") Later, however, Sir Edward returned to Mr Crosfield and even went to the length of challenging him on some subsidiary point of the Home Rule scheme. With delightful disingenuousnesq, Sir Edward condemned brute force, which he said had never defeated liberty in England, and never would—a sentiment quite foreign ti Sir Edward's supporters in Ulster, but one which Liberals will heartily endorse. A man at the back of the hall ventured to interrupt and was assailed with cries of "Out With the Fenian." As he refused to be quiet, he was summarily ejected by the police. A little later, another interrupter met with a similar fate —hut that was all the disturbance that was experi- enced. One of the ejections gave Sir Edward an , opening for a little joke : " Now that we have got rid ' of the leader of the temperance party, we may go on." Having mildly complained of English apathy on the Home Rule question, Sir Edward turned his attention to the "plethora of Radical rhetoric" which he had seen in that morning's papers. The only criticism which he vouchsafed of Lord Haldane's reply to Lord Roberts's alarmist speech was that

When the Lord Chancellor left the Woolsack for the purpose of Showing how wrong Lord Roberts was, it would be just as well for Lord Roberts to give up his marshal's baton, put on a wig, and sit on the Woolsack. (Laughter.) After Lord Roberts had decided a point of law they would be able to esteem how well the Lord Chan- cellor could decide a military matter. (Laughter.) Unwittingly, no doubt, Sir Edward cast a grave reflection on Tory management of our defent es in the notorious days of the Sputh Africa' ar. He remarked:

When the South African War was on he remembered that he was told to wait until our artillery got out there. But when it did get there our guns could not reach the artillery of the enemy. So it was at the present moment—all the warnings that were given set aside by those who had never been in war themselves. Coming to "the real master of the situition," Mr. John Redmond, Sir Edward did not scruple to accuse him of falsehood and hypocrisy because he professed loyalty to the Empire. His ground for doing so was that he read in the " Irish Freeman," which he described as a great Nationalist newspaper, a declara- tion that : —" Our aim is the establishment of an Irish Republic, for the E imple and sole reason that no other ending of our quarrel with England could be either adequate or final ln the English Empire we have no part, and want to have no part." Sir Edward next argued upon the well-worn lines that the ,Nationalists intend to use the Home Rule Bill simply as a lever to exact more concessions. He is quite unrepentant as to the recent disgraceful happenings in the House of Commons. As regarded anything he had done or been a party to, he said, not only was he not ashamed of it, but he was proud of it. Referring pathetically to the "gag and guillotine" prccedure of the Government, Sir Edward remarked with a great show of leeling : I find it very hard to bear it at times. So would you. Of course, Mr Crosfield could bear anything. (Laughter). Sir Edward declared that he would be a Home Ruler to-morrow if any one could -show him either of two things—that the Union had failed or that Ireland could get any advantage under Home Rule—a curious vommentary on Ireland's diteistent demand for the right of self-government for generations past. He also advanced the extraordinary claim that he pleaded for "the only democracy that exists in Ireland."

Address to the Junior Liberals. SOME LOCAL ILLUSTRATIONS.

The Junior Liberal Association is continuing its educational work and there was another large gathering in the association's rooms in Horsemarket street on Monday evening to hear a lecture by Mr J Bethel Jones, of Stockport, on the land question. Mr J Griffiths presided. The lecturer emphasised the enhance! value which occurred to land through the activities and:enterprise of the community. Giving an illustration, he said that some years ago the Glasgow Corporation sold certain land at. 25 8d per square yard. Such was the com- mercial progress of the city that eventually the Cor- poration had to buy back the land and the price charged was £35 per square yard. No-one would pretend to argue that the increased value had been created by the owner • it was due entirely to the citizens of Glasgow. Mr Lloyd George in his famous Budget proposed that in future the unearned increment should be taxed to the extent of 20 per cent. In order to arrive at the basis upon which tbe tax was to be levied, it was necessary to have a valuation of the land. The whole objection of the Tory party to the land clauses of the budget was really aimed at the valuation proposals, because when that valuation was completed and the people of this country realized the huge sum they had allowed to go practically untaxed, they would be ready to kick themselves for having allowed themselves to be taxed, so far as their necessities were concerned, when all the time there was that vast field ready to be tapped. At the present time we depended for our knowledge of the value of land, upon a valuation made over 200 years ago. The second tax Imposed by Mr Lloyd George was designed to meet the case of the man who tried to evade the first tax. It a man owned land which was needed by the community and did not choose to sell it, he would have to pay every year a tax of one halfpenny in the .0 on the capital value. It sounded a very small sum but it worked out to a great deal more than people imagined, because unused sites In the middle of big cities were extraordinarily valu- able. Mr Lloyd George did not introduce the taxes with the idea of getting large amounts of revenue during the first few years. They were, however, destined to provide the means of carrying out future schemes of social reform and he telseved the people would be amazed at the large sums which those com- paratively small taxes would yield. The discussion which was going on at the present time was somewhat premature because, until the valuation was completed, it was difficult to know exactly where we stood or to decide whether the single taxers or the land nation- alisers were right. He personally considered that the amount which the single taxers estimated as the annual value of the land was probably exces- sive, but thst the value which the land nationalisers placed upon it was probably under- estimated. The Commisaion which was making investi- gations would also have important pronouncements to publish. Oae thing was certain—that land reform was not going to end with the two taxes already introduced. Mr Lloyd George was careful enough to say they were only a beginning. Public opinion was not yet educated on the question as it should be. If those engaged in business aril commerce only understand the real effect of land taxation there would not be the stupid and insane opposition to it which prevailed in some lquarters. If shopkeepers realised that under the present system they were pa, ing not merely their own rates but those which ought to be paid by the big land owners in their districts, they would welcome land reform. Great efforts had been made to create distruat among the owners of small property. Any man holding property of less value than £5OO was, however, excluded from the provision, of the land taxa', because Mr Lloyd George did not want to do anything to hit the smallholder or the man in a small way who owned his house. The whole idea was to get at the big landowner, to make him split up his land and sell it, so that it could be used for the benefit of the community. The big landowners had literally starved tbo people of thiscountry for land and consequently our agriculture and other industries were hampered and many men were living 'louder conditions which were more suitable for pigs than for human beings. We frequently boasted of our freedom but we did not possess what was really most essential to us as a nation—freedom of land. It was only by keeping the Liberal party in office, pledged as it was to land reform, that they could achieve these ichals which were so dear to them. (Applause.) The CH/alias/VS? said there were cases in their own town of the inequalities and wrongs of the present system. Bridge-street had been widened at tremendous cost to the ratepayers, the Corporation having had to compensate the property owners whose premises were required—and a fine tune they called. There were at the present time three vacant plots on the right side of the street which were being held up for high prices. Two of them had, to his personal knowledge, been vacant for at least ten years. Had they been built upon, they would, he believed, have produced a rental of £l5O a year. Therefore, on tbe lowest estimate, the rate- payers bai, on assessable value alone, lost £1,500 in rates on the three plots in the past ten years. In addition, supposing the land belonged to the Corpora- tion—and he believed two a the plots did--the interest on the capital value amounted to £5O a year. Conse- quently £2,000 had been lost in rates and interest through thoLcs sites lying unused. That was equivalent to a fifth of a penny on the rates. Mr Griffiths also quoted ati ins.ance of land in Warrington which was originally let for agricultural purposes at £6O a year. Now that it was occupied by an industry, the fortunate owner was receiving no less than £B6O a year for it. The only way in which he had hitherto been taxed for that increased value was by the income tax. Ile (the speaker) did not see why the increased value to which an owner had contributed nothing but which had accrued through the activities of the workers ought not to be taxed for local purposes. There was no question that, as education advanced, the inequalities and economic wrongs which bad faced us for so many years would have to crumble. (Applause). Mr E LLOYD JONES (chairman of the association), suggested that the projected extension of land reform would tend to benefit the farmers. He believed that if they went into the question locally, they wou'd find sufficient material to convert Warrington to land reform. (Applause). The Lecturer was thanked on the proposition of Mr G TOULNIIN, seconded by Mr IV WRIGHT, both of whom expressed themselves in favour of land nationalisation on the ground of its mid effects.

Turning to the finances of the Home Rule Bill, Sir Edward said: Let me make a CHALLENGE TO MR, CROSFIELD

to contradict this if he dare. Under the new Home Bill, so framed is it that if you are to take a halfpenny or a penny off tea or any of the other customable commodities with a view to that goal of Liberal statesmanship called 'the free breakfast table you cannot take it off for England and Scotland without taking it off,at your own expense, for Ireland. You are going to pay old age pensions for Ireland. I am very glad you are. (Laughter.) There are plenty of them over there—some of them are manufactured and some are real. But you are giving something like £2,000,000 a year, or more, for the purpose of paying Old Age Pensions. What is the provision of the Bill? "Prue it is that we are giving you £2,000,000 a year for the payment of Old Age Pensions, but when y‘iur Parlament is set up you can abolish or put an end ti the pensions, if you like. But we will go on and give you £2,000,000 a year to spend if you like.' I wish Mr Ure would put that into his pipe." Sir Edward threw an interesting side-light on the recent hooliganism in the House of Commons by , telling the audience of one of his supporters in Ulster who said to him " I should like to tell you what you should do when you go back to the House of Commons." " What's that?" queried Sir Edward. " Make a hell of the place " was his answer. Evidently Cit. Edward and his colleagues are endeavour- ing to put that advice into practice. Sir Edward assured the audience that he hated , bigotry and that he and his followers did not wish to be top dog. He wound up his speech with the assurance that they were not going to have any more demonstrations. The time for demonstrations is over. We are going to take care of ourselves—(cheers)—and you are going to help us. (Cheers.) We have made up our minds, and when Ulster men have made up their minds do you think they are going to back out of them? If you don't believe us we cannot help you. We have done our best. You may believe us or you may not. My duty at all events is to make friends for these men. That is why I am here." THE MEMBER'S MANNERS.

Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., emulated the Chairman by beginning his speech "Ladies and gentlemen." Then he added the very truthful explanation " When in Warrington I always follow Sir Gilbert Greenall." In his owa inimitably elegant way, Mr. Smith referred to Mr. Crosfield :

" Two years ago I was elected as Member for this borough. I fought and beat one of the few indiscreet members of the Radical party who, with more curage than discretion, placed Home Rule on his election address. We fought him as much on Home Rule as on any other question. We beat him and we will beat him again. I notice to-day that there are psters round your town : ' Crosfield on Carson '—a fiy on a barrack ! (Laughter.) You don't even bother to take your handkerchief out of your pocket and whisk it off the wall. You just let it stay there, because it is doing nobody any harm, if it is doing nobody any goad. S t I don't think you and I may discuss the effusion of the late member for Warrington who, writing from a certain hotel as near as he could get to the railway train that had come to take him away from your b trough, has given you his views on the great sacrifices which Sir Edward Carson has made. We leave him to bask in the sunny South of Europe— (laughter)—an occupation for which be is eminently more fitted than for the work of the political arena, at which he tried his hand and at which he hopelessly failed in his own Dative town."

Mr Smith's instinct for the theatrical here asserted itself and, at his request, the audience stood and repeated the words " We will not have Home Rule." Sir Edward Carson, in replying to a vote of thanks. mentioned the famous " halfpenny per signature" telegram which he a*ured the meeting gave the Ulsterites great encouragement. At the open-air meeting Sir Edward could not for- bear another dig at Mr Crosfield on the ground of his staunch adherence to the Horne Rule c iuse

The meeting over,there was a procession, consisting largely of the starved Women Tariff Reformers (whose circulation at least, benefited) "Lady Greenall's bugle band," and its juvenile followers.As Sir Edward Carson and Mr. Harold Smith were taking their places in the waggonette which served as their triumphal chariot, a young man of considerable lung power greeted them with cries of "Trade Union Wreckers ! " At intervals in the procession were carried boards bear- ing the "No Surrender" war cry, and the women, of course, displayed their Union Jacks. After the march through the main streets, there was more speech making from the balcony of the Conservative Club, and thus ended Union Jack Day.

BIG SEWERAGE SCHEME.

STRONG OPPOSITION AT GRAPPENHALL INQUIRY.

===== 0002601_19121207_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
"WILD CAT AND EXTRAVAGANT PROPOSAV

• An unusual degree of public interest centred in an inquiry conducted in the Large Room, Grappenhall, on Wednesday by Mr F 0 Stanford, A.M. Inst., CE, an inspector of the Local Government Board, into the extensive sewerage scheme, approved by the Runcorn Rural District Council, for the townships of Grappen- hall, Latchford Without and Thelwall. The cost of the undertaking is estimated at £18,500 which will of course mean a very appreciable increase in the rates of the three districts concerned. Consequently very strong opposition has been aroused. The inhabitants of Grappenhall contend that a more modified scheme would meet the necessities of the case—in fact they have submitted alternative proposals for treating the sewerage of that township independently—while Thelma!! pleads that, as far as its present or futura requirements are concerned, a scheme is quite uncalled for. After proceedings lasting over four hours, the Inspector adjourned the inquiry until Tuesday next. Mr G F Ashton (clerk) appeared for the Runcorn Rural District Council. Amongst other representa- tives present were : Dr Young (county medical officer of health); Mr Harold Smith, MY. (instructed by Mr Herbeit Hatton, of Messrs Robert Davies and Ca) for the overseers and ratepayers of Thelvvall ; Mr Henry Greenall,for the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Co. Ltd., and the Wairington Tube Co. Ltd.; Mr Hatt-Cook for the Parish Council of Grappenhall,Lord Barrymore and Mr J Charlton ramthe principal land- owners in that township; Mr Kershaw for the Manchester Ship Canal Co; Mr Joseph Twiss, for the Warrington Estates Co; Mr W Roberts and Mr It T Fairclough for the Parish Council of Latchford With- out; Alderman Bennett, for the Warrington Garden Suburbs Ltd ; Mr F W Wright for several ratepayers of Latchford Without. The members of the Runcorn Rural District Council in attendance were Messrs G Pilling, Burrows and Hewitt. There was a large gathering of the public. THE SCHEME EXPLAINED. Mr Ashton, in an explanatory statement, pointed out that the necessity for a sewerage scheme for the parishes of Grappenhall, Latchford Without and The!- wall was first officially considered by the Council in the year 1908, and in that year Mr James Diggle, C.E., the Council's engineer for the present scheme, was instructed to advise the Council. According to the census of 1901 which was the last taken prior to a scheme of sewerage being considered, the population and number of houses in each of the psrishes wits as follows :—Grappenhall, population 987, houses 202 ; Latchford Without, population 295, houses 67 ; Thel- wall, population 481, houses 95. At the census of 1911 the population and number of houses was returned as follows :—Grappenhall, populatiem 1803, houses 436 ; Latchford Without, population 755, houses 202 ; Thelwall, population 517, houses 117. The Council's attention was first called to the necessity for a scheme of sewerage through the large amount of building operations in the parishes of Urappeobell and Latch- ford Without and the

NUMEROUS COMPLAINTS received of the pollution of streams in those parishes. The sewage from the three parishes at the present time had outfalls without treat mint into the Bridgewater Canal, the Manchester Ship Canal and the non-tidal portion of the River Mersey, and the Council were of opinion that such sewage should he prevented from entering those waters until it had been thoroughly purified. The great amount of building which had taken place in the parishes of Grappenhall and Latchford Without during the past ten years and which still continued, made this absolutely necessary for those parishes, and in a lesser degree it was con- sidered also necessary for the parish of Thelwall Following the instructions given him in 1908, the engineer submittei a scheme in 1909 for sewering the three parishes, with sewage outfall works adjoining the syphon under the Manchester Ship Canal in the parish of Thelwall. This scheme sass carefully considered by the Council and negotiations were opened with the Manchester Ship Canal Co., but the scheme was finally abandoned, in so far as the outfall works were con- cerned, on account of their close proximity to house property. The engineer was then instructed to prepar - a scheme with its outfall in snch a situation as would remove all reasonable ground of complaint. The present scheme, which had i s outfall on the estreme eastern boundary of the Council's area, was the outcome of those instructions. The Manchester Ship Canal cut off the natural outfall of the parishes to the River Mersey and in consequence thereof it was impossible to obtain access to the tidal portion of the river an i so deal with the sevrge of the parishes without treatment or purification. The Canal also passed through the parishes of Grappenhall and Thelwall and it was impossible to deal with the drainage of the areas on each side of the canal in the same scheme, The present scheme was therefore confined to those portions of the parishes which lay to the south of the canal. A pro- visional agreement had been entered into for the purchase of the site proposed for outfall works and a further agreement had teen entered into with the Manchester Ship Canal Company for the protection of their property in respect of storm overflaws and other works. Notices had been served upon each of the parish councils concerned of the Council's intention to construct works of sewerage and sewage disposal and, by instructien of the District Council, he attended with the engineer at a joint meeting of the Par!sh Councils in February last, when the engineer fully explained his scheme. Notices of apportionment of the cost of construction and after—maintenance of those portions of the works which were common to more than one parish were also sarved upon the overseers on the 18th July last, based upon the number of houses which it was expected would be connected wi h the scheme and subject to revision, based upon the actual connections for the time being, at the expiration of every quinquennial period. No objections bad been received by the District Council from the Overseers of Grappenhall and Latchford Without to those apportionments, but the Council were informed that objections to the scheme would he raised by each of the Parish Councils upon other grounds and the Council had also received intimations that it was the intention of certain land- owners to object. MEMORIALS AGAINST THE SCHEME bad been presented to the Local Government Board by the overseers and certain ratepayers of the parish of Thalwall and by His Manchester Ship Canal Co. The District Colima had already submitted their observa- tions in writing on the memorial of the overseers and ratepayers of Thelwall, but they had not had an oppor- tunity of replying to the memorial of the Manchester Ship Canal Co. who asked "that a separate area be constituted for the district ta be nerved by the scheme to the exclusion of such portions of the parishes as lie to the North side of an Imaginary line drawn along the centre of their Canal." It was submitted in reply to this Memorial :—(1) That the detached 'areas of Grappenhall and Thelwall on the North side of the Ship Canal were much too small to be constituted separate parishes; (2) It was possible that these areas might be developed, and especially the Grappenhall area, at an early date; (3) All expenses for sewering, dust semovals and other s tnitary works, incurred in respect of the said areas, would have to be charged as special expenses against the parishes to which they respectively belonged: (3) There were frequently certain areas of parishes which could never benefit directly by a particular scheme of sewerage, but that was not cot sidered a sufficient reason for eaclud- lug such areas from a contribution towards the cost of the scheme; (5) The construction of the Manchester Ship Canal had cut off the natural outfall of the parishes to the tidal portion of the River Mersey and had thereby necessitated the construction and after-maintenance of extensive outfall works and to now relieve the properties of that Company from a contribution towards the cost of the works would be unreasonable and unfair to the remainder of the ratepayers in the said parishes ; (0) In view of the fact that special expenses incurred in respect of any portion of a parish must always be charged against the whole area of that parish, it was inequitable to exclude certain areas from a contribution towards the repayment of a particular loan, as it was not known when similar expenditure might have to be incurred on behalf of such excluded area and charged against the whole of the parish in which it was situated. The estimated cost of the whole acheme was £18,500, and the assessable value, apportionment of cost and estimated rate in the £ which would be required in each year for repayment of principal and interest, but exclu- sive of maintenance expenses, were as follows : Assessable Ap'tionm't Rate in £ Value. of Cost. p'r annum s. d. Grappenhall 10,900 ... 11,761 ... 1 3 Latchford Without 5,450 ... 4,068 ... 10 Thelwall MEDICAL OFFICER'S EVIDENCE. Dr Adams (medical officer of health to the Rural District Council) said there was no proper system of drainage in any shape or form. There was no out-fall and no, means of obtaining one without a sewerage scheme, except by polluting streams. Going tack to what was known in local history as the " Thelwall nuisance" he said the investigations necessitated by that matter led to them being compelled to examine the sewerage arrangements for that portion of Thelwall which was invaded by diphtheria. There were some EXTRAORDINARY REVELATIONS of a most remarkable system of sewerage. There were sewers made of slabs of stone with an occasional bit of piping which was often broken. He could not say that there had been any excessive rate of zymotic disease in any of the parishes concerned. ENGINEER EXPLAINS VIE SCHEME. Mr James Diggle, the engineer, then described the new scheme in detail. He said the population of the district was scattered over a large area and that necessitated a very great length of sewers—in fact the

estimate allowed to: about 8} miles. They had to lay down a system which would meet the future requirements of the district, with regard to capacity as well as length. Between the years 1901-11 the population of Grappenhall had increased 90 per cent ; that of Latchford Without 160 per cent. ; and that of Thelwall 8 per cent. There was no reason why they should not anticipate at least the same rate of increase in the future. It was not pro- posed to incorporate tho existing drains in the new scheme, except for purposes of surface water, as they would be put to the constant expenditure of pumping ff the water. A gener tl pumping scheme was indis- pensable. The disposal works would be situated at the extreme rnd of the district, and the methods to be adopted were such that there would be no cacao for complaint with respect to smells or otherwise. Fourtern acres of land had been purchased fur £1,050— very satisfactory terms. The works were estimated to provide for a population of 5,000, which would soon be reached at the rate of increase which had been going on. A separate sewage disposal works for each parish would be most objectiorable and most costly. While paper schemes were often brought forth to show that there would be a saving by separate treatment, experi- ence always showed that such schemes were a loss. The initial cost was invariably greater and the expense of management was i ala-iably greater still, as com- pared with a combined scheme. The erection of sewage disposal worts in any other place than that selected would have been not merely an act of spoliation to that pretty dict but an ACT OF VANDALISM Referring to the effluent from tanneries, Mr Diggle said the Council did not want to take trade waste without any treatment and throw the cost upon the district, but if the owners were prepared to put down efficient works the Council would treat them fairly. Mr Harold Smith, M.P., intimated that his objection was not confined to the apportionment. He was opposed to Thelwall being brought into the scheme at all. (To Mr Ashton) : Can you tell me of any person in Thelwall who approves of this scheme? Mr Ashton : 1 have not really consulted them to see whether they approved of it.

Mr Smith further contended th it the number of houses in Thelwall had increased by only ten in twenty years. In answer to Mr Smith, Mr Ashton agreed that in one sense Thelwall would benefit less by the scheme than the other townships, because it wouli have: fewer houses to be accommodated by the scheme, lie also admitted that the other parishes were growing more rapidly than Thelwall at the present time. At Grap penhall, however, building was now getting to the boundary and one would naturally suppose that in a short time it would be extended into Thelwall Thelwall had railway facilities which Grappenhall did not possess and which must assist in the development of the district. Mr Smith : It has not assisted in the last 100 years. Mr Ashton : I appreciate that point. You might have said the same about Grappenball ten or twelve years ego. Mr Smith : Is not Grappenhan situated within easy access of the tram terminus at Latchford ? _

----- Mr Ashton : Nearer than Thelwall, certainly. Further questioned, Mr Ashton said the Council admitted that owners of property on the north side of the canal would not benefit from the scheme, but if they were excluded the expense on the remainder of the parish would he so much greater. Mr Smith : has any influence been officially brought to bear with the idea of seriously considering a less ambitious and less expenbive scheme? Mr Ashton : The Council could not see, from their examination of the district, that they could get out, any scheme that would embrace these three parishes on a less costly principle. Have the Council, by means of an engineer, acquired information as to the advisability of any other scheme? —Except through our own engineer, we have not. We have every confidence in our engineer that he will lay before us the scheme he considers best. for the district.

The Inspector : You have not had any other schemes before you ? Mr Ashton: No.

They did not wish to waste the ratepayers' mocey OD something they thought would not be carried out when it was submitted.

MANUFACTURERS' OBJECTIONS.

Mr Henry Greenall said there was no possibility that his clients could make any use of the scheme and it would therefore be most unfair to impose any Fitt of the ere, upon them. GRAPPENHALL'S ALTERNATIVE SCHEME.

Mr llatt-Coolr, in stating the case for Grappenhall' said that although they appeired as strong objectors, they were not doing so for objection's sake. The scheme might be an excellent one, provided that money was no object, but in this case the money question was a very serious one indeed. They agreed that a good and efficient scheme was rNuired, and his ins%ructions were to do all in his power to support that but to press the question of economy. The present scheme only dealt with a quarter of the 1,600 acres of which the to ran- ship consisted and, in view of the rapid rise in rotes and taxes, a much less expensive but more efficient scheme should be formulated. As the apportionment at present stood, agricultural land would be saddled with 40 in the £ for thirty years and the land actually built upon with Is 4d in the £ for the same period. No lets than £11,764 of the cost was to be borne by Grappenhall which meant that £643 per annum must be raised by the towbship for the next thirty years—a permanent rate of Is. 4d. on the top of the present local rate of 4s. in the £. An alternative scheme was tubmitted in the interests ofthe inhabitants of Grappenhall and not in any way as a criticism of 'Mr Diggle's scheme. Their hope was that it would be referred to the Rural District Council, a conference with the Grappenhall Council arranged, and the scheme considered on its merits as an attempt on the part of the people of Grappenhall to deal with their own sewage, to pay their own debts and to do so in a way which would commend itself to the District Council. If the present scheme were carried out the

DEVELOPMENT OF GRAPPENITALI. WOULD BE STOPPED, as the rents would have to be raised to a prohibitive extent. If the alternative scheme were accepted, Lord Barrymore was prepared to give the necessary :and to build the outfall works, because he felt most thoroughly that the present scheme was absolutely unfair to Grappenhall. The Parish Council was unanimous on the question and would undertake to carry out the scheme efficiently without delay. If two sewers which originally served Grappenhall had not been cut across by the Ship Canal, the question of sewage disposal would never have arisen. Mr Richmond, agent to Lord Barrymoremorroborated Mr Hatt Cook's statement as to his lordship's willing- ness to co-operate in the alternative scheme. Alderman Bennett asked whether the alternative scheme would be adequate to meet the future require- ments of the garden suburb? Although the progress of that movement had been somewhat slow, there were prospects of extensive developments in the early future They must, therefore, look ahead and, although he was most anxious that the cost of the scheme should be reduced to the lowest possible limit, he was equally anxious that the estate of the company should be adequately provided for. Mr Mountain said his scheme would provide for that portion of the estate which was within Grappenhall. TIIE COMING INTERNATIONAL EKHIBITION AT MlEN'''.

The next great international exhibition is to be held at Ghent from April to November, next year. Already the chief buildings are practically finished, and the whole work is well advanced The British section is a fine one, and with the exception of the French, lie largest, having an area of 161,000 square

feet. Almost half of this is to be filled with a display of textile machinery and machine tools. The area of the whole exhibition is roughly 250 acres. Our picture shows a general view of the Court of Honour, looking towards the entrance. The British section is on the right.

MEMBERS CHARGE EACH OTHER WITH FALSEHOOD.

THE ARCHITECTS FOR COUNCIL SCHOOLS.

PROSECUTION.—WiIIism Wood, Six Houses. was charged with having neglected his four children.—Mr Browne, who prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.O , said the chief ot ject of the prosecution was to get the children of the prisoner into the Workhouse. On the previous day the Inspector had succeeded in doing so. On November 20th the wife of Wood was admitted into the Workhouse suffering from an enlarged knee. The priscner was a bricksetter's labourer and had had the children at home, and since the wife went into the Union they bad been badly neglected. Dr. Peacocks, WU% called in, with the result that an order was made and they were admitted to the Union. The children had been Oiort of food and but for the kind- ness of friends would have been starved. Dr. Peacocke said the children were verminous and badly clothed. The house was dirty and also there was no food. There was only one old bed and no covering on it. The case was adjourned for twelve weeks. A 800 us WORRNlAN.—Frederick Walter Judd. 53, New-road, was charged on remand with obtaining food and lodging by false pretences between November Bth and 22ad inst., from Alice Littler, 53, New-road. He told her be had to start work on Monday and he went out, but returned and said he was unable to commence until Wednesday, as the man whose place he was to fill did not leave until that day. On the following Friday she became suspicious having regard to the fact that prisoner had been leaving the house rather late. She made inquiries and found the prisoner's statement was fictitious. Judd was afterwards arrested. The safety razor was missed and it was found to have been pawned by Judd.—Judd was sent to prison for a month on each charge and ordered to await a military escort. He did not take the money from his pocket, but snatched it from his hand. His wife did not charge interest for the money she lent Bennett. From a photo taken in THE LATE MR. GEORGE WORSDELL.

Warrington, and probably in Lancashire. Busineas steadily it creased, and in 1851 be received the much- coveted medal of the Great Exhibition 'for excellence of iron and of railway plant." Those who can recall Mr Worsdell in these years of early prosperity speak of him as abounding in energy, open-banded, sociable, and greatly beloved by his wtrkpecple and the young men whom he gathered round him at his house on Sundays. Unfortunately the high tension of his life induced insomnia, which in 1857 led to a temporary but complete breakdown of health, and he was compelled to relinquish to others all interest in the business he bad founded and had brought well on the way to the great prosperity it has since attained. A residence of two years at Working- ton followed, and in the year 1860 Mr Worsdell's health was sufficiently restored for him to take an engagement in the railway carriage and plant works of Mr John Ashbury, of Openshaw. Here his abilities were quickly recognised, and by the year 1803 be was acting as general manager of that concern. His health however, again gave way, and under medical orders he was compelled to throw up all work for a bile. In 1806 Mr Worsdell took over the management of ttle Lancaster Waggon Company—returning, by a curious coincidence, to the town in which his early childhood had been spent, —and brought it to a satisfactory financial position. In 1851, Mr Worsdell married Jane, the youngest daughter of the late Mr. Edwatd Bolton, of Sankey- street, and of Brookside, Penketh, a well-known Quaker, a gentleman much intere,ted in philanthropic work and in b cal affairs. His daughter had been educated at Penketh House under Joseph Edmundson along with the late Jacob Bright, M.P., whose more famous brother John had been also at Penketh under Mr. Edmundson in 1821. Her brothers are still well remembered in Warrington— Mr. Edward ,Bolton as the glass manufacturer, and Mr. Thomas Bolton and Mr. George Bolton as the founders of the Widnes Alkali Company. A son and two daughters were born to Mr. Worsdell at Warrington. The two daughters are still living; but their brother Edward died in 1008, having been seized with fatal illness whilst addressing an adult school. He was in his fifty-sixth Near, and left a widow and three children. In 1.001- Mr and Mrs Worsdell celebrated their golden wedding. The latter passed away at the age of eighty-three after a few weeks of painless decline, on Christmas Day, 1903.
