DEATH OF THE FOUNDER OF HALLAM FORGE.

HIS ASSOCIATIONS WITH WARRINGTON.

Aveteran engineer, whose work has been f Taught with incalculable benefit to Warrington, passed away at Lancaster on Sunday in the person of Mr. George Worsdell, the founder of Hallam Forge, Warrington. Mr. Worsdell was born at Preston in 1821, and was therefore in his 92nd ye_gt His father was a coach- builder from Long Acre, London. Some years after the birth of George, his third and youngest son, the father was entrusted with the construction at Crown- street, Liverpool, of the first passenger coaches for the new Liverpool and Manchester railway. George Stephenson said of him that " he was the best coach- builder he everhad."

The eldest son, Nathaniel, helped to set up the Rocket, the wooden tender of which was built at Crown-street. Later in life he became chief carriage builder to the London and North-Western at Crewe, and later their principal stores purchaser, retiring after a service of over fifty years. Be was the inventor of the method, now in universal use, of nicking up or dropping mail bags without stoppage of the train ; the original and entirely successful apparatus being first experimented with, we believe, at Winsfoid, near Crewe. Mr Nathaniel Worsdell died at Birkenhead in 1886. His sons Mr Thomas William Woosdell, now of Arnside, and Mr Wilson Worsdell, of Gateshead, are well known as two of the leading locomotive engineers of to-day. Mr Thomas Worsdell, the second son, was a clever, inventive man. As a lad of 16 he was the winner of the prize of £2OO offered for the best model of a carriage to be adopted by the London and Birmingham Railway, then in course of construction. The model (in which the screw coupling the lad's own idea— was first employed) was but Gft, in length, and yet its transport from Crown-street to London by Pickfords' vans cost £l7. Such was the difficulty and expense of transit in those days. The model has now a place in the collection of curiosities at Euston. Its ycung builder devoted himself to locomotive construction, and after engagements on the Continent (where the services of English engineers were in such request that at the age of 20 he was offered the locomotive superintendency of the Leipsic and Dresden Railway. the earliest of the German lines) he established a business in Birmingham, in which he trained as assistants the brothers Tangy e, later famous as engineers. Mr Thomas Worsdell, clicd at Ulverston lin 1893. _ .

Mr. George Worsdell, the third son, the subject of the present notice, was educated near Blackburn, under Mr George Echnundson,a famous Quaker school- master; who has a place in the National Dictionary of Biography. Later, after passing through the Crown- street Works, George Worsdell accompanied his father to Leipsic in 1836, to construct the carriages for the Leipsic and Dresden Railway. Returning to England about 1838 Mr George Worsdell devoted himself, with the energy of an eager nature, to acquiring a wider know ledge of the engineering profession. He took engagements at Euston and New Cross, and also at Swindon, where for a few months he was associated with Sir Daniel Gooch. Then, after a tudying for a few months under a well-known civil engineer in London, Mr Worsdell was engaged on locomotive work at Hull. Here it fell to his lot to repair the engine of the Sirius, the vessel of 700 tons which in 1813 had accomplished, in 25 days, the first crossing of the Atlantic under steam. In 1845 Mr Worsdell, who wished to start the manufacture of railway plant on his own account settled in Warrington, as a town mid-way between Liverpool and Manchester, and presenting certain other business advantages. It was then (says the " Manchester Guardian,") a small, quiet, old-world place, deprived of its activity as a coaching centre by the new railway system, and with little in the way of industries. •Here Mr Worsdell transformed an old bone-fertiliser works into the Dallam Forge, rolling with his own hands the first bar of iron produced in

LETTERS OF A SLENDER SCHOOL CHUM TO HER DUMPY FRIEND.

First Letter.—ON THE FOLLY OF EXERCISING AS A

MEANS OF Renucisu FLESH. My dear Sallie, How I wish you could see me now. You wouldn't know me; that's certain.

I've been saving up the news until the witchery should be fully accomplished, that is to say until I your dearest relic of the days of your girlhood should have cast aside the terrible sheaf of fat that once encumbered me, and should have emerged frisky and slender, like a graceful and energetic butterfly, newly rid of its roly-poly wrappings. It is quite true, Sallie dear, the pcdginess and wobbliness are entirely gone. I can trip along as lightly as any fair maid of your acquaintance, and the change from ugly fatness to simple plump graceful- ness bas been so natural and safe and easy that I have been scarcely aware of how rapidly it was taking place. Do you remember that depressing interview we had with Dr. Bontree when he ordered me those terrible exercises as the only possible means of reducing my weight ? How very hard I tried to carry out his ideas, and how I cried from sheer childish weariness after each painful attempt. Well, I have just simply quit everything in the exercise way, and here I am, graceful and giddy as heart could 'desire.

And now for the magic prescription that has " sent down great marvels and wonders" upon my happy head. Marmola Prescription Tablets, Sallie—Just one after each meal. Nothing else in all the world— simply and solely Marmola,sans exercise,sana dieting, sans interference with my usual round of business and pleasure. Don't be hurt at my suggesting it, dear, but if you would follow my example and take a short course of the same treatment, you would soon be as slender and winsome and bonny as Your devoted MARGARET.

P.B.—ln ease you haven't heard before of Marmola Tablets, you get them from your chemists at 2/9 the bottle, or post free direct from the Afarmola Company, Dept. 123, 86, Clerkenwell Road, London, E.C.

LOCvAL REMINISCENCES

A Warrington correspondent writes :—The old bone works which stood on the south side of Hallam- lane was a very small affair, and the forge and foundry which Mr Worsdell started was mainly employed in the making of railway wheels and axles. The operations were on a small scale. Mr Worsdell was a Quaker, and his first partner was a Mr Gilkes, also a Quaker connected with the North of England, and the firm was Gilkes, Worsdell and Co. The business was not financially successful and in 1849 was in considerable straits, and Worsdell persuaded Mr William Nield to join him. Mr Gilkes retired, and the firm became Nield, Worsdell and Co.' In that year the late Mr Henry Meekly was recommended by an old schoolfellow. Mr William Milner, to put some money into the concern. Mr Bleckly was then a banker in London, and had pre- viously had no connection with Warrington. Still, Nield and Miller were both old Warringtonians and both Quakers, as was Worsdell himself. The business was still a very small one, and continued to lose money, and in 1856 the position was so bad that Mr Henry Bleckly came in the firm to look after his interest in it, which was then considerable. The name of the firm was altered to Nield and Co.', and consisted of Messrs William Nield, Henry Bleckly, and Thomas Fell, whose initials Ii.N.F. are still retained as one of the principal trade marks of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company. Mr Worsdell retited altogether in 1857, and his connec- tion with Warrington and the iron trade ceased from that date, and the progress since made was due to the old partners and their successors. All the former have been dead more than twenty years, and Mr. J. J. Bleckly is the only representative of the second generation.

ST. HELENS.

COMBATING CONSUMPTION.

PREPARATIONS AT ST HELENS.

The St Helens Health Committee has decided to lease Eccleaton Hall and adjoining laud for twenty-one years as a site for the treatment of chronic cases of tuber- culosis.

The committee also recommends the appointment of Dr S J C Holden, at present assistant medical officer of health, to be medical officer to carry out tuberculosis work in the borough as from January 15th next, at a salary of £3OO a year, with an allowance of £lOO for board and residence. A tuberculosis nurse and a clerk- dispenser are also to be engaged.

===== 0002601_19121207_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
HOUSING AT ST. HELENS.

The unsatisfactory housing conditions existing at St Helens, are still a subject ot much local discussion, and an important contribution to the controversy, from the point of view of the sanitary authority, was made by Alderman H B Bates, chairman of the Health Com- mittee at the meeting of the Town Council on Wednes- day. "So much has been said about the housing conditions," said Alderman Bates, "and so much has been done by the Health Committee, that I think a statement should be made in regard to the matter. St. Helens is not as black as it is painted, and there are many worse industrial towns. We admit as a health committee, that we have in our midst a great many insanitary and badly lighted houses, but you must remember that St Helens has an essentially working class population who cannot afford to pay high rents. The Health Committee for some time past have been closing insanitary property, but I regret to say that sufficient provision has not been made for other houses for the people. In many cases people are overcrowded by their own wish. Houses have been overcrowded for years, and will continue to be overcrowded, but I think the time has arrived when we ought to devise some scheme for the better housing of the people. We have attended conferences and inspected garden cities, and we have seen beautiful and well-planned houses, but we have never seen a house that we could ' recommend to be built in St Helens for the rent the people would be able to pay. Unless you establish the housing scheme on a sound financial basis lam afraid you would get into trouble early on. It is a question whether it shall be a municipal scheme or one by private enterprise. We are doing all we possibly can, and we have in the town philanthropic men who are willing to take their share of the burden. We appeal to the ratepayers to help us in solving this problem. You must educate people to keep their houses clean, to give them a sense of responsibility."

Councillor BROADHURST expressed the hope that now the thoroughfare at Sankey Bridges was being widened a 6' little wooden hut " would be provided there, as it would be vety acceptable to it largeinumber of workmen who used the trams.

Councillor PEMBERTON suggested the construction of adequate wood and glass shelters. The committee had plenty of profits. (Laughter.) The MAYOR: The same remarks apply to the other termini. Alderman SMETIII7RST, in replying on the discussion, , said no member of the Council desired a motor omnibus service more than he did, but it could not be provided in five or ten minutel. This question had been under the discussion of the committee for two years, but changes for the better In the construction of the buses were being constantly made, and if the committee had bought them a few years ago they would have had to replace them with new ones. At whatever cost, Cie committee must have the best, but the Council must not expect them for some months, as other people were also waiting for theirs. With reference to the shelters,l the difficulty was that the commitc,s—i--mld not get land, They might, however, be aide to do so at Sankey Bridges. The shelters would b 3 a great comfort to the passengers. WARRINGTON BRIDGE. MORE COMPLAINTS OF DELAY. Alderman SNIZTRURST moved, and the Mayon seconded, the confirmation of the minutes of the Bridges Committee. Councillor LASHFORD raised a discussion on the building of the bridge by alluding to one of the minutes which stated that orders had been made for payment of accounts amounting to £707 29, £5lO of which as for the contractors. It was high time that they were asked to acclerate the work as tbe present rate of progress was making the Council a laughing stock. He had been asked how it was that sufficiant men were not put on the job. Going to Stockton Heath the other day on the car, he found that this was the whole topic of con- versation. Fifteen months had elapsed, and not half the bridge had been built and yet the contract was let for 20 months. The bridge should therefore be finished by April next. Alderman SMETHURST said that no-one could have tried to push on the work more than he had tried to push it on. He had asked why it was that more men were not engaged, but, he supposed, the contractors knew their business better than he did. They were in the hands of an engineer who was regarded as one of the greatest of experts' and it was he, if anybody, who should be spoken to. Only the previous day he had expressed the opinion that double the number of men ought to be be put on. What could he do more than than that? Alen had been paid to look after their interests. At the last meeting of the General Purposes Committee a great deal was said about the bridge. Remiss of the members had never seen it, and he invited them to meet him on the spot the following day, but not a single man turned up. (Laughter.) Alderman TINNioN : As usual. (Laughter.) Alderman SMETHURST : And yet they come here, talk about delay, and got their names in the papers tti: if they took an interest in the construction of the btidge. On Thursday afternoon he gave a similar invitation, but not a man turned up. Probably if he had provided cigars, &0., there would have been a good muster. (Laughter.) .

Alderman BENNETT did not think the progress was as slow as it was stated to be. He bad frequently visited the bridge, and had found that the progress during the past few weeks had been rather rapid. The building of the bridge was a matter of tremendous importance, and with ferre-concrete, which was to some extent an untried material, it was far wiser to " hasten slowly " 1 and to be sure that the work was well and thoroughly done. (Hear, hear). Councillor ROBERTS Is it not possible that on the historic occasion to which Councillor Lashford has referred the community present on the car knew what a distinguished visitor they had amongst them and were I What is commonly called" pulling his leg " ? (Laughter;. Councillor PEMBERTON said it was no trival question of pulling people's legs. He sympathised with Alder- man Smethurst who had done all he could in the matter and it was not fair to expect a gentleman of his age to give any more time to the work. If the Council were anxious to get the bridge finished, they ought to sup- port him. It was deplorable that so few members troubled to visit the site. He had frequently seen the works and he regarded the job as one of the slowest he ever knew. EVENING SCHOOLS. MORE PRIZE DISTRIBUTIONS. Mr. A. J. Jolley on Present-Day Clap-Trap. Some interesting remarks on scientific methods of thought as applied to politics were made by Mr A J Jolley (deputy-chairman of the Education Committee) in distributing the prizes to the students at Beathside Branch Technical Schools on Tuesday evening. In the absence of Councillor R lienshall, the chair was taken by Mr J Moore Murray (director of Educa- tion) who said Mr Jolley's chief aim was to give every boy and girl a chance to rise in the walk of life in which he or she was placed and to make their lives better and happier. (Applause). Mr JOLLEY said his recollection went back to the time long before continuation classes in any form were run by the local authority at the expense of the rate- payers. Some of the ratepayers grumbled occasionally at the amount of money they had to find for the cost of education, and a portion of that money was expended in providing evening classes such as those held in that building. Before that time some of them who were very keen on trying to afford oppor- tunities to the young people to improve their position, formed and conducted classes voluntarily. The chances they had at the present time were far better than those which were available to their predecessors twenty years ago. A considerable amount of the ratepayers' money was spent in providing those facilities for their education, and the only feeling of despendency on the part of the members of the Education Committee was that the facilities were not always used to the fullest extent by those who had the chance. One of the defects which presented themselves in connection with their evening school work was the tendency among so many cf the students to try to fasten on to what might be called

BREAD AND BUTTER SUBJECTS. They thought all they needed to take up was something which would enable them to earn better wages, either sooner or later. That desire was alperfectly good and legitimate one, but they did not want to limit their attention to that only. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Education ought to mean a good deal more than simply improving their chance of earning a livelihood. Anything worth calling real education ought to lead to a fuller and better life in every way. They wanted to

LEARN TO LIVE AND NOT MERELY TO EARN MONEY. It was open to every one of them to acquire something like a real knowledge of one or more branches of science, and they would find that such equipment would be of lasting benefit throughout life. They must aim at acquiring not merely the khowledge of scientific facts but the scientific spirit or the scientifio way of looking at things. They, as members of the evening classes and later of technical classes, would have an opportunity of deepening, widening and extending the rudimentary scientific knowledge imparted in the day schools and of making it of benefit to their lives. One outcome would be the improvement of their knowledge of the univer:e in which they would have to play a man's part in years to come. The scientific spirit would also affect their ways of thinking and their whole attitude towards the problems of the day. They heard a great deal of Socialism at the present time. It was a very vague and often carelessly used word. They all ought to take an interest in social problems, and it would be a tremendous benefit to the town and country if they would take a scientific view of them. He rather imagined that the kind of talk they beard at political meetings would be different from what it often was.and the kind of stuff they read in the newspapers would also be different, if people bad a scientific training and cultivated the scientific point of view. They would be able to distinguish more accurately between truth and error and, as the Chairman had told them' "Truth is the highest thing that man may keep." Those who had drunk in the real scientific spirit would not be readily taken in by the clap-trap, trashy talk and writing of the present day. Those who went to a political meeting did not usually go in a very critical spirit, and the rule of humbug would be very much less frequent if people were scientific in their training. (Applause.) They sometimes beard people talking about social problems as if they could alter the laws of nature as easily as they could alter the laws of the land. Anyone who had bad any scientifio training at all knew that was not only nonsense' but it often led Ito nonsense which was pernicious and dangerous. They would be able to see through the fallacious nonssnse which was sometimes talked under the disguise of social reform and to distinguish between measures which would be a real good and those which would do a great deal of harm (Hear, hear and applause.) Having emphasised the great responsibility which attached to the use of the vote, Mr Jolley said they must further bear in mind that truth was not settled by majorities Almost every great beneficial change had been carried out against the wish of the minority. We were yet a long way off the democratic ideal but we were moving towards it and every member of the British democracy should qualify himself to play his part in that system in the best and highest way. The ideal man was the One whose mind had been ,trained on scientific lines and whose character had been formed on Christian lines. (Hear, hear and app'ause). Votes of thanks were accorded to the Chairman and Mr Jolley. PRIZE AND SCHOLARSHIP NVINNERS. . .

BEAMONT SCHOOL.

On Wednesday evening the Rev Canon Morley Stevenson (chairman of the School Management Com- mittee) distributed prizes and certificates to the students at the Beamont Branch Technical School. The gathering was notable for the large attendance of parents and friends of the prize-winners. Councillor H ROBEILM 'chairman of the Education Committee), who presided, said the excellent results obtained in the examinations proved beyond question that a very great amount of devoted and disinterested service must have been given by Mr 'Longshaw (superintendent) and those associated with him. They

k also spoke volumes for the feeling of co-operation between the students and the staff. (Applause). ' Canon STEVENSON said few things were more striking • or satisfactory than the wonderful change which had • passed over the evening schools of our country during the past forty years. He recalled the days when he used to teach in an evening school in the famous city of Oxford. It was situated in the poorer part of the town and they had some " very' rough gentlemen " Ito deal with. Having given instances to show the kind of material handled and th unruly behaviour which prevailed at that time, the Canon said that to-day they lived in happier times and possessed much greater advantages. They met, not. in a very poor building with I poor seats and appliances, but in beautiful schools with everything as well arranged as it possibly could be, with excellent teachers and with the students them- selves eoming, not to make a disturbance—such a thing would never enter into their heads—but to learn, to get. on and to benefit by the teaching they received. Having paid a high tribute to the painstak- ing work of Mr Longshaw, whom be had known for many years, Canon Stevenson said the students at the evening scnools learned certain things which were going to be stepping stones to other things that they would learn afterwards. They would help to fit them to lead useful lives in whatever profession they might enter. That was all to the good, and he hoped they I would follow up their evening school work by a course at the technical institute which would perfect them, as far as possible, for whatever line of life they intended to adopt. He wished, however, to impress upon them 1 the advantage of taking up something in addition to the means by which they earned their living. He had found that a great help in his own life and he believed it invariably tended to make people happier and more useful. A great German teacher had said : " Strike. into life wherever you like ; it is all interesting." That was quite true and he hoped the students would interest themselves in some pursuit, not because they were going to get paid for it, but because it was going to open out their lives. They must endeavour to show the world that there was still life and talent in the old town so that when something noteworthy was accompliahed people would say "A Warrington man did that." (Applause.) One thing they should all cultivate was a love of good literature. As they grew older they would find their books were their friends. There was some- thing about their books which they would not find in their other friends. They would never get their books to say a nasty word or to quarrel with them. They were always patient, send however neglected or ill-treated, their friendship would le just. as great whenever their help was sought. He nsked the young people to take courage and begin to acquire books which would give them comfort and pleasure throughout life. Milton said of books:

" You'd as well kill a good man As kill a good book." By that he meant that a good man might be replaced by others, but a good book could not be replaced ; it went on living for ever and doing good to people whilst the world lasted. (Applause). On the proposition of Mr J Moona MURRAY, seconded by Mr F H Lotcosirmv, a vote of thanks was passed to the Chairman and Canon Stevenson. Councillor ROBERTS, in replying, said it was not the pleasantest thing in the world to be a public man. Motives were attributed and insinuations made which did not help to make public life altogether a bed of , roses. He always felt, however, that those little matters could be put on one side, in view of the greater question of what was a man's duty to his fellow men. He sometimes thought that the public men who were required for the government of the town did not come forward as they ought to do—that the necessary interest was not taken in municipal affairs by those who were qualified to give assistance, However, when he saw the type ofthe students who were beiag trained in their evening schools, he did not despair of the future of Warrington. (Applause.) THE PRIZE LIST. THE LEATHER TRADE IN WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT.

There was a regularly good enquiry for all lines of late, and buyers have been over the ground frequently on the look-out for the odd lines which crop up occasionally in all yards. The figures for November appear to have came out above expectation, as most tanners confess to having had a better month's trade than they anticipated, and one volunteered the information that his total sales for the five months since June were over ,t.:15,000 beyond those for the same period last year, The same yard usually carries a finished stock worth .4:10,000, out I am told at the moment .4.1,500 would buy the lot! The demand for manu- facturers' goods has been much as usual, but the recent wet weather has evidently caused a stir with the repairers, as orders from that branch have been very numerous and very urgent, and in its turn the same cause must soon have the effect of throwing much more work on the factories, and we may expect more determined buying shortly. At the moment prices of sole leather are largely stationary, with the proviso that buyers do not talk , about distant delivery ; if this is mentioned, up go 'quotations, as tanners are unanimous in turning a. deaf ear to contracts at present rates. Rough.leather continues to appreciate, and the market for strap butts in particular is so strong that prices are likely to go much higher yet. Apparently the yards in the district are very busy, but it is reported some are slackening down on the input, as they fear that even current leather values do not jusdfy their paying present rates for fresh hides, which are going anything up to 5 per cent, per month worse in condition. There has been a brisk foreign enquiry during th,-+ week, a particularly noticeable centre being Hong Kong, and it is rather a pity this should have come at a time when British tanners have practically nothing to offer, for by all accounts the opening may be a very important one, and worth close cultivation.—Prorn the Leathcr Forld.

WORKING MEN AS KNIGHTS. ARCHDEACOM MADDEN'S IDEAL. Archdeacon Madden addressed the Sunday afternoon gathering at the St. Helens Parish Church Men's Bible Class on the occasion of their twenty-fourth anniversary on Sunday. About 600 men were present in spite of the very bad weather. He appealed for more thorough- going Christian service. It was not enough for the men to simply attend the class in an afternoon, but they ought to go to church in a morning. They must awake, cast off the works of darkness, and put on the whole armour of light. In the workshops and homes of St. Helens they must be knights in shining armour, giving a profession of faith which might be seen in their character, conversation, and all the affairs of life. He sometimes became tired of the thought of men merely sitting in a Bible class. He wanted to know what they were doing outside. Ai tending the Bible class was only like the soldier going on parade. The Bible class was their drillground. Were they really fighting the battle of God ? Christ was their leader, and at his great appearing what would he find ? Would he find them skulking among the baggage in the rear or would he find them in the forefront of the battls ?

THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY DECEMBER 7, 1912.

Some people seem only now to THE have awakened to the fact that WORLD'S condition, that fewer men are DESIRES. the country is in a prosperous unemployed than at any period since the South African War, and that the prospects of still greater trade are ex- ceedingly rosy. The " boom," as we have learnt to call it, has been visible for a very long time, and all the experts agree that it has not yet reached its height. There is really no reason to suppose that a period of depression must regularly follow a period of prosperity, though past exp.eri- ,ence teaches us to expect such a succession. Perhaps it is better to say that the extremes of good and bad trade will not be so far t apart in the future as in the past. It is to riohoffy's interest to have violent fluctua- tions! in business, and, although there are Still a great many improvements to be de- sired, it may be noted that we hen:- nothing nowadays of gigantic corners, and that we seldom hear of huge bunk failures in any part of the world. In finance we have raised the standard of ability everywhere, and prudence and foresight influence the dealings of responsible men and corpora- tions much more than ever. But it is only possible for financiers to regulate the, World's enterprise and to conserve its capi- tal. Not even they can control its natural activities; and when we read that there has been ain ttnprecedented catch of herrings in the North Sea, for instance, we can realise how little man has to do with the way in Which nature provides his food supplies. Plenty of food means 'abundant human activity, and that in its turn both increases the productiveness of man's labour and im- bues him with the desire to gain greater Comforts and more of the physical pleasures of life. So the wealth of the world is multi- Plied. Everywhere there are signs of this realisation that a little more effort will yield a great deal more delight. The semi-bar- baric and the uneducated peoples of the World are gradually finding out, through the enterprise of modern commerce, that many articles of comfort may be purchased for a little more outlay in labour; and countries like our own, that have grown rich by 6upplying the needs of others, are reaping ,abundant harvests. It is this wonderful •spread of the knowledge that more good

.?. ? , • ,74

===== 0002601_19121207_art0067.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
111

===== 0002601_19121207_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
things are to be acquired by a little harder work that is deepening the diligence of men everywhere, and making it possible for them to become profitable customers. And so long as this steady process of opening up the potentialities of the world's re- sources continues there is no reason to expect a plunge back again into the depths of commercial depression.

Nothing was more notice

WHAT DOES LORD able about the Bolton LANSDOWNE MEAN? election than the sup- pression of Tariff Reform policy by the Tory Party. The -Cory Party, Lord Lansdowne had explained, had regained its freedom. There need be no nasty Referendum. If a majority was obtained by the Tory Party at a General Election, food-taxes could be imposed with- out any bothering" poll of the people." And in his message to the Tory candidate at Bolton, Mr. Bonar Law said not a word about Tariff Reform. Does Lord Lansdowne mean, when he says that Tariff Reform Referen- dum "is not a business proposition," that he knows a Referendum would put an effectual stopper on food-taxes?

Lord Roberts is still THE DANGER OF unrepentant. After CONSCRIPTION, writing to say that his utterances at Man- chester did not bear the meaning placed upon them, he takes the first opportunity of adding fuel to the fire by describing the Territorial Force as "a make-believe army" and arguing for the compulsory military training of every able-bodied man in the country. Mr. H. W. Massingham, one of the most distinguished writers and publicists in England, in an article which we quote on another page, says that "Lord Roberts is out for Conscription," and the destruction of the Territorials. The Tory Party, he adds, are also "on the eve of that fatal step," even though it ensures them twenty more years of opposition. The trend of events has been steadily in the direction indicated by Mr. Massingham for some time, and now that Mr. F. E. Smith has gone over to the side of Lord Roberts, there can be little doubt that his party is very near to following suit. Under the circum- stances it behoves us to take the scare-mongering and the insidious pleas for compulsory military training, seriously. The voices of the National Service League and Lord Roberts were as those crying in the wilderness, but with the possibility in view that the Tory Party may make a desperate "last throw" for national support by fostering national alarm, the position becomes one that cannot be too seriously regarded. Fortunately there is no fear of the country, in its sane moments,ever agree- ing to conscription, but there are times when peoples as well as individuals are not wholly sane. The greatest safeguard we have to-day is the Government— a Government fully aware of the criminal folly of con- scription, determined on that encourage- ment of the Territorial Force which it has lacked so long, and alive, as Mr. Massingham points out, to the fact that the preparation of a continental army here would not only mean war with Germany, but would "cripple our finances and banish all hope of a definite and serious rise in the standard of our people's life."

The waters in the RELIGION IN THE education pool are again Scmom, being vigorously stirred loy way of preparation for the Bill which the Government has promised to introduce next session, and the cross currents of opinion are appearing as of old. The National Education Association is giving special attention to the present condition of educational finance, which is creating a serious deficiency of school accommodation, and urging the Government to provide a building grant sufficient to secure without delay an adequate supply of Council schools for all who require them. The Northern Counties Education League is also urging the necessity for such a grant, and at the same time favouring a bold treatment of the Single School Area problem. On the other hand the. Anglican party is concentrating on the Right of Entry, and representative Free Churchmen like Dr. Clifford, Mr. Meyer and Dr. Scott Lidgett, are co-operat- ing with the Nonconformist Committee in the House of Commons in demanding the abolition of religious tests for teachers, and the public control of all schools maintained by public rates and taxes. Meantime two things seem tolerably clear—the widely diffused discontent of educationists can be relieved by substantial aid in the form of a building grant, partly retrospective, to local authorities; but the real crux of the ques- tion at issue will remain so long as the principles of religious equality are set at defiance, and sectarian privilege is allowed to bar the way to public service.

Lady Cynosure was born in 1903. She is a bay mare by Cyllene - Maid Marian, covered by Beppo.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

The extraordinary value to which land on the far side of a borough boundary can attain when a Corporation is a possible purchaser has received striking illustration in the case of the Storeton estate. Some time ago (says the "Manchester Guardzan" the Birkenhead Corporation made inquiries as to the price of certain portions of the estate. The quota- tions received ranged from £4OO to £l,OOO per acre. Now, through Sir William Lever's decision to forego any profit on his purchase, Birkenhead gets 1,500 acres of the land in question at £BO per acre. * _

Candid Tories are 'ever-ywhere expressing disappointment with Sir Edward Carson's speech. The new King' of Ulster who was responsible for so much sound and fury at Belfast, and who there roared forth threats of rebellion and civil war, cooed like a dove on the Parr Hall platform. He failed so entirely to make the opposition to Home Rule appear a real, live cause, that the audience went away thoroughly convinced that for all Sir Edward Carson and his friends could do, Home Rule was as good as carried. Beating the big drum had a rousing effect in Ulster, but in Warrington—well Sir Edward did not even beat the drum! ik *

"The state of Sankey street on Sunday night is a disgrace to Warrington" declared Mr. J. Moore Murray, director of eduction, in an address to evening school students. The opinion will be endorsed by everybody,except perhaps the crowds of young people who aimlessly fritter away their time by parading that thoroughfare and indulging in horse play. We are afraid the culprits will be quite unresponsive to the appeals of educationists to spend quiet evenings in the home circle. For some unaccountable reason the streets seem to have an irresistible attraction and we can only trust to firmness on the part of the police to make the condi- tions a little more tolerable for the peaceable passer-by. * * *

A recent paragraph in the "Morning Post" anent Canon Henson's appointment to the Deanery of Durham throws the spirit of certain members of the Establishment into strong, if unpleasant, relief. The new Dean is exhorted to restrain the friendliness towards Nonconformity which he has dis- played "in season and out of season," and to remember that there is an "out " season as well as an "in." Specially is he told to avoid interchange of pulpits. He may still be sympathetic, but in such wise as not to "compromise the Church." That is the spirit of a privileged Church in a nutshell. Nonconformists are to take what condes- cension they can get, and be thankful ; and. Church folk may make them a distant bow as to inferiors, but no more. What miserable and petty Pharisaism it all is ! *

The process of making the national Universities freely accessible to all citizens irrespective of religious belief or profession has been going on for more than sixty years Up to 1854 all honours, emoluments and offices were limited to members of the Established Church, but in that year the scholarships and the Bachelor of Arts degree at Oxford were thrown open. Two years later Cambridge, with greater liberality opened the degree of Master of Arts to Non- conformists. Similar reforms, based on the principle of religious equality, have marked the intervening years, and now comes the news that by a majority of 435 to 326 the Cambridge Senate has resolved to open the Degrees in Divinity to ministers and laymen 'of all communions. Sectarianism dies hard; but it is well for the cause of learning that it is being steadily driven out of the lUniversities.

* * The references to Nonconformity made by the writer of the Church column in the "Morning Post" are often amusing. He informs us, for example, that because many of their places of worship possess a trust deed, Nonconformists are far more under State control than the established Church. How any grown man can write such non- sense passes comprehension. Trust deeds are not drawn up by Parliament, as, for instance' the Act of Uniformity was. The State will, of course,see that trust deeds are not violated as it will see that a man pays his debts, and does not willfully break any formal engage- ment into which he may enter. But that is quite a different thing from maintaining a Church in a position of exclusive privilege and investing it with whatever moral authority can be conferred under a special sanction of law. However, it is useless to notice such things except to laugh at them ; and, indeed, it is only for that purpose that we call attention to the paragraph in question. * • * . _

One amusing featu-re of the Tory demon- strations over the week-end was the nervousness of the speakers at the telling attack made by Mr. A. H. Crosfield in last Saturday's "Examiner." They one and all simulated an appearance of indifference so indifferently that their concern was manifest. First Sir Edward Carson. He had something important to speak about, so he was going to ignore Mr. Crosfield—but he could not do it you see. Then the Member. " Crosfield on Carson" was likened to a fly on a barrack. Mr. Smith ought to have used" pub" for " barrack." Brushing flies off newly-painted " pubs " is a favourite employment in certain grades of labour, though the workers are not yet scheduled for insurance. Mr. Smith missed an opportunity of conciliating this section of his supporters. They are influential men at election times. Even on Monday,Mr. Crosfield's letter seemed to still stick in Mr. Smith's throat and impede the free passage of hot-pot. He tried to comfort himself by presuming that none of his hearers had read the letter. Perhaps not But the " Examiner " certainly went into many new hands last Saturday. * * * Although the Mayor has responded to the toast of "The town and trade of Warring- ton" on occasions well nigh innumerable, he usually contrives to invest his speeches with originality. At the Caledonian Association's dinner his Worship advanced the novel suggestion that Warringtonians should show their loyalty to the old town by living in it instead of fleeing to the suburbs. Dr. Joseph is convinced that, to the townsman of good constitution, life in Warrington is quite healthy,if pursued on regular lines and varied with sufficient recreation. Further- more he expressed the view that there would be less disorder in the main streets, if more people dwelt in the heart of the town. What- ever measure of agreement there may be with his Worship's opinions, the resident in suburbia will probably be in no hurry to exchange his lot for that of the townsman who has to spend his days in unlovely streets under a pall of smoke. Lifetin the country has undeniable ch arm siand advantages and it will require an abnormally strong sense of local patriotism to stem the constant migra- tion from the borough of the increasingly large number who find it convenient to take up their abode on our rural outskirts_

The Wirral Peninsula seems likely to become one of the most favoured districts in the north-west of England. It has already three seaside resorts, New Brighton, Hoylake, and West Kirby; a model village, Thornton Hough; a model town, Port Sun- light; and now, by the Birkenhead Corpora- tion's acceptance of Sir William Lever's offer of the Storeton Estate, recently bought from the executors of the late Sir Thomas Brockle- bank on advantageous terms, its attractions bid fair to include a garden city in the near future.

The Education Act of 1902 is apparently working its own nemesis. Under its opera- tion the number of non-nrovided schools has been reduced from 14,268 to 12,751. This means that in ten years 1,517 schools, with places for 903,633 pupils have been closed or transferred. The average attendance at the non-provided schools has fallen within the same period by 351,812. At present it is only 2,193,675, whereas in 1902 it was 2,545,437. On the other hand, during the past ten years, the Council Schools have increased by 2,128, and the average attendance of scholars has risen by 794,611. It is this success of the Council School system which, according to the Northern Counties Education League, is constraining the Anglican party to concen- trate on the Right of Entry. In letting slip a portion of the educational machinery main- tained by public funds, they would capture the whole for denominational purposes.

* "If they were annoyed by Mr. Lloyd George let them not read his speeches. He would one day have to settle with his Creator for what he said." Lord Selborne St Halstead, 19th October.

This has all the appearance of sincerity though it is rather outside the usual run of talk against the Chancellor at meetings con- vened to denounce him, while upholding the tattered and torn banner of Tariff Reform. We have a belief all the same that in the final judgment of Mr. Lloyd George, he will be judged like others, not so much by what he has said as by what he has done fur the race, and it looks at times as if he were going to have in this respect a fairly good record. At the Bar,Lord Selborne will have to admit that he attempted to put taxes on the food of the people and Lloyd George can be pointed to as a man who did something to prevent him committing this crime.

An old Warringtonian passed away yesterday in the person of Mrs Martha Mather, 23, Rolleston-street The deceased, who was 82 years of age, was born in Church-street and had lived in Warrington all her life. Ask for Whitbread's Famous Ales and Stouts in screw-bottles.—Sole agent, PETER LEIGH, Horse- market st.—Advt. Sale of Corporation Surplus Lands. At the Blue Bell Hotel, on Wednesday, Mr A H Clive, on behalf of the Corporation, offered for sale by auction several lots of surplus lands which remain on their hands after carrying out street improvements. Only two lots were disposed of. One adjoining the Feathers' Hotel, in Bridge-street, Was sold to Messrs Grecnall, Whitley and Co., for £1,410. The other at the corner of Howley-lane and Church- street, fetched £305, the purchaser being Mr A J Willett, Since the sale there have been several inquiries as to the other lots offered for sale.

Warrington Co-Operative Society Limited. The 208th quarterly meeting of the WarrinBton E.l. Co-operative Society was held in the Co-opera- tive Balt on Monday evening. Mr. Wadsworth presided and there were about 120 members present. A vote of sympathy was passed with the wife and family of the late Mr. Bower who had been for many years on the committee, and whose term of office expired with this sleeting. Mr. Bower had been taken sud- denly ill early on Sunday morning and had passed away within a few hours. After the election of scrutineers the report of the committee was read by the Secretary showing that the sales for the "three months had reached a total of 5:77,058, an increase of £14,211 over the corresponding quarter, and bringing the total increase for the 12 months up to date to £36,755, and the total sales to £203,060. An interim dividend of 3s. 2d. in the 1. was suggested. The membership of the society had increased by 354 on the quarter and stood at 12,346. The President move the adoption of the report in a brief speech, giving some striking features of the progress of the Society during the past 10 years and the report, together with the suggested interim dividend, was carried unanimously. The re-election unopposed of the president was next notified, Mr J W Bouehier who had been nominated having withdrawn for business and personal reasons. Mr J Green Chappell was re-elected auditor for the ensuing 12 months. The meeting decided that the Walking Day trip elould he run to London. The question had been raised by a letter from a correspondent as to some recognition being made by the society of the services of the late Mr F Monks, who has been connected with the society in its earlier years and had acted as its first Secretary. It was resolved" that we place on record on the minutes of the Society our appreciation of the services rendered by Mr Monks to the society." A suggestion had been made by the Committee for the erection of a Memorial Tablet but this did not meet with the favour of the meeting. Election of the members to fill three vacancies on the c.ommittee resulted in Mr Allen„Mr H Sinclair, and Mrs Maybury being placed upon the Board. The secreta-y gave a brief report of the work done up to the present by the ißenevolent Fund Committee.

LEIGH, Horse- market SL—A dvt. A Publican's Failure. At the Bankruptcy Court yesterday, before Mr Registrar Ridgway, Henry Hurst, formerly licensee of The D...ck Hotel, Weston Point, Runcorn, appeared for his examination in bankruptcy. Statement of affairs showed liabilities of £497 1.4 s 10d and assets amounting to £lll Os 4d, leaving a deficiency of £356 14s 03. Debtor attributed his failure to lack of capital, decline in turnover owing to bad trade and his own ill-health during the whole of this year. The liabilities included .£2OO borrowed from his sister for which she held a valuable life policy. He had already offered a composition of 10s in the S., to his creditors and that offer still held good. Time examination was closed.

The Philomathic Society. An interesting and vigorous debate on the subject of "Should the Parliamentary Franchise be extended to women ? " took place at the fortnightly meeting of The Warrington Philomathic Society, held at the Old Acadatny, Bridge Foot, on Wednesday night. The President (Mr James Ball) was in the chair and there was a good attendance of members. Mr A H Rathbone, who took the affirmative, pointed out that the extension of the Franchise was already past the experimental stage in several countries and had proved successful, but in Britain, in spite of numerous extensions of late years to males—with beneficial results, woman was still debarred from giving expression to her views by the means of the parlia- mentary vote. They were in many instances taxpayers, were bound by the laws of the country, entered numerous professions, and undertook various public duties, yet the all important item of citizenship was denied them. Mr Rathbone criticised fully the physical, intellectual and sentimental objections raised by the anti-suffragists, and submitted that no longer could the vote be refused on any logical grounds. The negative was taken by Mr N Neild, who opposed the giving of votes on the grounds that women were notoriously bad organizers, too prone to hysteria, and lacking in a sense of proportion. He contended that woman, controlled more ty impulse or instinct than reason, was totally unfitted to be enfranchised, and comidered that the evils of this attitude of Suffragism and Feminism would not lead to equalization but to demoralization, to the destruction of society. A keen discussion followed, in which Messrs E G Burgess, J Walsh, Dr Mellor, H H Taylor, and T E Knowles, took part. Messrs Rathbone and Nield suitably replied, and on the opinion of the meeting being taken the result was a victory for the negative by two votes.

i Wesleyan Temperance Rally. The new " Methodist Temperance Movement formed the subject of an address by the Rev. Henry Carter, of London (Connexional Temperance secretary) at the Bewsey-road, Wesleyan Church last night. There was a good attendance, presided over by Mr J. G. Withinshaw. The rev, gentleman pointed out that the movement had already been started in the War- rington Wesleyan Methodist Circuit, branches having been established at Penketh and Latchford and as a result of last night's meeting branches were to be established in other churches of the circuit. The objects of the League were to enroll. Methodists,young and old, as abstainers and to give to all an opportunity of re-affirming their pledges. The League was being founded with great success in many parts of the county, over 1,200 branches having been already established.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS. ENTERTAINING FRIENDS AT THE WORKHOUSE.

Lively Debate.

The monthly meeting of the Board of Guardians was 'held on Wednesday in the Board Room at the Work- house. Mr EL Bent (presiding), Messrs A J Jolley, W Farness, A Brooks, W C Brooks, W Gill, J Backhouse,W Houghton J Billinge, 0 Mather, W Flanagan, J A Hughes, It Lee, J A Rusnworth, Dr Murray, Heys A M Mitchell and T R Matthews, Mrs Simpson, Mrs Collingwood, Miss Broadbent, Mr A Bottomley (clerk), Mr R I Partridge (assistant clerk), Mr C E Lawrence (superintendent of Padgate Schools), Mr W J Brown (master of the Workhouse). CHRISTMAS EXTRAS. The CLERK read out the Christmas extras which the committee had recommended. The supplies included beer, whereupon the Rev Mr-rcitett. asked the Chairman for the quantity to be supplied He understood beer was only to be supplied to a few of the inmates. Mr G MATHER said the beer would be supplied to inmates on Christmas Day as usual, except to the " ins and outs." The Rev MITCHELL : That is an advance in the right direction and much appreciated, THE FUNERALS OF INMATES. At a meeting of the House Committee further com- plaiuts having been received by the committee as to the conducting of the funerals from the Workhouse, it was recommended that the Clerk be instructed to write to the contractors informing them that in future four bearers similarly attired most assemble at the Workhouse and accompany the hearse to the Cemetery, walking in proper order and with decorous behaviour. At a meeting on November 29, a deputation of under- takers attended with reference to the complaint con- tained in the letter to the Board that when a death took place at the Union some persons, authorised or unauthorised, sent the name and address of the relative to an undertaker who then solicited the order for the funeral. Particulars were given of one or two cases where the relatives had been canvassed by an undertaker before the notice of the death had been received by them but no information could be elicited from the deputa- tion to enable the Guardians to find out how and by whom the reports of deaths were being given. The Chairman informed the deputation that the Guardians had considered the matter on previous occasions but had not been able to find the source from which the information came. The deputation could rest assured that the Guardians would do all in their power to stop a practice which every member of the Board strongly resented.

===== 0002601_19121207_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
ENTERTAINMENT OF VISITORS..

The Committee considered at length the question referred to them by the Board of the visiting of friends of officers to the workhouse. The Committee were satisfied that no rations purchased by the Guardians had been supplied at any time to any person in addition to the amount allowed to each officer and servant. The committee felt assured that the Guardians were desirous that the conditions of services of the indoor officers and servants should be such as to secure the greatest liberty and comfort, consistent with the proper dis- charging of the duties of each and were of the opinion that could be best secured by the exercise of dis- cretionary power being vested in the master in respect of the visit of friends and relations, to members of -the indoor staff, subject to the general control Of the House Committee and they recommended accordingly by 9 votes against I vote. Mr W C BROOKS contended that his charges could not in any shape or form be considered answered. The matter was not discussed at length. Mr BOTTOMLEY said at the committee mseting that he could not answer the question unless he really understood the motive which inspired Mr Brooks. Some kind of reply had been made that not one farthing of the ratepayers' money had been spent in entertaining these people. For a man to say that was simply "piffle" when they knew the true statement of facts. The CHAIRMAN: The charges were answered to the satisfaction of the committee. Mr BROOKS : Some members will accept anything from the officials at any time. The CITA IRM AN : We took a long time to consider it. - The CLERK ; On a point of order I absolutely deny the statement that I did not answer the charges of Mr Brooks in regard to the last item. Mr BRooKS : net is a matter of opinion. I am prepared to prove otherwise. Continuing, Mr Brooks raid he questioned the return made by Mr Brown, the Workhouse master. On Monday last he attended the offices with the object of going through the books, but the Master refused him that facility on the advice of the Clerk. He would further allege that the statement made by the Master was incorrect and a false one. He made that charge advisedly, and he was prepared to prove it. He had consulted Glenn's Poor Law Book, and it was there stated that it was the master's duty to allow any member of the Board to see the books if he so desired. The Rev A M MITCHELL asked if the names of the visitors who came to the Workhouse were duly recorded in the porter's book. Ile had no recollection that visitors who had stayed any length of time had been recorded. The CHAIRMAN: That is so ; the name of everyone who passes through the gate is recorded in the porter's book. Mr W BROOKS said the resolution of the committee was prepared by Mr Jolley and submitted by him and the members all fell in line with it. Several members spoke at the meeting including Mr Flanagan, Mr Hughes and Mr Rushworth. They were of the opinion that they could see no fault whatever in 'the officials entertaining their friends at the expense of the ratepayers.

Mr FLANAGAN: I must contradict Mr Brooks. I asked him if he had any other information other than that which he obtained from the porter's book to which he received a reply "You bad better ask me what I have got in my pocket. In his (Mr Flanagan's) opinion there were persons inside the House who were spying on their fellow officials and giving the information to some person or persons outside. Such conduct was a dastardly thing. 'Mr RCISHWORTII was of the opinion that there was nothing wrong. The food was supplied to the officials and if an official thought fit to give it to his friends when entertaining them, he thought he had a perfect right to do so. Mr BACKHOUSF. asked if Mr Brooks trade the assertion that the officials were abusing their privileges. The CHAIRMAN : It means practically speaking that the officials have been entertaining people here. Mr Brrooits : I said no such thing. Mr BACKOUSE : It appears Mr Brooks is making that charge. When an official bad to live inside the Workhouse he thought he was entitled to as many privileges and liberties as if be resided outside, The CLERK explained that in regard to the question of food the officers were allowed a certain amount of rations. He would like to correct a statement made by Mr Brooks with regard to the refusal to allow him access to the books, If any member of the Board came to the Workhouse for information such information would be supplied to him, This matter was referred to the House Committee, and the Master was asked to submit a return. It was rather a difficult task to prepare, becausethe had to find the people who had stayed in the Workhouse every night, and be could not tell exactly by the names in the porter's book. He had to go back for months, and had also to make inquiries from other officials. The Master had assured him that the return was made out as correctly as possible. The whole matter was dealt with by the committee. If Mr Brooks knew other informa- tion and he wished to assist the Master, why did he not correct the mistake? If he knew of any other person who could come in the Workhouse, he (Mr Bottomley) thought it was his duty at the committee meeting to assist Mr Brown in order that it might be dealt with by the committee. He thought if a Guardian wished •to see the books he first of all should have the authority of the Board. _ .

-. .---_. Mr A BROOKS said the question of a Guardian's authority had been thrashed out at South Manchester and the individual Guardian had lost his case. .

Mr IN FI,RNESS thought Mr Brooks ought to have the opportunity of supplying data and facts. In justice to Mr Brooks and the Master these facts ought to be produced. or the matter should be dropped. He agreed with Mr Backhouse that the workhouse was the home of the official for the time being and they ought to give a fair amount of latitude. Bawe they discussed the matter any further Mr Brooks ought to state whether or not he WM prepared to combat the statement made by the Master. Mr BROOKS lam prepared to combat those state- ments. He added that if the chairman would give him permission to go through the books he would at the next meeting prove his allegation. The CHAIRMAN : It was your duty to have told the committee at the time. . . . .

Mr BgooKs : I was not in a position to know that it was incorrect. Mr W FURNESS : HI am in order I move that Mr Brooks shall have that opportunity. Mr A J JOLLEY : The matter has heen carefully con- sidered and a decision arrived at by the committee. It was a reasonable decision and the7e was no reason to reject the minutes siuTly because of the suggestion made for further information. If the master had left out accidentally two or three cases it would not affect the general principle. As there was no amendment, the. minutes ,s ere confirmed.

Mr BROWN (the Workhouse master) said he went carefully through the books. Mr Brooks seemed BO strong on the point that he had got more information. Be was inclined to believe thit someone was poll- ing his leg. A short time ago when discussing the Christmas extras Mr Brooks told the committee that in addition to the extras ordinary food had been charged. Mr BROOKS : I did no such thing. The minutes were confirmed by 16 voted to 3. Mr W FURNESS moved and the Rev A M MITCHELL seconded that Mr W C Brooks should be allowed access to the books. This resolution was carried unanimously. MENTAL DEFICIENCY BILL. The Bishop of Liverpool administered the rite of confirmation on Tuesday afternoon at Holy Trinity Church. Sixty-five candidates were presented, includ- ing three from Wargrave. Later in the evening his lordship held a similar service at St Ann's Church, where a large number of candidates were presented. Mayor's Sunday. Buckley-street, P.S A.

The monthly meeting of the above was held on Sun- day at three o'clock. There was a fair attendance when the chair was taken by Mr W Sinclair. Mr LieWelyn Cs'4B the speaker. The soloists were Misses JODf a and Wardle, their songs "Nearer my God to Thee" and " Flee as a Biro" being sweetly rendered. Mr H Woolley read the lesson.

St John's Presbyterian Guild. The members of the above Society met on Monday evening to hear a paper on "Folklore," by Mr T L Robins, the chairman being Rev gi Reid. In the lecturer's opening remarks he drew attention to the vastness of the subject. Everything seemed to come within its scope, life in its manifold forms, the elements, the whole universe teemed with legends, traditions, superstitions, the love of the people of bygone ages. After classifying the different branches of this study, the speaker proceeded to give illustra- tions of legends etc. bolding sway in different countries and then to bring the matter nearer home spoke of the folklore of Cheshire and surrounding counties. A most entertaining evening was spent and a general discus- sion followed the lecture.

Bold-street Wesleyan Church. Ile preacher on Sunday evening vvas the Rev Hugh Williamson, 8.A., choosing for his text "He is despised of men—but He shall see of the travail of His soul and be satisfied." Mr Williamson went on to speak of the lonely souls of human history, and the work which they have accomplished. "Vox populi, vox Dei " is an easy doctrine to accept, but its acceptance fails to make men of stamina. Moreover it is not all truth, for the popular catch cries are oft-times the voice of the Evil One. The voice of history testifies to this. The lonely souls of science, literature, art and music, whose wisdom was received with scot:flags, have proved them- selves the saviours and redeemers of the childien of men. So in the realms of spiritual truth and biblical knowledge, the multitude has not infrequently been wrong, and has perpetrated the greatest tragedies of history. To listen to the crowd, to follow the popular fancy is equivalent to shutting the ears to the personal message of God to the individual soul. In spite of what Carlyle said, it is often very necessary to get rid of hero worship, and learn that in the common people there is the capacity for doing something really great. Men must be willing to be Nonconformists in the real sense, willing to listen solely to God's call, if they are to serve their fellows, and enrich the world.

Wycliffe P.S A. Society. There was a poor attendance on Sunday, in conse- quence of the wintry weather. Mr Harold Gray pre- sided and spoke upon "Our privilege to be co-workers with God." Mr Acock, of London, gave an address, his subject being " The futility of war." The world, he said, was bankrupt, because of war equipments. He spoke of the fallacy of preparing for war to ensure peace, likening it to individuals carrying revolvers with the same object. Weapons were apt to be used. Attacks had a motive. When there was peace in the air, motives for war had a poor chance. War often meant as great a loss to the victor as the vanquished. The idea of " beggar my neighbour" was played out. War brought misery and pain as well as poverty. 11l fortune to other countries brought misfortune to our: selves. With our Lord we must work for peace, and pray that peace counsels might prevail all over the world. Mr Joseph Owen read the lesson. The Chair- man led the prayer. The President invited members to sign a petition, praying the Watch Committee to make use of their powers to prevent Sunday trading in Warrington. One new member received a cordial welcome.

St. John's Presbyterian Church. Preaching at St John's on Sunday evening last from Job xxiii, 10, the Rev NV Reid said man was made for happiness, or rather blessedness, a condition in its very nature eternalt and which demanded a Manhood perfect. The Divine wisdom to realise this operated through joy and suffering. The joy of life in every or at least nearly every, instance, greatly outstripped the suffering, but the discipline was indispenable, and the Hebrew writer thought of it as the refiner's crucible. After all man's attitude determined the value of discipline. That of the stoic, regarding all things as a blunder, and harden- ing the nature against it, served only to dwarf the man and missed the refiner's object. Again to deny pain and affirm all disease a delusion was to deny the efforts of all religious and social warriors and the continued fight against suffering led by Christ himself, and led eventually to basest selfishness. The way of progress was the acceptance of the law of sacrifice It held in the spheres of natural science,education and commerce. It was acknowledged by Christ as the only way of salvation and by him left as a legacy to his followers. The end was gold—pure and without alloy, to be removed from the crucible when the perfect expression of the refiner's face could be reflected therein.

Rev. John Yonge at Wycliffe Church. The Rev. John Yonge, for the first time since his retirement in June last, preached morning and evening on Sunday, in Wycliffe Congregational Church, and his re-appearance in the pulpit was cordially welcomed by his many Wycliffe friends. He spoke apprecia- tively of the innovation which had been made of devoting a portion of the morning service to the young people, and in a charming manor he told a story to the children illustrating the harm which often followed from insisting upon things which their elders warned them against. His sermon was based on the text " Thy shoes shall be iron and brass and as thy days so shall thy strength be." (Deut. 33c. 25v). He stated that even to-day after the lapse of more than 30,0u0 years these golden words would kindle enthusiasm in those who earnestly pondered them. God looked for integrity of purpose and to such He promised what he always performed—that according to their necessities would be His bestowals. We lived from day to day in the faith of that assurance. Sometimes when it looked to us as if God bad forgotten to be gracious, was it not a fact that the failure was on the human side ? There were those who might be thinking that his representation of God as personally watching the individual lives of men, knowing their condition and mrcumstances, and suiting His mercy to their daily need was fanciful, unreal and impossible, but from personal experience he could assure his hearers of the truth of God's personal care of men, and there were many who could bear witness of this. "We have redemption through His blood, even the forgiveness of sins " (Colossians i., 14). That text, he said, was the key-note of the New 'testament. There were some Christian people who said otherwise. Powerful classes of Christian thought, voiced by able and eloquent teachers and learned thPologians, told them that the true key-note of the New Testament was the call to the better life which came from the character and teaching of Jesus Christ. Human , redemption, these people contended, was to come through the evolution cf a pure and lofty character resulting from loyal and faithful acceptance of the teachings of Christ and obedi- ence to his beautiful example, lie ventured to say that before any man could truly and effectively follow the perfect example; before he could put himself into complete accord with the teachings of Jesus there was a previous question to deal with. Man was a sinner by his nature. The earliest realising con- sciousness of an earnest soul as it came into contact with God was the sense of shortcoming and sin. The deepest note of the human heart was the naed of divine forgiveness. The preacher proceeded to speak of Christ's atunement for human sin as declared in the New Testament and commended it to his hearers as a sub- ject of earnest personal thought. The communion was after wards celebrated.

Ever since his arrival some ten years ago he has taken a great interest in the church, and his daughter has been one] of our most devoted helpers. ft was the wish of the family that the funeral service should be held in the church, and it accordingly took place on Tuesday, November 12th, and Was attended by the Rector and the Rey A J Bratt and other officers of the church."

LOCAL PARLIAMENT.

HOUSING BILL PASSED.

The discussion on the Bill for the Housing of the Working Classes was concluded at the weekly meeting of the Parliamentary Debating Society on Thursday evening. The Deputy-Speaker (Mr J McDermid), presided. A division was taken on the Bill and the Government escaped defeat by the narrow majority of two votes. Some of the Socialist members abstained from voting, or otherwise the measure would have been defeated. In the absence of Mr C D Parkinson (the member for I Preston), the discussion was re-opened by Mr F It ISHERWOOD (the President of the Board of Agriculture), who confined his remarks to a criticism of the speeches of Liberal and Socialist members at the previous sitting. He contended in opposition to Mr C E Parker that if they taxed land on which there was no property they were putting a hindrance in the way of the person who was willing to build upon it. The Bill was a con- scientious effort on the part of the Government to do something at least to alleviate the suffering and the trials of the working classes Mr G DAVEY SMITH (L Gainsborough), offered the most uncompromising opposition to the measure. A few weeks ago the Prime Minister spoke to them some kind words and said that hard hits might be expected from the Government, from time to time, but the hope was expressed that they should take them in Pick- wickian sense. He was going to ask the Government to accept his remarks in a Pickwickian sense He was sick and wearied to death of the cant and hypocrisy underlying all that had been said in the name of and under the disguise of social reform. It was not social reform at all but simply a vote catching measure. There were other problems which must be faced before they could touch the question of housing. The Bill only proposed to help that class of people who could best help themselves. The £500,000 which they proposed to dole out annually was not a great sum. Such an amount would be needed in Warrington alone for at least half-a-dozen years before they could really touch the problem. Whilst the Conservatives were talking of raising huge sums for the Navy and asking the people to subscribe, yet they were offering only half-a-million per annum for the saving of lives. Democracy was fast gripping the reins of the Government in the country and in less than one generation it would demand more than they were diving in their Bill. Mr A D HEYS (Ind., Leamington) said, in dealing with such a question, great difficulties had to be met owing to the indifference which characterised the lower classes. Many homes were notoriously unhealthy because of the way they were overcrowded. The proper control of housing constituted an important element in the matter. They lived in days of industrial unrest, but the question of housing reform did a good deal towards fomenting unrest. There were only two alternatives in dealing with the question :—(1) The community prevailing upon employers to pay a decent wage ; (2) the community must be prepared to provide decent houses for the people, even though Bt a loss. He was of the opinion that the latter course would be adopted in the country.

The Rev Dr MELLOR (Socialist,lludderstleld) thought that the limitations of party politics were the curse of the whole political situation, and these limitations had seriously hampered the Government in the formulating of this measure. Be felt sure the introducer would have said more had he dared, but he had a feeling that party traditions had to be main- tained. The continued presence of the caucus and party tradition was utterly stilling the free expression of opinion. He did not blame the President of the Local Government Board ; he rather nitied him. and he seriously invited him to cross the floor of the House. (Laughter.) The Bill, in his opinion, suggested that there vsas a class which could confer benefits upon some other class beneath it. It seemed to be the idea of the Conservative and Liberal parties that if they only pot the workers of the country into better dwel- lings they would become contented, which meant that capitalists hoped by better housing, clothing and feed- ing and insurance against sickness, to secure a healthier and more efficient set of wage slaves. (Socialist applause.) No legislation would be satisfactory unless it attacked the economic system at its roots. A Bill which would directly control industry by the workers they as Socialists would work, but any other legislation they regarded with suspicion. Discontent was born not simply because of a distaste for the slums, a dislike tor poverty, but because of a growing sense of the fundament,l wrongfulness and injustice of our present economical system. God forbid that any sort of legislation should make the workers contented. What they wanted was more discontent. MrJL WARBURTON (C., West Bristol) confessed that it would require a lawyer to declare whet the Bill meant. He would prefer it more substantial measure. Mr E HANDLES (80C', Jarrow), remarked that the Liberal and Tories were to blame for the conditions the people were living in to-day. Tne builder deserved flogging for erecting such houses. The object of the Bill was not that the Government wanted to make people cleaner but was simply to make more dividends for the capitalists.

• Mr P BRisav (L,St Pancras),said so far as the Liberal Party was concerned all remedial measures were not introduced with the idea of contenting the incrividual, but that he was to get justice and more and better results for the labours he put forword and for his reproductions. He agreed with the suggestion that dis- content necessitated remedial legislation. One of the Socialist speakers bad put forward the contention that the owner of the slum property ought to be penalised instead of compensated. He would to recall a statement of Mr Joseph Chamberlain who Once said that a man who owned insanitary property and who had had notice to improve it and disregarded that notice, had no more right to com- pensation than a man in possesssion of bad meat which bad to be destroyed. The measure before the House was really to amend the Bill of 1890 which was intro- by a Conservative Government when there was no nasty itadizal House of Lords to bother them. (Laughter.) It went through the House of Commons but it had never been taken up by any municipality in Great Britain. Talk about g iillotinine measures !Chat Bill ought to have been strangled before it went up to the House of Lords. (Applause). Mr C DUKES (Soo., West Ham.) said be was afraid that in the past they had suffered from the co-opera- tion of the landowners and the authorities. They bad also too many j.trry builders, who had caused the slum dwellings. Individualism had failed to provide healthy conditions for the workers. The upper classes were afraid of discontent and they were prepared to throw any kind of doles to the workingclasses. In the amendment to the Bill suggested by the Socialists, it was recommended that where local housing com- mittees were established that only trade unionists should be eligible for election. Other people did not understand the requirements of the workers as those men did. -

Mr J MCDERMID (Cons., Paisley), who vacated the Speaker's chair to address the House, said there was a good deal of misconception with regard to the question of compensation. The Bill did not propose to compen- sate the owner of insanitary dwellinEs unless the build- ing area after demolition was left for the use of the community. The Socialist party had no practical remedy for the evils which existed ; when it came to putting these ideals into practice they were sadly deficient.

Councillor HAWTHORN (Lib., Montrose) remarked at the outset that they were not discussing Socialism and remarks to that effect ought to be left for a more opportune time. He thought the Bill would not fulfil the desires which the promoters had at heart. Such a reform would Dot be settled by the expendtiure of half a million of money. It was a question which went back for manyiyears in the history of this country slum dwellings had arisen owing to the inadequate knowledge of the people who built them. They had advanced in their knowledge with re:;ard to building and that would ..ave an effect on the housing question in thJ future. There was a tendency at the present time for people to move out of the town into the country. The Insurance Am would have an important bearing on the question of housing reform. After Mr NIELD (President of the Local Government Board) had replied to the discussion a division was taken and resulted as follows, 15 for and 13 against.
