GAS PROFITS AND RATES. MANCHESTER ENGINEERS' VISIT TO ST. HELENS.

The quarterly meeting of the Manchester District Institution of Gas Engineers was held on Saturday at St Helens,where they were received in the Gasworks by the president, Mr S Glover, gas engineer of St Helens. Tbe members inspected with considerable interest the latest installation of the Glover-West vertical retorts for the carbonisation of coal, a system which has lately been adopted by many large corporations and gas companies in the United Kingdom and abroad. Subsequently they were entertained to luncheon at the Town Hall, where they were officially received by the Mayor, Councillor J Crooks' 3.1?., who is the chair- man of the Gas Committee. His Worship said the Manchester District Institution of Gas Engineers had been of great benefit not only to the members, but to the community at large. Notwithstanding the keen competition that gas had to contend with it was still well able to hold its own, but it behoved those inter- sated in gas to be on the alert. Ile concluded by pro- posing the toast of the Institution to which Mr Glover responded. Mr J C Belton of Chester, proposed the toast of "The St Helene Gas Committee,, 'and remarked that in 1882 they were carbonising 12,000 tons of coal in St. Helens, but this year they carbonised 50,000 tons. The price of gas was 3s 2d per 1,000 cubic feet, and to-day is 11_1(1. The consumers of that day, out of a popula- tion of 60,000, were only 4.46 per cent., but to-day out of a population of 96,000 they were 1823 per cent —17,500 against 2,600. Those figures were a record of magnificent progress. St. Helens was to-day supplying cheap gas of a good quality, and at the same time making handsome contributions to the relief of the rates. He was glad to say those contributions did not come to the same proportion as in other corporate towns, where practically the whole of the gas profits were appropriated to the relief of rates. SCAFFOLDING FATALITY. INQUEST AND VERDICT.

An inquest was held at the Coronet's Court on Tues- day by Mr Brighouse, into the circumstances of the (loath of Henry Kettle, aged 32, boilermaker's labourer, 28, Laira-street, who died in the Infirmary on Friday of last week, as the result of a scaffolding accident at the works of Itlessrs J Croatiald and Sons Ltd.

Mr Pollard, ELM. Inspector of Factories, C 170.9 °resent Mr A Browne appeared on behalf of Messrs E Forshaw and Co. Ltd., by whom Kettle was employed, and Mr T E Knowles on behalf of Mr IV S Steel for the relatives.

It was explained by the Coroner that Messrs Forshaw and Co. had a contract with Messrs Crosfield and Sons to erect an oil tank. On November 27th a scaffold was placed inside the tank for the purpose of building on the roof. The scaffold was tested on the Wednesday night. At 7.30 the next morning Kettle with five other men commenced to get the roof supports on the scaffold. They had been woniing for two hours, when without any warning the whole structure collapsed, and every- body with the exception of a man named Yates fell to the bottom. Kettle died in the Infirmary. William Hilson, 34,Ellesn3ere-street, boilermaker,said he was sent by Messrs For.haw to superintend the erection of the scaffolding After the work was finished he tested it by jumping on the planks. He was perfectly satisfied it was quite safe. They had been working on Thursday morning about half-an•hour when the scaffolding collapsed.

I The Coroner : Wh-at is your opinion as to the cause? Witness : The morning was frosty and it might have been that the structure was slightly over loaded. The Coroner: Did you say to the police that in your opinion the scaffolding gave way through the standard which was pieced by a plank ?—Yes. By Mr Pollard : Have you ever seen a nine inch plank fastened to another plank which was only four inches in diameter ?—Yes. we do it on other jobs. Dr Law, senior house surgeon at the Infirmary, said that the cause of Kettle's death was fracture of the spine. Bartholomew Walsh, general labourer, who assisted in the erection of the scaffold, stated that when asked his opinion of it he remarked that it was a nice piece of work, but in his mind the utmost support ought to have reached the top instead of being pieced, or it should have been lashed instead of being nailed. He also said it was Ifilson who was going to the top, and not himself. On Thursday he was working at the bottom of the scaffold when he heard a noise, and look- ing up saw the centre giving way through one of the uprights breaking where it had been pieced. He bad doubts of the scaffold from the first, and when not forced to go on the weak part, kept as far away from it as possible. Another workman named Yates said when the scaffold was erected on Wednesday night it was quite safe. The Coroner said the weak points a bout the scaffold was that the pole, not being high enough was elongated by a piece of wood of a different character. The scaffold collapsed unaccountably for no reason what- ever or because it was not constructed properly. The jury ret u.ned a verdict that death was caused accidently through the upright giving way. They expressed the opinion that the upright ought to have been lashed with a rope as well as nailed.

After an existence of one hundred years, the " new " friendly society at Halherton has come to an end. The sixteen remaining members received ten guineas each from the funds that remained.

After living together happily for over twelve months in one kennel at a Belvedere (Kent) hotel, a family consisting of a spaniel, wirehaired terrier, a black cat, four pigeons, a bantam cock, and n tortoise, has been dissolved, the spaniel killing the birds.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

A KEEN "BOBBY."

With respect to the Chief Constable's suggestion as to the special training, of his men,not merely in police duties, but in legal questions, may I give a leaf or two out of the book of my own experience? When I lived in the West Riding of Yorkshire, a young fellow who had joined the county police was sent from headquarters to the town in which I was stationed. I soon had occasion to "take knowledge " of him. As I have previously stated in this column, I have personally known as constables quite a number of men who now occupy responsible and lucrative posts in connection with important constabulary forces ; and the recruit now in question displayed the characteristics of the most successful of these He took his profession very seriously, and it was plain that he was destined to make steady headway. The outward and visible signs of his grim determination were sometimes rather amusing. Once I met him bearing along in evident triumph an old copy of Stone's 'Justices' Manual," with which a friendly local solicitor had enriched his store. Observ- ing high glee, I pointed to the comparative age of the edition. " Oh, yes," said the p siiessor of the prize, "it's a bit out of date, of course—l couldn't expect to be presented with one of the latest ; but quite a lot of it must still be good law, and when I'm off duty and at liberty t sit quietly by the fire at home I shall enjoy studying this! " After all, the more a policeman knows of such matters, the less likely he is to be betrayed into serious indiscretion when called upon to act on his own initiative in some sudden emergency. PROFESSIONAL JEALOUSY. The late superintendent of the Wakefield division, now retired on superannuation and resident in Southport, was an officer of that stamp. When I made his acquaintance he had the rank of sergeant, being first in the office and afterwards on an outside section. Whatever he could lay hands on that was calculated to be of service to one in his position he eagerly devoured; EO that when he became superin- tendent, and had occasion to cross words with legal gentlemen in court, he generally contrived to hold his own. He took especial care indeed, to have the latest law at his finger ends, for he had a standing order with the King's printers to send him a copy of every new Act of a character requiting police administration. Once, in my hearing, an old advocate who had long practised in that Court, and who was defending a prisoner against a police charge, interposed with a very plausible technical objection. The superintendent promptly rose to cite fatal contrary authoi ity, and the disgusted lawyer, anticipating the result, answered, "Oh, y u'll pass your final yet." THE ROAD HOG. The fact that the scorching motorist has been so much with us of late leads me further to remark how the same police superintendent had that tr mblesorne customer hoist with his own petard. The authorities of a borough , within the area over wh:ch he had con- trol decided to apply for powers to impose a speed limit upon drivers of automobiles within their boun- daries. This was strongly restnti d by one or two wealthy local motorists, and at the public inquiry which ensued they were actively assisted by the big guns cf the powerful national union of which they were members and succteded in defeating the attempt of the Corporation. I asked the supernitt ndelit of p 'lice what he thought of this, and he assured me be was heartily glad of it. He went on to explain that, while proof of precise speed might conceivably be very difficult, it was always open to charge an offender with driving to the danger of the public, aid, that being in every way preferable from the point of view of the police, he was unfeignedly delighted by the prospect of compulsory retention of the old form. Prosecutions soon followed, with invariable success. COOL AUDACITY. This was more than the motorists had bargained for. What they had really, though of course not con- fessedly, contended for, was liberty to lush to and fro with the velocity of express trains, using the King's highway as a racing track, and involving us perpetu- ally in the perils of a railway level crossing. The magistrates and local gentry in general had been accustomed more or less liberally to respond to an annual appeal made by the police on behalf of one of their chat itable institutions, and when a change occurred in the suptrintendency of that division— bye-the-bye I am not now nor have I for a while been writing of Wakefield - the road hogs, evidently believing in the absolute power of money bags, thought to corrupt the police and by undue influence prevent further C tart proceedings so far as they were con- cerned. The constable on the beat of one of these called for the customary guinea subscr:ption to the police orphanage. It was handed to him with the remark that the donor gave it on the understanding that if the constable should happen at any time to see him motoring at a speed which he might deem rather txcessive, he was not to take official notice of the circumstance ! The man in blue very properly declined to accept it on any such conditions; he civilly thanked the supposed "gentleman," discreetly adding that he must report to his superior the terms accompanying the offer. It is hardly necessaly to state that the subscription has not since been asked for. A LOCAL SCANDAL. Speaking to a vote of courtesy at last week's gather- ing of young folk at the "Gym." Mr. J. Moore Murray took occasion to call public attention to and to protest against the worse thin foolish horeeplay in which certain classes of Warrington lads and lasses systematically indulge in Sankey-street on Sunday nights. That which so sorely vexes and perplexes Mr Murray has equally puzzled social reformers and lovers of their kind, especirlly those who had particularly at heart the welfare of the rising race, in various Lancashire and 'Yorkshire towns in which I have worked in the course of the last 40 years. In the earlier part of that period, those to whom was com- mitted the maintenance of law and order had a short and simple,and at the same time quite ffestive, way of their own of dealing with the disorderly youngsters. I have at this moment vividly before my mind a picture of a plain clothes "bobby" armed with a stout strap which he carefully concealed about his ample person until he came upon a group of noisy boys and girls obsiructing the footpath or behaving after the shameless fashion of their kind. With all the power of his strong right arm he would speedily disperse the genuinely frightened crowd in all directions ; but they did not escape wiihout two or three of the erring lads having received the full weight of the formidable strap on that part of the anatomy supposed to have been designed by nature for convenient castigation. This, undoubtedly did a great deal of good, for the youths never knew when or where the dreaded strap would turn up next, and I for one am really sorry that such methods are now-a-days out of the question. Naturally, the police, who might report them for disorderly. conduct, are loth to institute legal proceedings against these misguided young folk. Still, as it appears to be the only alternative, I think the authorities would be quite justified in making a raid for once in a while. Ido not need to be reminded that, in case of an occasional wholesale issue of summonses followed by txemplary penalties, the 'punishment would largely fall upon parents who would have to pay the fines. I am, how- ever, by no means sure that these can be held blame- less in a matter of this kind. The moral is fairly obvious, and one need not " rub it in." INFANTS ADMITTED. A kindred 'question is raised by the resolution of the Watch Committee to communicate with the pro- prietors of places of public entertainment, respecting the admittance of young children "on their own" to evening performances. I know of at least one town (in fact I was a resident of the locality when the step was taken) where the authorities had a definite under- standing with amusement caterers on this very point every time application was made to them to issue an entirely new licence or to renew an old one. Surely the powers that be have all such things in their own hands. Anyhow, it worked admirably in the case to which I have referred, the understanding that unattended " kids" of 16 and under should be excluded being honourably observed —with exceed ingly beneficial results. MISS HORNIMAN. The enthusiastic and extremely praiseworthy actor- manager, whose company is giving " Hindle Wakes" at the Royal Court Theatre this week, is a highly diverting and most useful lecturer on subjects asso- ciated with the art which she has so profitably made her own. 1 was present at one of her characteristic

deliverances on the East Lancashire border not very long ago, and it certainly was a treat. Warrington is well in the forefront of communities among whom lectures are successfully promoted, and some of the best people in this line have from time to time been thus engaged in connection with one or the other of our local institutions. Perhaps scmebody will take the hint. Miss Horniman strikes me as singularly ready to oblige her friends with a lecture.

A CLEVER ELEPHANT.

There is no limit to the tameness and cleverness of an elephant if he is taken young and put in training. The story which I am going to give comes from India and is per- fectly true. An English gentleman who lived in India bought an elephant as a plaything for his two boys. The elephant took quite a fancy to the boys and so devoted was he that the father felt quite safe in leaving the boy under its care. _ _ .

One day the elephant and the two boys went off on a tramp together. They remained away so long, however, that the father became anxious, and finally went to look for them. After searching for some time, he came to the river bank, and there a funny sight met his eyes. The great elephant was standing knee-deep in the mud, with a happy small boy squatting on either side of him, and all three were fish- ing just as hard as they could. The boys held their rods in their hands, and their companion held his with his trunk. By and by, the elephant's line gave a flop, and the boys crowded up to see whether it really meant that he had caught a fish. He had, and while the big brute watched them solemnly, and they pulled out the line, detached the fish, and then, putting on another worm, gravely handed the rod back to its owner. JOHN GROOM'S CRIPPLEAGE AND FLOWER GIRLS' MISSION.

Exhibition at Warrington The attention of our readers is called to the six days' grand floral exhibition and sale of beautiful artificial flowers, made entirely by blind and crippled girls,which will commence on Monday next, in connection with the late seventh Earl of Shaftesbury's Watercress and Flower Girls' Christian Mission. The exhibition will be opened at three o'clock in the afternoon of December oth at the Parr Hall, and will remain open for a week.A contingent of the afflicted girls will be at work each afternoon and evening in making various kinds of flowers. No paper flowers will be made. The exhibi- tion will be open each following day from it a m to 9.30 pm. There will be no charge for admission. Refreshments will be provided at moderate charges, and there will be musics each afternoon and evening. A correspondent ss rites :—" Amongst all the innumerable , organisations IA bleb are doing noble work in the cause ' of philanthropy to-day, there is none, we think, of a more practical character than Mr Groom's splendid Industrial Training Homes for the afflicted, blind and crippled girls of the United Kingdom. How to provide for the blind, crippled, or otherwise afflicted of the population, especially of the gentler sex, and of the lowest possible rank of society, is obviously a difficult problem. The Crippleage solves it. It takes these unfortunate girls under its wing, gives them a home and elevating surroundings, and teaches them a useful and refining business, namely, that of the manufacture of artificial flowers. At the present moment there are three hundred of these girls in the Crippleage. They come from all parts of the United Kingdom ' • they are afflicted and physically incapacitated, blind, partly paralysed, without legs, or only one hand. They are received into the home because no one else will receive them. Surely such a work as this commands our sup- port." Full particulars are advertised elsewhere.

OUTSPOKEN DOCTOR.

"3.000 ARE PREPARED TO WORK THE

ACT."

Dr. John Esmonde, M.P., writes to the Press as follows from the House of Commons, under Saturday's date :—" The latest and float proposals of the Chancellor of the Exchequer are to be before the district meetings of the profession in a few days. They will then have to answer the question whether or not they are prepared to go on the panel of doctors. The whole future of the general medical practitioner will depend on that answer. "It is all very fine to abuse the Chancellor of the Exchequer for his earlier offers, but he was misled by the British Medical Association, who, from first to last, have fooled the general practitioner. In August, 1911, I moved the 8s tid amendment. The Chancellor denounced it as an unheard-of demand never put for- ward by anyone entitled to speak for the profession. "Did the British Medical Association help me then to get what the Chancellor has since conceded ? After consultation with the then secretary of the British Medical Association who was in the House,Dr Addison, on their behalf, spoke against it and called on me to withdraw it. If they had supported me then, we would have been spared 15 months of political bluff on the part of the ring who manage the l3ritish Medical Association. CHANCELLOR'S " HONEST ATTEMPT." "The latest offer of the Chancellor (for whom I hold no brief) is in my mind an honest attempt to meet the profession. £5,000,000, independent of drugs, is offered to the profession. It is possible some few men may suffer loss, but the vast bulk of the profession will be relieved from the constant strain of monetary difficulties. . .

---- " This will give the doctor a better chance of devot- ing his mind to new treatments, and will give him greater energy in improving the inadequate service which the poor now receive. The medical benefits will be given on January 15th. Over 3,000 medical men, to my personal knowledge, are 'prepared to work the Act. They are quite safe, but will get an undue proportion, of the money, if others are foolish enough to stand back. To my mind, it is a question to many men, shall we save the face of the British Medical Association, or shall we consider the honour of the profession? The nation calls on us to stand by and help the sick and the poor. I have no doubt of the verdict."

that the offender was sent to one of the High Courts of Justice there to be tried before a Judge and pay his costs. The Bishop's power, therefore, was not to be despised, as many of the early Dissenters were to discover. He could harrass them, impose fines and give them an annual piece of unhappiness. These Church trials were duly chronicled, and such manuscripts as have survived have more spice and variety than might be expected from grave Episcopal records. We crave our readers' forgiveness for turn- ing the searchlight upon their ancestors and promise that in choosing our illustrations we will not be needlessly cruel. We quote, of course, at this stage with a view to showing the nature of the complaints heard in the Bishop's Court at Warrington. First there are the Sabbath breakers, prominent among them being John Drink- water who flourished and made himself use- ful in the year of grace 1670. John Drinkwater f,r mucking his shippon in time of publick prayer on ye Lord's day. Next on the page is a case even worse: William Norton for playing at quoits on ye Lord's day. Then comes: William Platt for swearing much. Another batch of Sabbath profaners are thus described Thomas Goolden, glover, John Moss, labourer, Samuel Stocken, homas Talt. Ir, Margaret Alesbrook for loyterers and profaners of ye

Lords day. Very irritating to the Church-wardens were those people who got as far as the Church yard and then loitered or played games, and there is the sad case on record of one, (for his descendants' sake he had better be nameless), who got as far as the Church porch, stood there with his back against the wall, and could neither be dragged in nor put out by the Church beadles. Here is a case almost as bad: John Low for standing in ye church yard and refusing to come in to divine service when comanded by the warden. Then there were the awkward people who came into church but would not kneel or follow the service Richard Cook for his stubborn and irreverent behaviour in Church at time of divine service and his wife for a dissenter. Peter Uns worth for his stubborn and dis- obedient behaviour in ye Church at ye time of divine service. The Register note upon Peter is that he protested his innocence in the matter. When asked by the Bishop to "certify his reforma- tion" he did so; but this did not save him from a two and sixpenny fine. Then there are the phanatiques " who suffer from curious ideas and go through the process of getting them removed by being fined and threatened.

Here are two: William Birchall for saying holy dayes are idoll worship. The next is unspeakably bad. John Norman for saying that the Church of England was not a true church and that the worshipp there is odious to God and hateful to man. .

There was certainly ample variety in the business of the Bishop's Court. We choose a few more Warrington cases at random. But we must not be beguiled by side- interests. These crumbling volumes which rest over the Abbey Gate at Chester are now chiefly valuable as preserving the names of the early dissenters. THE DISTRICT NURSES.

PLEA FOR BETTER SUPPORT FROM RELIGIOUS BODIES.

A strong plea for more generous support on the part of the religious bodies for the work of the Warrington District Nursing Association was made at the annual meeting of that organisation which was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall on Monday evening, under the presidency of the Mayor (Councillor Dr Joseph). Among others present, in addition to a large number of ladies were :—Mr J D Fairclough (bon. secretary), Councillor Roberts (hon. treasurer), the Rev Canon Willis, the Rev John Valise, Alderman Smethurst, Alderman Bennett, Mr G Walker, Mr .3 Fargher, Mr J Fairclough and Mr Daintith. The Maltose, in moving the adoption of the report expressed his great admiration of the work of the association sad, from his personal observation, corroborated all that was said as to the efficiency and earnestness of the staff. During the year 14,G95 visits were paid to 595 cases. There core eleven new cases each eeeek and the visits numbered forty per week. As to the character of the cases, pneumonia headed the list, and they till knew how nisch that disease depended for its cure upon skilful nursing. '1 he class of patient attended was alt( gether rewarbable rind afforded a striking proof of the care and usefulness with which the inititution ass administered. Out of 595 persons, nursed, 296 were housewives and 102 children, or, roughly speaking, four-fifths of the patients benefited. If the mother was disabled there VMS no one to look after her and she required more nursing attention than any other member of the family. An indirect but none the less valu .ble side of the nurses' work was that it enabled their pstients to acquire some knowledge of hygienic principles and home management. There could be no doubt that the Nursing Association was one of the most benevolent of their institutions second only in impoi triune to the Infirmary, and it was the opinion of the medical men that that, institution was of the utmost value to the community. The con- fidence of the medical men in the association was proved by the fact that out of 395 oases only 12 had not been recommended by members of the medical profession. His Worship referred to the great advances which the nursing profession bad made in his own day, and said it was keeping pace step by step with the discoveries and improvements in the science of medicine and surgery which made nursing more complicated. It was a great boon to the poor people that they could be superintended and directed by such skilled nursing as was attached to that association. (Applause.) By the death of Mr Frederick Monks the association had lost a great friend, for it was Mr Monks who was mainly instrumental in the formation of the association, and by so doing he had done signal service to the town. (Applause). Mrs JAMES FAIRCLOUGH seconded, ond the report and statement of accounts were adopted. THANKS TO WORKINGMEN'S COMMITTEE. The Rev. JOHN YONGE proposed a vote of thanks to the Workingmen's Committee for their splendid work daring the post year. The institution was very much indebted to the cordial and generous manner in which the workingmen of Warrington had supported it in recent years, and the amount of money they Lad cen- tributed reflected the greatest honour upon them. Ile trusted that they would do equally good work in the days to come. (Applause.) lie endorsed the refer- ence of the Mayor to the late Mr Monks. The associa- tion which he founded WAS his pet child and he was always devising means of extending its usefulness. Miss SIAssY seconded the resolution, which was carried. NO SHIRKING OF RESPONSIBILITIES. Mr 0 WALKER, in moving the re-electicn of the President, vice-presidents and committee, said be was glad there was no evidence of a slackening of effort on the part of the working MOO. Notwithstanding the drain on their purses caused by recent legislation, he did not Sea any disposition on their part to shirk their responsibilities in conneotion either with that institu- tion or the Infirmary. (Applause.) Mrs CARLETON seconded the resolution, which was carried. Alderman SMETHURST proposed a vote of thanks to the Ladies' Committee: Miss Milne, the superin- tendent ; the nurses ; and the honorary secretary and the honorary treasurer. '1 he Ladies' Committee deserved the thanks of the town, and he looked on Miss Milne

and the nurfos, as angels of mercy, succouring the poor in the time of their direct need. Mr Fairclough fulfilled his duties admirably, and Councillor Roberts had done a good and glorious work for that association. They were now in a better position than ever since the monis tion was formed. (Applause.) The resolution was heartily carried. Mr J D Fairclough was unanimously re-elected bon secretary on the proposition of Mr J FAROHER, seconded by Alderman SMETHURST. Canon Wm.'s, in moving the re-election of Coun- cillor Roberts as hon treasurer,said he much appreciated the work of Miss Milne and her staff. On an average about 21 visits were paid to each case, which was a very large figure. He was glad to see there was a balance in band, but was sorry the subscriptions were somewhat less owing to deaths. He regretted the collections in the various places of worship were not perhaps all they should be. He was glad to see that what seemed to him to be a small religious body (the Gospel Hall) headed the list. Perhaps the churches and other places of worship had more calls upon them, but at the same time he should like to think Warrington showed its appreciation of that institution by giving a larger sum that £3O in the year. (Applause.) Mr J FAIRCLOUGH seconded the resolution, which was unanimously carried. A CLAIM ON RELIGIOUS BODIES.

Councillor II ROBERTS aid they ended the year with a balance of £lll, and he had received on 5030u01 of the subscriptions for this year another £ICO. It took nearly £4O a month to carry on the association. He was extremely grateful, and the association was much indebted to the churches and chapels whose names appeared on their list of subscribers, but there was still a large number that did not appear at all, and if there was an amciation which had a claim particularly and specially on religious bodies it was the Nursing Associ- tion. (ApplauEe.) They had no buildings to display and no means of calling special attention to their work but their ambassadors were carrying out in a practical form the finest telchings of the great Master to whom they owed service. They hal not been forgotten by some of those who had died during the year. Mrs Hartiett left them a legacy and Mc Monks had left them a sum of money which would practically carry on his subscription for all time. The legacy of £l,OOO which Mr Steel was kind enough to intimate to them had been left to the association a year ego, had not yet been paid over, but Mr Steel had explained the reason, which was a perfectly feasible one, and they hoped to get it some time shortly. They had £4OO invested at the present time. They would not be able to save very much in the future as the Ladies' Committee had recently added another nurse to the staff, but he had an ambition to see £2,000 invested on behalf of the association, so that they might be sure of something of a substantial nature in the way of a permain,nt income. ( applause.) The Mayor was thanked for presiding, on the proposition of Alderman BENNETT, seconded by Coon. cillor ROBERTS.

THE DIRECTION OF BRITISH EMIGRATION. According to statistics prepared by the Local Govern- ment Board it appears that the net emigration from the United Kingdom during the last 30 years aggregated 3,406,000. In the decade ending with the year 1891, a period of commercial depression, the net outflow was 1,633,000, and in the IDOL decade 682,000 ; while in the 1911 decade emigration reached high-water mark with the total of 1,671,000 during a period of marked pros- perity. The figures for the nice months of 1012 already show an increase over 1911, and it is believed that the net total for the year will not be far short of 270,000. The outstanding feature of the emigration of the last ten years is the gratifying change which bas taken place in the destinations of British emigrants, the propor- tion emigrating to the British Dominions having grown from 33 to 82 per cent, while the emigration to foreign countries decreased from 67 to 18 per cent. The highest proportion, namely, 82 per cent, was reached in 1911. 'lle position has thus been completely reversed. The reasons aro not far to seek. World events have happened which have impressed the Government and the nation with the folly of passively allowing the British stick to drift away and build up foreign countries rather than cur own Empire. It is distressing to think what the British Empire has lost in this regard during the last three decades. How resolute, therefore, we should be in future in bringing home to the people that the fullest opportunities for their energies and enterprise are to be found under the flag.

===== 0002601_19121207_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
ON TARIO'S CHEESE INDUSTRY.

The cheese industry of Ontario is a most icrportant one. The province exports more cheese than the whole of the United States. It is estimated that the output is over 65,000 tons with a gross value of three millions sterling. The Ontario Uovernment has taken many progressive steps on behalf of the dairying industry. By law, all cheese factories and creameries have to register with the department of agriculture, and the superintendents have to possess certificates of com- petency. The effect of:this is to raise the standard, and the result is that some of the best cheese in the world is made in the province of Ontario.

A COMMON-SENSE RULE

EY ORDER OF THE SUPPLEMENTARY COURT.

By RENNIE RENNISON, Author of "Mixed Relationships," " Georef

Georgina," Zre.

Henry Pooner lay thinking. Ho had been rather roughly used, and for the first time since his struggle he was allowed a breathing space. So he lay, too sore in body to think it was a dream, and wondering what it was all about. Everything had happened so rapidly that he could scarcely collect his thoughts. He had been walking home alone when, without the slightest warning, two men had rushed from the hedge and seized him. They were evidently used to the work, for in a moment he was gagged, blindfolded, and pinioned "by order of tho Supplementary Court." What was the Supplementary Court? He had never heard of it before. He had not been robbed, and not ill-treated except for the grip of iron which had held him at each side. He had simply been led away as a prisoner. In a few minutes a lock was turned, and he heard someone enter his room. The gag was taken from his mouth, the bandage from his eyes, and then he stared with open mouth in undisguised astonishment at the figure which had released him.

It might have been a man or a woman, but it looked like a draped turnip. The head was tied up in a bag of yellow calico, there being nothing but two holes opposite the eyes. A cape of the same yellow material covered the shoulders, while the costume was completed by a loose skirt, which almost reached the ground. No wonder Henry Pooncr gazed open- mouthed at this strange figure, and no wonder that twinges of conscience began to make them- selves felt.

"The Supplementary Court awaits you. Come!" said the figure in a deep but com- manding voice. "What in thunder is this Supplementary Court? I'll be hanged if I'll be treated to any rr:ore of this nonsense! What right have you to bring me here?" The figure did not reply, but firmly took hold of one of Pooner's pinioned arms. He com- menced to struggle, but a second figure, attired like the first, glided forward and seized the other arm. He was hurried forward along some dark passages, up some dark steps, over which he frequently stumbled, and then suddenly drawn into a brilliantly-lighted room. At first his eyes were dazzled, but as they be- came accustomed to the glare he began to think that something besides his ,eyesight had gone wrong. Immediately in front of him, at a large, red-draped desk, sat a figure tied up in black, while at either side of him were figures tied up in white. Thirteen pairs of holes peered at him from these bags of cloth, looking like the sockets in the skulls of so many ghosts.

Henry Pooner began to tremble. Ho had not been a good man, in fact, he had been a very bad one; but he had always flattered himself that he could keep on the right side of the law. He had had a legal training when he was younger, ana the knowledge then obtained ho had used to bad purpose—bad to mankind, if good for himself. This Supplementary Court, however, was outside his ken. He knew that it was illegal; but he was ignorant of its power. "Read the charter," called the bundle in black. _ _ .

Ono of the bundles in white stood up, and in a low, monotonous, sing-song tone read:

" That the Supplementary Court be an International institution, recognised by no country, and known to no local law administra- tion.

"That its working be kept entirely secret, and that death be the penalty for any member divulging any of its secrets or methods. "That its sole aim be the punishment of per- sons who are morally but not legally guilty of crime.

..... 'That no person be arrested until he has definitely proved himself to be a pest to humanity, and unlikely to repent. "That the minimum penalty be death; and that torture be applied as merited. "That the Court keep in touch with the latest chemical research, and uses the safest methods for disposing of the bodies of executed criminals."

When the reader began, Henry Pooner listened scornfully; but towards the latter end he turned pale. There were thirteen figures in front or him in addition to the two at his side, and he felt utterly helpless. He knew, how- ever, the value of bluff, and in a thick, husky voice began denouncing such an illegal proceeding. " Silence!" called the figure in black. "Exe- cutioner,. enforce silence." . .

One -of tho figures in yellow struck him across the mouth, and there was something in tho blow which suggested greater force if necessary. At any rate, Pooner thought it wisest to refrain from testing it further. „ . . . . -

"What a"re the charges?" asked the figure in black.

Another whito figure arose and began reading: "During December last, a certain widow named Smith, resident in house belonging to prisoner, was behindhand with rent. She bore good character, was hard-working when in health. Prisoner turned her out of house, and sold her furniture according to his legal right. Said widow slept in an outhouse the next night, contracted chill, and died of pneumonia in the workhouse a week later."

Henry Pooner gasped. There was no exagge- ration, and he had considered at the time that ho was perfectly justified in getting his own. He had bitterly blamed the woman for not going straight to the workhouse and remaining alive.

"Is that tho only charge?" asked the Presi- dent.

" Ne; there are others." "Read them."

"Ea-sT :ill:I-o. a young girl, name Hellery, drowned herself in the canal. Said girl had been a domestic in the employ of Penner. Through his injury and distnis&d, and having nowhere to go, she ended her troubles in the canal."

Pooner again winced. Ho thought his share of that horrible incident was unknown, and had bribed right and left to keep his name out of the scandal. He had managed to completely escape the coroner's court; but not this strange tribunal. " Next." "The prisoner has a good, faithful wife, but for the last few years he has made her lice miserable. He is not only—" " Are there any more charges?" interposed the President.

"This and six more, my lord." "I think we have had quite enough already. Do my brothers agree with me?" The twelve white bundles bowed, And the President continued, " Henry Pooner, you have heard these charges brought against you in brief. Full details of each are in our hands, and falsehood or denial will be unavailing. Have you anything to say for yourself?" " is—they are not true—not quite true."

"Name any point which has been wrongly stated or exaggerated, and we will refer to our collection of details." Pooner was quite helpless. The only satisfac- tion he felt was the curtailment of the charges, for he had one particular sin on his shoulders which he had long lived in fear of seeing light. He felt sure this Supplementary Court would know of it, and it was a case in which even he— with his hardened conscience—had felt sonic contrition. There was a short silence. Then the presi- dent said:

"henry Pooncr, this court pronounces you guilty of murder in a multiple degree. Legal reasoning is not recognised here, for we simply conclude that at least two dead persons would now ho alive hut for you. One victim died of exposure and the other by drowning; so withmy brothers' permission I sentence you to death by slow drowning, namely, to be placed in a tank up to the neck in water, and left until tired of life or too weak to continuo to stand. Do my brothers concur?" _ .

Pooner's knees had long been failing him, and at this sentence he fell to the ground. Like all cruel men he was a coward, while the utterly selfish life he had led made him terribly afraid of death. He screamed aloud; he begged for mercy. He was as abject as a whipped cur. The two executioners stood rigidly at his side, and the thirteen figures in front looked like a row of impassive statues. The man pleaded for mercy on his knees; but his judges might have been both blind and deaf, at regards the notice they appeared to take.

When he was at last exhausted and quiet, the president onco more turned slowly to the figures on either side of him, as if looking for the reply which had been interrupted. After a strained silence one of the white bags shuffled about, finally rose, and bowed. "My lord," it said; "may I point out that one of the victims died in great igony. Shout., not the prisoner die in like fashicial

There was a half audible murmur from the other bags, so the president, in an authoritative tone, replied: "We will discuss the matter in private Officer 346, take the prisoner away and strip hue in readiness for his punishment. No. 249, be at hand to return and preparo the torture chamber." . . . .

Poor.er again grovelled on the ground, but was dragged out like a dead dog. In a similar manner ho was dragged downstairs and curtly ordered to undress. His gaoler was a powerful man, as far as his grotesque garb would allow him to be seen; but apart from that, Pooner had -had the opportunity of finding that his muscles were like iron. Trembling as with ague he commenced to strip, and his gaoler received each garment as it came .off. The man took all the things out of the pockets and tied both valuables and rubbish in a bundle, casting it into a corner as if worth- less. When Pooner saw the money, however, he was struck with an idea. It seemed to him his only hope. "Look here, my friend," he faltered. "If you will help me to escape I will give you L 1,000." "We allow no bribery here," replied the "Oh, help me! Help me! Have you no compassion? Remember,'. I have a wife and family." " Yes ; your treatment of the wife was men- tioned a few minutes ago." "It was untrue. It was exaggerated. We only have our ordinary quarrels; and we dearly love each other." "She will find someone to console her when you have gone. I believe she will be rich; but you know best." "I will givo you £2,ooo—well, £3,000." The figure again shook its head and shortly replied : Go on undressing." tne weather was chilly and the room was hare and damp. He shivered and waited, wondering what his fate would be; but the council upstairs seemed in no hurry about the decision. At last the 'other figure in yellow came into tho room, and for a few minutes talked in an undertone with his companion, this latter eventually going out and taking the prisoner's clothes with him. The last corner was thus left alone with Pooner. He eyed the shivering man through the holes of his headgear, and although no ex- pression could be seen, he evidently felt some pity, for at last he shortly remarked: "Poor wretch!" Then from the folds of his yellow garment he drew forth s bottle. "Here, take a drink," ho said. "It will give you courage, and you will need all you have." Pooner hesitated, but the brandy smelt tempt- ing, and he took a deep draught. It did give him courage, and as a last hope ho began test- ing his new gaoler "It's no good," said the man. "I'd like to get out of this business myself; but it would mean death to both of Us." "No! no! no! I'll arrange matters 90 that we can both get out of the country. I am rich, and I will not desert you if you save me." "How do I know you won't?" "I swear it. I'll promise you anything. If you will be in danger so will I. We will fly and hide together." "Hopeless!" replied the man; but there was something in the tone of his voice which sugges- ted that it was not utterly hopeless. Pooner seized the opportunity. "I vow to keep you with me, to halve all I possess with you, if you will help me to escape." The man still wavered. "It's no good,' he said. "My mate has taken your clothes, and you cannot go as you are." "Yes, yes. It is dark outside." "It is bitterly cold, and I haven't much on myself. I've no clothes to spare when my uni- form comes off." • "Never mind! Never mind! help me! It is life or death to me." "Can I trust you to pay up?" "Yes, yes. I vow on everything that is sacred." "Come on, then. Quick!" The man opened the door, and they were soon in the dark passage. At the end the gaoler shot a bolt, and the next moment Pooner shivered as the cold night air whistled round his only garment. "Take another nip at the bottle," said the man kindly. "It will warm you up." * Pooner did as he was bid, swallowing the raw spirit more to quicken his nerves than pro- mote circulation. It did both for the moment, and then •the man hurried him away, past some outhouses to a high wall. "We cannot go by. the gate, for it is con- nected by an electric alarm," he explained. "Besides, they will miss us in two or three minutes, and we must take to the country." The man climbed on to the wall and dragged ripener after him, grazing his exposed shins in the process. "Now drop," he exclaimed, as he himself dis- appeared in the darkness. "Steady! It is only about ten feet." Pooner let himself down and then released his grasp of the friendly coping stone, dropping some four feet more—into a bramble bush.

----- -- "Hush! Hush!" whispered the man, "or we shall be traced at once. I'd forgotten you were barefooted, although a few thorns are better than the death you have just escaped." He hurried Pooner across a field of etubblo, the hard, short-cut stalks feeling to his bare feet like so many nails, and causing agony at every atep. ' Come on, man!" said the gaoler; "we shall soon be across. Here! take another swig at this Bask." Again revived, poor Pooner limped on. After dropping himself ho had dropped a few curses into the bramble bush; but he had now got past swearing. He was too miserable and broken. At the end of the field there was a hawthorn hedge to scramble through, and Pooner found his shirt poor protection, especially as part of it was soon claimed by the bushes. The gaoler cheerfully tried to encourage him, and helped him through in a way which might have been friendly, but which could scarcely have been called gentle. He appeared to be in a hurry, and he showed it. The next field was pasture land, and much easier walking; but it seemed to be badly kept, for it was not long before Pooner trod on a cluster of thistles. Every step became agony, while every limb shook with the cold; for even this active exercise did not warm him. Now and again he felt a drop of liquid trickle down his body, shewing that some, ••of his scratchen were deep; but the blood soon coagulated in the cold night wind. At last he could bear it no longer. "Oh! leave me! leave me! I can't go any further. Let me lie down and die." "Hark! Listen!" said the man. "They have missed us. cannot you hear? Come, along. This is nothing to the way they will torture you now. Another half hour and you will be safe. Here, take another drink, a good deep one." Pooner tried once more, but his strength was failing, while the repeated plyings of brandy did not tend to steady his steps. 'The man helped him along and encouraged him in his own rough way, for, as he pointed out, he wanted to win the reward he had been promised. In a fuddled corditien Peoner was lifted on to walls and rolled over to the other side. He was dragged through thornaet hedges and hurried

Miss McKennisome was surprised, for the gate belonged to her pigstye, and she knew that the pig had changed its quarters for the kitchen ohimney only a few days lvfore. "Well, I'll be blest!" she again ejaculated, for on looking over the gate sho beheld a strange animal amongst the straw, far from clean, and clad in a single torn garment. She sniffed suspiciously, for the reek of brandy came to her nostrils, and then she waxed wroth.

Then Miss McKennisome wis overcome by maidenly modesty, and a farm labourer was called out to transfer tho clothes from the fence to Pooner. Later on that day, Henry Pooner was brought up before the magistrates for being "on en- closed premises," but the charge was eventually changed to "drunk and disorderly." Ho tried to explain his adventures to the Justices, but nobody could be made to believe that he was yet sober, and ho was not givesa tho option of a fine. .

Whon he was once more a free man, his posi- tion was not improved, for he soon began to sus- pect from the rumours that came to his cars, that the " Stippleincntary Court" had been specially concocted for his benefit. He also began to hoar that a number of indignant and mischevious young fellows had planned his escape as the chief end of his arrest, and that the whole crowd from the " Court " had been leisurely following at his heels all the way. They had gone by way of the gates, while he had scaled the walls and been torn amongst hedges; and they had remained—at a respect- able distance—to admire Miss McKennisome's morning exercise. Ho had suffered a severe punishment physically, and his power to bully had gone, for he never knew who might not have had a hand in the little plot, and who had uot witnessed his discomfiture. [The Eno.]

'WOMAN AT HER BEST.

A woman can never be sure of having all her physical powers every day of the year. She is not always "at her best." Certain exhausting pains which women and women alone are often called upon to undergo produce a feeling of weariness, which disables the sufferer, and makes her long to bide and rest. These pains, most noticeable in the back and sides, and accompanied by a general- bearing down feeling, will, if neglected, wear down the strongest constitution. They should not be neglected. Many years ago a woman thoroughly tested and introduced a harmless herbal remedy of her own for the terrible sufferings of her sister women. The fame of it spread until it is known throughout the world—Mrs Lydia E Pinkham's Vegetable Compound. Thousands of women have blessed the day they first used it. It is soothing, harmless, sod prompt in its effect. There is no need to fear the return of these dreadful pains if a bottle of this wonderful Medicine is kept at hand, to be used when required. Girls and women who go out to work, and mothers whose duties are in the home, are alike benefited by it. Any chemief will gladly supply a bottle of it for 2s. 9d., or a much larger bottle for 4s. 6d. The Lady Manager,c/o The Lydia E.Pinkham Medicine Co., 42a, Hampstead-road, London, NW., will send it at these prices. Any woman who writes to the Lady Manager can obtain free a private book t women, which is full of certain information which every woman ought to possess.

HOW TO BE HAPPILY HUNGRY.

Doctor on the Workman's "Greater P!easures." " No pleasure on earth," writes Dr. Walter It Hadwen in an article on " Health before Wealth," in this month's "Commercial Review," "can surpass the joy of feeling genuinely hungry, and of being able to gratify this sense in the simplest possible way. The happiness of the working man in the enjoyment of IT frugal meal surpasses the whole world of luxury that is associated with the table of a pampered monarch. " Of Course, I am considering a working man in health and under healthy conditions. But such is the rule with them, and not the exception. Regular hours of work, regular hours of exercise, regular hours of feeding, and regula.r hours of sleep—these together make up the conditions whereby a good appetite and a good night's rest ar.e secured. In proportion to oircum- classes as rule, live more stances, the working regular, active, and reasonable lives, and thus secure greater pleasures ont of them than do their more advantaged fellows." The invariable tendency oi the leisured classes to avoid exercise by the use of tram, train, or cab and the tendency to overwork the internal organs by spasmodic feeding (often on highly seasoned foods) are the chief evils mentioned by Dr Hadwen, who recommends simplicity, regularity, and abstemiousness.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Queen and the Ilospitals.—Once more Q teen Mary bas been displaying that unceasing interest in the humblest classes, which has endeared her to the hearts of the people. During the relaxations of a brief holiday at Welbeck Ahbey, she found time to visit local hospitals, where she trade, not a mere perfunctory tour of the wards, but a painstaking inspection of all cl.pa•tments, chatting with the patients, inquiring into the details of their ailments, and showing an intimate acquaintance with hospital administration. When her Majesty visited Yorkshire she evinced a genuine desire to get into close personal touch with the working life of the miners, and her latest exhibition of solicitude for the sick and suffering poor affores another pr of of her trte womanly sympathy with human misfvrtune. The representatives of British Royalty are never seen to greater advantage than when they are engaged on meroiful missions of this kind. Hospitals are the most useful and the most important of all our philanthropic institnticns. Recent events have in some cases checked the flow of that broad and deep stream of charity upon which their fortunes rest, and acts of kindness like that performed by Queen Mary are calculated to st imulate public interest in these institu- tions and revive support for the noble work they are doing. It is the duty of everyone, from the King on the Throne to the miner in the pit to extend the hand of succour to those who have fallen by the way in the battle of life, and the humble will help all the more readily when the exalted show an earnest and spon- taneous desire to assist.

Shopland.—The great shopping season of the year is already in full swing sod it promises to be rather a dear Christmas for the public, but at the same time, a busy and profitable one for the shopkeepers. They are rejoicing in the fact that people have money to spend, notwithstanding high prices. There has been a great reduction in the number of head of cattle in the country compared with recent yearn, and the' roast beef of Old England " will be up in price accordingly. Turkeys are both exceptionally good and plentiful, but the home-bred birds will be dear. Ibe higher cost of food stuffs has made the rearing of poultry more expensive, and the public has to pay for the increased cost of pro- duction which applies not only to agricultural produce, but to nearly all manufactured goods. An interest- ing attempt to lower the cost of retail distribution has however, been introduced by one of the big depart- ment houses In the West End of London. This is called a" matinee sale," held in the afternoon on one of the ordinarily slack days of the week, when, during certain hours of the day, goods are sold at a considerable reduction in prices. The shop's daily working expenses are the StIDIP, whether it is slack or busy, and the object is to keep going at full pressure all Coe time, so thatgoods coming in and going out the next day mav bear a minimum tddition for working expenses. If purchasers are given allot part of the benefit, the public clearly stands to gain from schemes to lower the cost of retail distribution, which seem likely to be intro- duced in shopland generally. The Attack OD Public Correspondence.—The futility of the militant method of exercising pressure in favour of " Votes for women "could hardly be carried further than in the organised wanton destruction of the con- tents:of postal letter boxes, which has been carilod out in London, and many of the Provincial cities.. Hitherto the few isrlated owing s of this kind havo not attracted much attention, but in the form of systematic) raids, it causes igrtat public annoyance and lose, and melee further irreparable damage to the feminist political movement. It is so entirely senseless. It does not hurt the Post Office or the Government. It is simply an attack upon the public who aro the only sufferers, and as is lesson in the gentle art of making enemies, it could hardly be excelled. Many of tire people injured in this way may he in favour of " Votes for women," but it would be safe to say there is not o e of them who will not henceforth oppose all parties militant or non-militant in the suffragist movement. As a method of converting friends into foes it lb the most effective of all the militant tactics. It makes the demands of moderate women impossible, and if persisted in it will surely be the last nail in the coffin of woman's political cause. ‘veariog of Charms or Amulets.—More than one Cabinet Minister, it is said, wears an amulet for the purpose of warding off evil iofluences. This belief that inanimate things exercise an influence on the course of events runs through all grades of society despite the fact that it has been said to denote the lowest stage of human intellect. It is common most of all among the aborigines of Australia, generally regardedas the least enlightened of all the races in the human family In ancient times, the amulet sometimes rendered a useful service by inspiring the wearer with courage to face and overcome dangers which threatened him, but this much can hardly be said for the modern craze for wearing charms, or tokens, such as figure largely ie the jeweller's shops—for Christmas. Hardly any sensi- ble person will admit that he or she believes in these superstitions, yet in order, as they think, to be on the safe side, they carefully treasure, and carry about, some little charm or amulet. This custom is not only general among motorists.aviators, and those in the theatrical profession, but is also often followed by bauksrs, commercial magnates and the staidost of the staid. The Perfection of this Season's Materials.—The new home-made materials, as well as those of other countries, are as near perfection as it is possible to arrive. There is a note too of Oriental magnificence, and no limit to the superb hues of velvets, silks and satins. Nor, considering the beauty of these fabrics, can they be considered exhorbitant in price, some- times they are patterned iu large flowers, picked out ill gold and silver, or dull metallic tones. Velvets of the richest makes and colours are used for noats and skirts as well as for afternoon and evening dresses. Some of the ntw materials in softest stuffs resembling suede, are made in charming colours. Simpler Fabrice.—Manufacturers have taken no IeSI pains with materials intended for everyday wear, sn" many a homely stuff has become a beautiful fabric. Flannel for instance, has this year, a surface that nisY easily be mistaken for satin,and is so light and delicate that it may be combined with chiffon and gauzY,, materials. Detainee that provide such comfortable Rea far useful garments, have taken on the appearance of richer stuffs and in the matter of colours and designs/ rival brocadca and velvets. In fact, all the new. materials in quality, workmanship, and colour, far excel those of any previous season. Novelties.—So numerous are the trifles that fashion provides for her rotaries, it seems impossible to keep count of them. Very pretty are the tiny fur tiPPetg bordered with tails that just cover the shoulders. small wired bow of real old lace, or some good imita- tion, sewn over with pearls, makes a dainty fasterlinfi for an ermine accessory of this kind. Wired bows JP; lace are seen in every department Of dress. For !los evening coiffure,the lightest and most fairy-like fabri are used for bows of this kind, and when delicatelY poised have a charming effect. Fur collars are seoerin everywhere on coats, tailor-mades, and of cours.e re opera cloaks. For tailored suits, a modified Robes with small white chemisette of some transparent fa e is the most popular. Evening corsages are finis he? with immense roses or any other flower, made of bin velvet or satin. Tho effect when this decoration cove half tbe corsage, as it frequently does, is not artia But a black flower of reasonable dimensions is striking finish to a simple dress. Plebrrio'

TRADESMAN'S DEPART,RE.—MAny local people will hear with regret that Mr Pearcey, the popular manager of Burgons, Ltd., branch shop, Lymm, was about to leave the district. This coming removal has meant that Mr Pearcey has had to resign his position as president of the Lvmm Board of the National Amalga- mated Union of Shop Assistants, Warehousemen and Clerks. At the meeting on Thursday, Mr W H Blant was elected the new president and Mr Frazer vice- president. Mr Pearcey was thanked for hi a past services. THE LATE MRS BRIGGS.—The funeral of the late Mrs Brigg, of Dutton Villas, Lymm, whose decease, at the age of 76 is referred to in our account of the Wesleyan Missionary Anniversary, took place on Friday afternoon. The service in the Wesleyan Church was opened by the Rev E Morgan who was followed by the Rev JEI Pawlyu.A sympathetic: address was given by the Rev J Caldwell who made a touching allusion to the sterling faith that Mrs Briggs bore her Master. tie I also referred to the cheery disposition which was one of her outstanding features and she placed her village first in all things. The hymns " Peaca,perfect peace " and " Dive me the wings of faith to rise " were sung. Mr Robert Leather, who presided at the organ, played offertoire in B Flat' the Dead March in Saul and the Funeral March by Dr. Halloway. The cortege proceeded to the churchyard where the committal service was conducted by the Rev J Caldwell. The chief mourners were the six daughters and their husbands. Mr and Mrs Mahon, Mr and Mrs Dan Brown, Mr and Mrs II S Brown, Mr and Mrs Collins, Mr and Mrs Bowness, Mr and Mrs Day. A number of beautiful wreaths were received.

QUITE SO, BUT HOW? MR. KEBTV-FLETCHER'S PROGRAMME.

Mr J R Kebty-Fletcher,"M.P. for Altrincham Division in the course of an article in the "Standard," insists that the first duty of the Unionist party is "to get the Government out." Then they must "clear up the mess" made by their predecessors—reconstituting the Mout of Lords end properly av3ending the National Insurance Act. In remodelling the Second Chamber their scheme must be based on the elective principle, some of the' members conceivably being drawn from the chairmen of the county councils. The constructive programme proper must consist of Tariff "Reform," associated with Imperial 'Preference, the giving of facilities to the farmer and smallholder to become proprietors of the soil, and plans for the advancement of trade, commerce, agriculture, the mernantile marine and industrial and financial concerns.

The kidneys are the blood filters—the. blood is always flowing through them to be purified. As the blood circulates through the system it collects worn-out and cast-off tissue, and brings it to the kidneys. The kidneys filter it out and throw it off with the waste water, through the urinary system.

Every twenty-four hours the kidneys dispose of 500 grains of poisonous waste and 3 pints of surplus water from the blood.

But if they are diseased they cannot do their work thoroughly. Then the blood is only party filtered, and the poisonous waste that is left gets carried to all parts of the system—

Makes you feel heavy, drowsy, and irritable, makes your back ache, your head ache; makes you feel as if you have heart trouble—

LYIVIM.

AT THE PARISH Cliutecir.—The Lymm Parish Church Missionary Festival was held on Sunday last, Advent Sunday, when the Rev E R Tarbuck, Vicar of St. George's, Altrincham, was the special preacher through- out the day. Rather appropriately, the scholars had for their first Sunday in the new school year a Children's service in the Church. The offertories, which are to be given to the Society for the propagation of the Gospel in foreign parts, amounted to £ll 16s. 2d.

- - MISSIONARY SERVICES.—The annual missionary services of the Oughtrington St Peter's Church were observed on Sunday and Monday last. The deputation this year was the Rev W Mawson, a returned missionary from Northern China, On Monday night, before a good attendance, Mr Mawson gave a most interesting lecture in dealing with his work in China. Lantern slides were skilfully exhibited by Mr H Heaketh and greatly enhanced the value of the lecture.

THE SALVATION ARMY.—On Thursday night Lymm received a visit from Staff-Captain Tomlinson, of Manchester, who held a special service in the Congre- gational schoolroom. He gave an earnest address on The Life, Work and Will of General Booth," describing tbe remarkable process of growth which has been the lot of the Salvation Army since its inception 22 years ago. Unfortunately there was not a large audience to appreciate Staff-Captain Tomlinson's remarks, but he made a deep impression on those present. Mr J Holt was in the chair, and was supported by Mr J D B Smith, A o)llection in aid of the social work of the Army was taken up. - - -

WESLEY GUILD LECTUDE.—In connection with the Lymm Wesley Guild an interesting lecture was delivered on Monday night by the Rev R W LI Whiteway entitled "The Pilgrim Fathers and the sailing of the 4 Mayflower.'" The lecturer told the story of their heroism in braving almost incredible hardships for the sake of their faith, in such a graphio and stirring way that all felt how much we in the present day of religious liberty owe to their loyalty to principle in the face of all obstacles. Mr Whiteway paid a special tribute to the memory of the devoted leader of this little band and the pioneer of Nonconformity in England—Rev John Robinson, who gave up his living in the Church of England to throw in his lot with the Pilgrim Fathers He also pointed out that these were the men who laid the foundation stone of what is now the United States of America. THE DISTRICT ,NURSES.—In order to assist tbe Lymm District Nurse's Fund, a bridge and whist drive was held in the Conservative Club on Wednesdey night. The room, which had been tastefully prepared for the occasion, was filled with a very good attendance of players, who took active part in the games that had been provided. Handsome prizes were ultimately won as follows : Bridge.—Ladies : 1, Miss Warburton ; 2, Miss Rawlins. Gentlemen : 1, Mr Rawlins ; 2, Dr Burrowes. Whist.—Ladies : 1, Miss Rawlins ; 2, Mrs TF W Dixon. Gentlemen : 1, Mr Fraser ;2, Mrs W Cooke (playing as a gent). Dainty/ refreshments were served by Mrs Allman. Messrs F-E Evans and T Ridg- way were the Mo's, and the committee were formed by Mrs Bryden, Mrs A H Evans. Mrs Gotobed, Mrs Walter Jones, Mrs Palmer and Mrs Norris, assisted by several other ladies and gentlemen. . DEATH OF MRS. R. PAnxisson..--The death 000nrred at" Whitefleld," Rush Gieen, on Wednesday 'afternoon, in her 85th year, of Mrs Parkinson, widow of the late Mr Richard Parkinson. Mrs Parkinson has no children, and the sister who lived with her, Mrs Whipp, passed away at the are of 83 last October. This bereavement, together with old age, has been the apparent cause of Mrs Parkinson's decease. For many years an inhabitant of Lymm, Mrs Parkinson's warm- hearted generosity towards the poor people of both the Lymm and Oughtrington parishes and being a willing subscriber to all parish funds, has won for her a wide and deep affection. Amongst other innumerable gifts have been those of a bell to the St Mary's belfry, a flag to the Conservative Club, and an annual distribution of Christmas presents to the poor people, her forethought is made known to us by the knowledge that the gifts she intended for this coming Christmas are all ready for distribution. Mrs Parkinson has only one sister living (Mrs Lord of Rochdale) and at the time of her death had her nieces, Miss Mason, and Mrs Carter and daughter, living with her. The funeral is to take place at the Lymm Parish Church this week-end.

SII AKI:SPEAREAN RECITAL AT TRH GRAMMAR SCHOOL.—The belief that the drama has an educational influence was demonstrated by the action of the Grammar School authorities in securing the services of Mr James Bernard for the purpose of giving a recital from Shakespeare's " Merchant of Venice,' at the school on Friday afternoon of last week. The chosen portion was that of the story of Shylock given in two scenes; the first being that of the bargain between Bassani° and Shylock, and the second a graphic account of the trial of Bassani° and the suspicious defence by Portia. By a rapid change of voice, facial expression, and bodily gesture, the reciter made each character appear before his audience and the vociferous applause that was accorded Mr Bernard proved him to be a most skillful exponent of the dramatic art. Mr Bernard followed on his success by giving a trio of amusing stories" The Enchanted Shirt,"" A Change of Treatment"—one of Mr W W Jacob's capital sea stories, and" The man who apologized "—a lesson in restraint. Musical interludes were given in the form of two clever violin solos by Miss Marianne Hill, and a song by Mr CH S Willson,the headmaster. There was a large attendance of parents, friends and scholars. - -

LIBERAL MEETING AT RUSH GREEN.—It Is with pleasure that one regards the apparent waking-up and desire of the local Liberal Association to distribute their favours a little more widely, for on Wednesday , night we notice that after a longer period of inatten- tion to the Heatley district; they held a successful meeting in Mr Kent's Pavilion adjoining the Farmer's Arms, Rush Green. Mr J Radcliffe of Heatley, was in I the chair, and whilst introducing the speaker, Mr Coup- ' cillor Middleton, of Oldham, mentioned the fact that it I was on the same platform with Mr Middleton ' that his first public appearance on a political platform was made. The speaker took for his subject the general political situation,dealing in turn with Free Trade' the National Insurance Act, Home Rule, and Welsh Disestablishrnent. In speaking of the Insurance Act he mentioned the fact, not generally known, that one of the benefits has already been in operation since the 15th of July last, viz., sanatoria treatment, and gave instances of how this is already saving valuable lives to the country. A racey, yet instructive and highly appreciated address was only brought to a conclusion by the speaker having to leave for his train. On the motion of Mr J D B Smith, seconded by Mr J Tilzey, a hearty vote of thanks was passed to Mr Middleton. A vote of thanks to the Chairman was proposed by Mr I It Barker, seconded by Mr J Webb, jun, and carried unanimously. MAY QUEEN CONCERT.—The first effort of the May Queen Committee to diminish the adverse balance caused by the disastrous climatic conditions which assailed the last festival has passed off in an encour- aging manner. The effort took the form of a concert i I the Congregational schoolroom on Wednesday night when the whole entertainment was provided by the Humoresque Concert Party. That the endeavours of the artistes were appreciated was shown by the fact that after the opening chorus and duel oi conundrums between Messrs Bridgford and Hockney, every item on the first part of the programme was encored. Vocal solos were given by Miss M Seel, whose rich contralto voice was beard to advantage in" 0 happy childhood " and "The rosary " ; bass solos by Mr F Hitcher' were " Drake goes West " and "The yeoman's wedding song " ; tenor solos by Mr G Hallett, "'lwo eyes of grey," " September day' and "Molly dear " ; and solos by Miss E Hutson, " I hear you calling me," and " If no one ever marries me" ` Kandahar," and " The Intermezzo from Cavaliers Rusticana " were a couple of violin solos played with taste and skill by Mr G. Buckler Comedy duets by Miss Hutson and Mr Bridgford, entitled" The modern maid," " Will you love me just the same" and " Our farm," were well received. A musical monologue "The green eye of the yellow god" and an impersonation of Urlah Heap from David Copperfield, were contributed by Mr W Hockney. The moat popular items were those of Mr Joe Bridgeford whose patter and songs in " The next department, please," " Reoipies," in which the air of "Pop goes the weasle " was utilised, and in the concerted piece " Our stories," which were treated to rounds of applause. During the interval Mr J D B Smith (president of the committee) gave the reason why the present concert was being held (mentioned above) and said that the committee were looking forward to a continuance of the support of the present audience at the one or two concerts which they hope to hold early next year. The second part of the programme which had been made short in order to enable some of the artistes to catch convenient trains, consisted of :—Violin solo " La Serenata " (Braga) by Mr Buckler, solo by Miss Seel " My sin folk," humorous speciality by Mr J Brideford "If the Misses says its black, its black," character impersonations of Martin Harvey as Sydney Carton in "The only way," Sir Beerbohm Tree as Condemned Jew. and Gaspard the miser in " Les Cloches de Corneville" and as a worthy finish to a capital concert, the vocal duet "Night is down the valley stealing " by Messrs Hallett and Hitchen. The accompaniments were pro- vided by Mr C M Margerison. FOREIGN MISSIONARY ANNIVERSARY.—The Wesleyan Methodists of Lymrn held successful services on Sun- day last, when Mr W Pearce, of Warrington,Preached in the morning, and the Rev Ebenezer Nlorgan,of Padgate, in the evening. The meetings wore continued the fullosing Tuesday by a public tea at 6.30 p.m. when a good company filled the tables. A specially pathetic feature of this Anniversry was the decease of Mrs Brigg on the morning of the public meetings in which she had taken a particular interest, having in fact, given all the edibles necessary for the tea—the Foreign Missionary cause being especially near to her heart as an erstwhile missionary's wife and the proud possessor of a son engaged on the Foreign Missionary Field at the present time ; this unfortunate occurrence was suitably referred to by the Rev E Morgan at whose invitation the company at tea stood silently for a while as a token of respect for one so highly esteemed. The annual meeting began at 7.30 in the Chapel under the presidency of Mr F Et Harris, senior steward of the Warrington Circuit, supported by the Revs. J H Pawlyn and E Morgan, the former leading the devotional part of the meeting and the latter speaking from the annual report which showed a considerable increase in the returns from Lymm compared with the previous year. The missionary deputation was the Rev. W Dyson Faster, for 15 years a missionary in Lucknow district,

While the Turkish and Bulgarian pleni- potentiaries before Chatalja are still diA- cussing the terms of an armistice, warlike operations are Still going,on. The Servians are now in actual possession of Durazzo, following the ports seized last week, and they have been received with manifestations

of welcome by the inhabitants. Our picture shows the General in command of the Servians with his Staff officers surrounding two cannon and the Turkish flag which they captured. They would not be induced to raise the flag, but placed WWI the ground and trampled on it.

ORFORD.

OLD BOYS' SERVICE.—The annual Old Boys' service was held in Orford Church on Sunday evening. A fair number of the members met at the school at six o'clock and walked in procession to the Church. The vicar, the Rev 0 Vale Owen, conducted the service, and the president of the association,Mr J Cann read the lessons. The special preacher was the Rev G C Briggs, vicar of Christ Church, Latchford. The words of his text were, 'Thou contest to use with a sorrow." ANNUAL TEA AND ENTERTAINMENT.—The annual tea and entertainment in connection with Orford Church was held in the School on Friday, the 29th ult. There was a good attendance at tea which was on the tables at 6.30, and the numbers were increased at the enter- tainment at 7.45. A good programme bad been arranged by Mr Spencer and a few helpers. The first half of the programme was opened with a pianoforte duet by Mrs it U Brandreth and Miss Hill. Mr W Povey, jun., sang " Empress of the wave" and " Down in the Deep." Miss E Bateman earned well-merited applause with her song "Motherland." Mr J Gibson was in good trim • and was well received with "When the ebb tide flows " and " Bombardier.' A few of the choir boys created much amusement with their nigger songs and jokes and Master Hackett was loudly applauded after singing a waif song, surported by an invisible chorus singing "Home sweet Home." • Mr E Gibson sang two humorous songs in his usual style. The second half was taken up by a humorous dialogue "Fortune Hunting." All the characters were well sustained by the Misses Povey, H Spencer and Holt end Messrs 0 Higher°, H Williams, L Cross and W Povey. The Vicar the Rev G Vale-Owen occupied the chair in the unavoidable absence of Mr H P Densham. Mr C Woods proposed a vote of thanks to all those who had helped to make the gathering a success. Mr L Cross seconded and coupled with it the names of Mr and Mrs W Povey who had very kindly lent them one of their rooms for rehearsals.

At the London Sessions, on ,Thursday, a prisoner asked to be allowed to withdraw his application for legal aid, remarking that he thought he could defend himself better.

WIN WICK.

WHIST DRIVE AND DANCE.—The Winwick Social Club held a whist drive end dance in the schoolroom on Fiiday night. A very enjoyable evening was spent. The prizes were given by Nr J Greenhalgh, Mr 0 I' Greenway and Mr and Mrs Gerosa, and were presented by Mrs GI Shaw ,o Mr GI McAllister, Mr U Astnll, Miss Yates and Mrs A Baker. Mr F Uncles was al C for the whist and Mr G Finch did duty for the dance. ACCIDENT TO A 'Bus.—An alarming accident happened to the omnibus, which runs from Warrington to Winwick, on Wednesday night. The horses were stand- ing in the usual place, in front of the Swan Inn, and for a minute or two were left unattended. It only wanted a few minutes to the time of starting, 6.15. when the horses started off and knocked the lamp off the porch of the Swan Inn. When they got on to the main road the horses began to gallop. Their career was brought to on end by the vehicle turning over on its side by the County Asylum Lodge. There were only four occupants at the time, Mrs Sweetlove and her sister Mrs Bent, and Mrs Naylor and child from Winwick Post Office. No one was seriously injured. Mrs Bent complained of a sprained wrist and cuts and bruises about her face. All of them suffered from shock.
