GRA PPENHA Lb.

INDEPENDENT METHODIST CHURCH.—Another of a series of concerts and social evenings was held In the above church on Tuesday evening last. Councillor R Henshall presided ever a crowded audience. He stated that they were indebted to Mrs Savage and Mrs Andrews for the refreshments, and also to Captain Savage for the use of his very fine cabinet gramophone which was so ably manipulated by Mr :? Andrews. Con- tinuing, the chairman stated that it was owing to such kindly acts as these, that they,as a body of people were able to progress so satisfactorily with their work of raising funds for new premises. They had now some- thing like £5OO (with promises) iu hand, and they expected to make between £4OO and £5OO from the old property, and considering all points they certainly felt gratified that the movement was progressing in the right direction. This he assured them I was necessary as their present accommodation was entirely inadequate to the demands. (Applause.) An admirable entertainment followed :—Mr George Gough a song, " Literman Tom " Mr Thomas Leah, a humorous sketch entitled " A Slate Off " ; Miss May Gough gave recitations entitled " The land of story books " and " My shadow " ; Mr James Hudson by special request, recited " The three warnings " ; Mr Gough, songs '• When the ebb tide flows " and " The Miner." The gramaphone selections were highly appre- ciated. Miss Gladys Foden was the accompanist.

OLD SCHOLARS' RE-CNION.—The third annual re-union in connection with the Grappenhall School was held in the large Room, Grappenhail, last Saturday evening when a good number of old scholars and friends spent a very pleasant time. The school managers were represented by Mr R C Parr and the Rev H. V Pigot, Mrs Pigot was also present. The schoolmaster, Mr A Evans. gave a cordial welcome to all present, and during the course of his remarks spoke of his indebted- ness to the performers and to the committee who had worked so well in getting the programme together. Songs by Mrs Wicks, Misses B Woodcock and Amelia Sarjeant ; duets by Misses C Shakeshaft and Thomas, Miss M Foden and Mr A Evans were rendered in a creditable manner, while the humourous element was in the capaia?e hands of Mr M Lomas. Mandoline and pianoforte duets by Messrs J Leah and G It Hale and a pianoforte duet by Miss G It Hale and Mr C Clayton also added to the enjoyment,while two sketches created much laughter. In addition to the above, old school songs were sung in a very hearty manner. Miss G R Hale very efficiently acted as accompanist. A pleasing evening's entertainment was concluded with the sing- ing of "God save the King " and "Auld Lang Syne." Mr Woodcock catered for the refreshments in a satis- factory manner. PARISH COUNCIL MEETING. A special meeting of the Grappenhall Parish Council was held on Tuesnay evening last. RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

At Dutton on Tuesday, Mr J Spruce presiding. TUBERCULAR DISPENSARY.

A letter was read from Dr Annett of Crofton Lodge, Runcorn, stating that he had made an offer to the Runcorn Urban Council—which they had been good enough to express their appreciation of—to establish a tabercular dispensary in Runcorn for the free treat- ment of the poor and needy cases of tuberculosis and also to examine and report upon specimens of sputum. He was pleased to be able to make the same offer to the Runcorn Guardians. The dispensary would be opened for treatment of patients on Tuesdays and Fridays at 11 a.m., commencing November 19th. On the motion of Mr A R Norman it was decided to thank Dr Annett aud accept his offer. SUPERINTENDENT OF CHILDREN'S HOME. LIGHTING OF THE CHILDREN'S HOME. The Children's Home Committee reported having con- sidered the various systems of lighting the children's home and recommended electric light and the tender of Messrs F W Smith and Co. of Manchester for £175 Os. 6d. This it was explained was the lowest tender ; others made on same specifications amounted to £lB9. £2OB, £249 and £295. The tenders included wiring and installation all ready for lighting. It was proposed to put the petrol engine dynamo and accumulator in a shed in the yard which would be built for the purpose. The question of having a duplicate engine arose and after much discussion it was decided to adopt the recommendation accepting Smith's tender and that the surveyor should obtain the price of a duplicate engine and submit to the next meeting.

RUNCORN URBAN COUNCIL. "GOOD BARGAIN" CHALLENGED.

At the Runcorn Urban Council, on Tuesday evening the medical officer reported that during the past month there had been twenty deaths and forty-three births as compared with twenty deaths and forty-one births in the corresp,mdiug period of last year. On the minutes of the Highway Committee Mr Baker opposed the seal of the Council being affixed to an agreement with the Rev H N Perrin for the conveyance of land in Moughland-lane to be used for widening and straightening the road there. He said be considered it an unwarranted expenditure, and not called for by the people of the town. On the other hand, the Council would not spend 'money on improvements which the inhabitants desired should be made.

Mr Gibbs st.id he understood that the land had been presented to them. Mr Imeson said the people were in favour of this road being widened, and he considered that a good bargain had been made. The Chairman (Mr R H Posnett) said that the Council had been in touch with the C mnty Council for the last twelve months, and that authority had now agreed to take over the cost of the repair and upkeep of Greenway-road end Moughland lane. The County Main Roads Committee had realised that the Council were trying to make improvements, and consequently hadlgranted these concessions. The minutes were adopted, and the local members of the County Council were thanked for their services in the matter.

WESLEY GUILD.—On Thursday evening last the monthly devotional meeting was held in the school- room, when a good number assembled to hear the Rev E Morgan 'give an address entitled "The virtuous woman." His remarks were based on the 31st chapter of Proverbs and proved very interesting. Mr Lawton presided.

STOCKTON HEA ni.

ST THONIAS' BAND OF HOPE.—A very successful entertainment in connection with the above took place on Tuesday evening in the parish room which was crowded. The chair was taken by Mr Hallows. The proceedings was opened by a pianoforte solo by Miss Acton, which was well received as were also two songs by Miss Sherwood. Sam Smith got a good reception for his violin solo as also did Misses H Acton, Mather, and Nellie Hampson for their songs. Mr Bagnley was specially thanked for his rendering of "Killarney," Miss Baguley was the accompanist for the evening. Recitations were given by Mrs C Hallows, Mrs White- man, Mr Whiteman and Edna Goodlifle, all being much appreciated. The newly-formed choir under the . leadership of Mr Baguley did themselves credit in " Fight the drink" "Love at Home " and Quarantoma's Mountain " The entertainment was brought to a close by a humourous sketch " The patient man," given under the care of Mr Whiteman by the Misses Vernon. Baguley, Wheeler, E Vernon and Messrs Hatton, Helley and Livingston. In proposing a vote of thanks to all those who had taken part Mr Stannard and Mr Noden spoke of the encouragement given to the workers by the interest shown by the parents and friends in the work of the society. The National Anthem brought the meeting to a close.

PRESENTATION TO MR J HALLOWS.—A very pleasing ceremony took place on Tuesday evening when the Vicar on behalf of the committee and workers of St Thomas's Band of Hope presented their leader Mr Hallows with a long service Band of Hope medal. The Vicar spoke kindly of his good work in the past and hoped he would be able to continue for a long period Mr Hallows had not the least knowledge of what was being done but he feelingly thanked the Vicar and fellow workers for the beautiful medal, and still more for their kind appreciation of his labours in the Baud of Hope and temperance cause. As he looked back over the 50 years he had been so employed for his Master he saw much to be thankful for in regard to work done and the great change in public opinion. Every member of his Sunday school class was a Band of Hope boy and had a pledge card. Many of them, he was pleased to say, had kept their pledges until the present day. This was, at one time, the only Band of Hope in connection with the Church anywhere in the district ; now few parishes were without.

MR. HENRY VIVIAN AT RUNCORN. DILEMMA FOR THE TORIES.

Mr Henry Vivian, ex-M.P. for Birkenhead, speaking at Runcorn on Wednesday night, said we could only be guided by experience in the matter of Home Rule, and we had our experience in Canada and South Africa. The Tories constantly acclaimed themselves in favour of an Imperial Parliament, but about the first thing an Imperialist Parliament would do would Le to grant Home Rule to Ireland. If Sir Edward Carson and Mr F E Smith became Attorney-General and Solicitor- General in the next Parliament, and Home Rule had not gone through, they might find themselves in a peculiar position. The four-fifths of the Irish popula- tion who were Home Rulers might knock down a few policemen and blow up a few buildings as a result of the rejection of the Bill, and follow generally the course Smith and Carson had suggested to Ulster. He submitted that the "Constitutional party " should be the last to encourage rebellion.

TRAGEDY ON A LINER.

On the arrival of the German East African liner VVindhuk at Southampton on Thursday frem the Cape it transpired that when the vessel was off Cave Verde. a first class passenger, a Mrs Clarice Allport, who, it is understood, was proceeding to Lancaster, was drowned. At midnight she called for the steward to bring her a glass of water, and on his arrival he saw her disappear over the side. The vessel cruised about for a time, but no trace of the lady was found.

The Examiner, Saturday, December 7, 1912.

REUNION WITH ROME.

Sir,—As Mr. Jaye grows older we thust hope that he will learn wisdom and grow in knowledge and humility. It is certainly not wise to jump to unwarrantable con- clusions. The Rector did not mean by "Right Terms" Recognition of Anglican Orders. They need no recognition from Rome. Intelligent study of history is all that is necessary to prove their validity. What he did mean by "Right Terms" is the Renunciation by Rome of her additions to the Faith, e.g., Papal Supremacy. On these terms the Rector does wish Reunion with Rome from the bottom of his heart.- 1 am, sir, yours truly, FREDERIC W. WILLIS. The Rectory, Warrington. December 4th, 11912.

WAR VICTIMS RELIEF FUND.

Sir,—ln response to our last appeal (kindly inserted in your paper) various sums of money have been received, ranging from 2s. 6d. to One Guinea, and we have been able to send up £l6 to the Quaker Fund in London.

May we mention again that the money is solely to be used for non-combatants who have been rendered destitute through the war. Thousands of the village folk have lost everything, and as the cold weather is beginning there will be no improvement until the crops of next year. May we, therefore, appeal again ilk fur- ther donations large or small. All money subscribed will be used entirely for relief, as the distribution expenses are met in other ways. The fund now amounts to over £6,000 and is growing daily, but the need is great and urgent. It is thought that many be- sides Friends may desire to avail themselves of this., opportunity to contribute to the relief of innocent sufferers from the war and to take advantage of the experience gained by Friends in the distribution of similar funds in the past (notably during the Franco-German \var and the Russo-Turkish ‘var). We hope to publish the subscription list later in your advertisement columns.— Yours, ezc.,

===== 0002601_19121207_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
E. M. BROWN, Penketh School; HELEN M. RAN,SOME, Shirley, Hill Cliffe.

MISS CROSFIELD ON WELSH DISESTABLISHIVIENT.

Sir,--In referring to Miss Crosfield's re- markable address to the Women's Liberal Association last week, let me at once dis- claim any lack of gallantry; but if ladies will act and talk as men they must sub- mit and suffer themselves to be criticised as such. Miss Crosfield, we are told, " re- ferred to moral courage in which women had never been found deficient." No one, even with elementary knowledge on the sub- ject of Disestablishment, can say that, assuming her to be correctly reported, Miss Crosfield is deficient as regards courage; but whether it is moral or not is, I venture to think, doubtful, hiving regard to the travesty of history and facts which her address discloses and its apparent infringe- ment of the ninth commandment. Probably Miss Crosfield is a victim of Liberationist tracts which she has innocently regarded as history. At all events I fear that she will find it difficult, if not wholly impossible, to support her historical references by the authority of any recognised historian. But we shall see. " What did Establishment mean?" asked Miss Crosfield—" It means " she answered, "that the State maintained the church in a position of privilege and set it up over other religious bodies in Wales." Indeed! Now what is meant by " maintained "? If financially, it is not cor— rect. No budget nor any statute can be produced showing that the church is finan- cially maintained. Further, what is the " position of privilege " Does it lie in the fact that the four Welsh Bishops have seats in the House of Lords? If so, this can be remedied without Disestablishment and Disendowment. Again, when did the State (by which I presume Miss Crosfield means Parliament) set up the church over other religious bodies? The church set up herself long before there was a Parliament. If anything, the church set up the State. Would Miss Crosfield say that the prodigal son's father maintained his eldest son in a position of privilege and set him up over his other children? Was it the eldest son's fault that his younger brother left the parental roof? The number of worship- pers in any sect," says Miss Crosfield, was arrived at by taking the number of com- municants." Well, I do not know for cer- tain, though sometimes I entertain a sus- picion, how the number of worshippers is arrived at by the sects, but they are cer- tainly not arrived at by the church in the manner mentioned by Miss Crosfield. The proportion of communicants in the church furnishes no clue to the number of her worshippers or adherents to her faith. But if Miss Crosfield makes comparisons she should take care to compare like with like. A communicant in the church is not, I believe, quite the same thing as a member amongst the sects. Still, supposing them to be the same, Miss Crosfield's figures are in serious conflict with the figures given to the recent Royal Commission. She puts the church communicants at 138,000, whereas the figures in the lists presented to the Commission are 193,081; She claims 533,000 as members for "the three big Nonconfor- mist bodies," and forthwith gives details which only add up to 487,000, being 46,000 less than she claims! Moreover, she de- clared that " the Calvinistic Methodists alone out-numbered the Established Church by nearly a quarter." Well, seeing that the Calvinistic members were returned to the Commission at 170,617 and the church communicants' at 193,081, Miss Crosfield's declaration is a little steep. Ladies are notoriously weak in arithmetic! If Miss Crosfield and her friends arc so certain of the church being in a minority, why do they object to a religious census? Before the Irish church was disestablished, the dis- establisheris agreed with the holding of a religious census. Why not one for \Vales? Not that I agree that this question is one for Wales alone. - Wales may be a nation but she is not a kingdom. This question is one for the people of the kingdom and not the principality. Who fears the people? I propose with your permission to deal with the remainder of Miss Crosfield's speech in your next issue, after which Miss Crosfield may perhaps feel inclined to make some reply.—Yours, &c., 11. J. SOCIALISM AT CAIRO STREET CHAPEL.

Sir,—l think your correspondents who re- plied to "A Comrade," hardly grasped the , principle underlying his vituperation of ' Socialists and Socialism, and his criticism of the recent sermons delivered at Cairo- street chapel. It is my belief " A Com- rade " did not express himself as he felt, but allowed his prejudice to so run away with him that he forgot the principles in belabouring personalities. I think that I discern that what " A Comrade " objected to in reality was that in the pulpit at Cairo- street other than only strictly theological subjects should not be treated. This is a question, in my belief, which affects the whole of the Christian church, whether Unitarian, Roman Catholic, or Protestant. Are the churches to consider only the best means of taking care of our souls, and remain indifferent to every other condition affecting the body in which the soul is con- tained? Is it right that the church should preach the brotherhood of man while at the same time remaining acquiescent to a social system which violates every principle making for the brotherhood of man? Or is 'A Com- rade " so blind to social conditions that he cannot see that every tissue of our social system is contradictory to the ethics laid down by Jesus Christ? I contend that the Christian church, holding the principles which they profess they hold, should by all means be amongst the foremost ranks of leaders of social betterment. Such per- sons as " A Comrade " commit the same error as the Jews did when they persecuted Jesus Christ for preaching fresh ideas, for preaching against oppression and all that was bad for the general good. The Rev. Dr. Mellor is justified in taking the atti- tude that he hds taken. The pity is that the general public are always prepared to let men who take a bold stand upon social questions shout in the wilderness. Never- theless, I do not wish it to be understood that I believe that those who criticise do not wish for a better state of society than at present. But I think the Christian churches' attitude to social problems is deplorable. Perhaps " A Comrade " will further ex- plain his reasons for taking the other view. —I am, yours truly, LABOR OMNIA VINCIT.

STORY OF A CHEQUE. BANK SUCCESSFUL IN WARRINGTON

ACTION.

His Honour Judge Stanger, K.C., gave judg- ment.at the Warrington 'County Court on Thurs- day, in the action in which Lloyds' Bank, Ltd., sued John Griffiths, Rylands-street, Warrington, and his wife for the return of a cheque for .R. 28 or its value. ,

Reviewing the case his Honour said a customer named Mr. Dale, of Relsall, near Chester, whose account at the bank had for some little time not been very satisfactory, paid in the cheque in ques- tion, which was drawn upon the defendants in his favour. The bank credited, Dale with the , £2B and allowed him to draw upon the cheque, which was, by accident post-dated. No one sug- ,' gested that it was post-dated on purpose. He I issumed the cheque was intended to be dated August 28th, but the date actually appearing on it was Sept. 28th. The bank appeared not to have observed the mistake, and sent the cheque to the clearing house, but, it was returned dis- honoured by Parr's Bank, upon whom it was drawn. The bank returned it to Mr. Dale, who in due course sent it to Mrs. Griffiths. She took the attitude that she issued the cheque as a con- venience to Dale, and that as he had not done what she expected of him she did not see any ' necessity to return it to the bank. The question in law arose as to whether the bank had lost their rights as holders in the value in due coursel by parting with possession of the cheque. Mrs.' Griffiths said they transferred the property on ' the cheque. The question arose as to whether he was satisfied that the bank, when they sent it back to Mr. Dale, intended to part with the pro- perty and their rights in it. Of course, if they had left the cheque until September 28th, there was every reason to suppose that the drawer of the cheque would have had an account and that the cheque would have been met—at any rate, the bank would have had a right of action if it had not been met. They might have handed it back to Mr. Dale in order to have the mistake cor- rected. The difficulty arose because they had not asked Mr. Dale "please correct this error," but merely handed the cheque to him pointing out that it was post-dated. When they did not get it back in the (course of a day or two, they did riot write and say, "Why don't you return the cheque?" but waited for something like twelve days. They debited him with it. On those' facts Mr. Burgis (for the defendants) contended that he should arrive at the conclusion that they in- tended to part with the possession of the property. The two propositions laid down on behalf of the bank were that they had become the holders of the value, and that, unless they intended to part with the cheque, they still preserved those rights, although they had tentatively released possession. It was further contended that the onus of proving that they intended to give up their rights lay upon the defendants. He accepted those propositions in law, and he thought that the question turned on one fact, namely, whether the defendants had satisfied him that the bank did intend to part with the property in the cheque. It was contended that from the facts that there was no direct re- quest to correct the error, that if Mr. Dale's ac- count was debited with the cheque, and that no 'notice was taken of the non-return of the cheque by hirn for ten or twelve days, he should arrive at the conclusion that the bank had relinquished their rights. On the other hand, it was pointed out that by doing that they were giving up a valuable security without consideration, and there was no reason why they should and every reason whey they should not. Inasmuch as there was an error, it was contended that it was' a natural thing that they should write to Mr. Dale in order that he should correct it. Of course, it was an unfortunate thing that they

did not state what their purpose was when they wrote Mr. Dale. , The sub-manager, however, in answer to him (the Judge), declared that it was his intention that the date should be corrected and that he had no intention to give up the rights or part with the property. Having regard to all the circumstances of the case to the improbability that the bank would give up without ally considera- tion that valuable right of which they had been placed in possession, and to the evidence of the sub-manager, he did not think he would be justi- fied in arriving at the conclusion that the bank had the intention of transferring the property and passing on their rights: He therefore thought the plaintiffs had made out their case, and gave judg- ment in their favour for £2B.

On behalf of the plaintiffs, application was made for interest from the date of action, but His Honour refused it.

THE BOYS AND THE CARTRIDGES. COUNTY COURT SEQUEL TO AN EXPLOSION

The sequel to an explosion at Latchford, in which a boy lost his eye, was heard at the War- rington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Stanger, li.C., when Reginald Goulding, an infant sueing by his father, Edmund Goulding, joiner, 136, Oldham-street, Warring- ton, brought an action against John Mainwaring, auctioneer, Warrington, to recover .klOO damages for the loss of an eye. Mr. Dehn, barrister (in- structed by Messrs. Diggle and Ogden, of Man- chester) appeared for the plaintiffs, and Mr. Robert McCleary, barrister (instructed by Messrs. Arthur Browne & Co:), represented the defendant. . .

Mr. Dehn, in outlininj the case, said the boy, who was ten years of age, lost an eye under cir- cumstances which, he animated, made the defen- dant liable. The defendant was an auctioneer and an owner of property, which he was apparently developing for building purposes at Latchford. ' Certain cartridges were left lying about on that land by a man Darned Harry Rowe for whose ac- tion Mr. Mainwaring was responsible. Ultimately one of the cartridges was exploded and struck the plaintiff in the eye, which was consequently lost. The land was an open piece of ground on which, apparently, there had been brickworks, and some of the machinery had been placed on concrete beds. Operations were being conducted to blast out the concrete beds and explosives were left about on the land under circumstances which he submitted were most negligent. The land was open, and on one side there was a street of small houses, the yards of which opened out on to the land. There was a shed, in which, if proper ar- rangements had been made, tho cartridges could have been left locked up. The ground was a per- fectly natural playground for children, who were in the habit of using it for that purpose. On Sep- tember 13th, Rowe, who was engaged in blasting operations, went to his dinner at twelve o'clock, leaving a box of cartridges in a drain pipe and placing his coat over the pipe. Almost immedi- ately afterwards three boys, the eldest of whom was seven years of age, came along and found the coat which they lifted up, disclosing the cart- ridges, which they appropriated and ran away. The names of the boys were Harry Taylor, Jos. Rogers, and Leonard Cunliffe. They went to Cunliffe's back yard, and one of them hit a cart- ridge with a hammer, causing an expldsion. Two of the boys wete slightly injured. Rogers told his mother, who made him throw the cartridges away. They were found and picked up by George Sav- age, aged eleven, who put them in his pocket. The next day he and Goulding got some Chinese crackers, and having fired one or two off, Sav- age took out of his pocket one of the cartridges. He extracted the powder, applied a match and the powder flamed up. He then set a light to the remaining powder in the cartridge, in order to get a "bang," whereupon the cartridge exploded, part of the iron striking Goulding in the eye, which ultimately had to be taken out. The duty cast upon people who chose to keep explosives was a very high one indeed, and he contended that where the work that was being done was of such a character that unless reasonable precautions were taken it would involve danger to others, .a person would not escape liability by delegating it to an independent contractor. Mr. McCleary maintained that the placing of the explosives in any place, whether negligent or not, was part of the normal work contracted to be done.

Evidence as to the accident was then given by Goulding and his companions, and Mrs. don, who lives near the open space. Mr. McCleary submitted that there was no evi- dence of negligence. His Honour : At present I am against you with regard to that point. For the defence Mr. Mainwaring was called. He stated that Rowe entered into a contract with him to break up the concrete bed. Apart from that contract, he had no other connection with

Rowe affecting the land in question. Rowe was not interfered with or supervised in any ;way by him (defendant) or anyone on his behalf. He (defendant) never went across the land while Rowe was breaking up the concrete bed. He had never given permission to any children to play on the land. Before the contract was signed he said to Rowe You must take all responsibility," meaning that he must be responsible for any damage or injury that might be sustained through . the operations. Asked whether Rowe was a person of any sub- stance, the defendant said he knew nothing as to his financial position. He had not made any inquiries as to his competence in blasting. Harry Rowe, 1 Plumpton-street, who described himself as a contractor for the dismantling of buildin,s and machinery, said he did not blast on the land in question at times when the children were likely to be away from school. While doing his work he was not supervised or interfered with in any way by Mr. Mainwaring or anyone in his employ. He considered that the pipe was as safe a place as any in which the cartridges could be left,—if the children had left them alone. The cartridges were so placed that they were not visi- ble, even when anyone looked down the pipe. He had no access to the shed, which was kept locked by Mr. Mainwaring's representative. Witness left a Humane Society's medal in his coat pocket, and that also disappeared and had not been re- turned to him.

William Monaghan. labourer, 2 Matthew-street, Marshhouse-lane, gave evidence as to the plac- ing of the cartridges in the pipe and said they were not visible to passers-by. The hearing was adjourned until .December 19th.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

-- From the overture to the National Anthem there is not a single dull moment at the Hippodrome this week and crowded houses have been the rule nightly. In an effective instrumental scena " A night in Egypt," Alice Raymond and her troupe of able players are easily the premier turn. The efficiency of the principal, as a cornettist, trumpeter and born soloist is very marked, her pieces being treated in a very artistic mid feeling manner.. The instrumental selections by the male troupeare also very praiseworthy, being given with skill and precision. A feature of the scene is its magnificent setting, and the effects, especially in the final number—the famous Tschaikowski overture—are very, well done. Wilson Hallett is indeed a very clever and refined entertainer, and scores a decided success with his humorous and pathetic studies of children ; his representation of a married man's trials with a squalling babe after the witching hour, being exceedingly funny in its realism. A fine sample of equine intelligence is seen in Mascot, the white beauty described as the best educated horse in the world. One really marvels at the varii ma feats the sagacious animal can accomplish, and the fine degree of perfec- tion to which its education has been brought, speaks well for its trainer. Gordon Brown, the boy tenor, possesses a splendid voice, which is heard to decided advantage in popular numbers, especially in " When we go down the Vale," and he merits the unstinted approbation which is lavished upon him by the delighted audience. Clara Coverdale's incomparable eight Boston boss and girls, are highly successful in a refined and up-to-date vocal and dancing act. The simultaneous dancing is very clever, as numerous intricate steps are included. Julian Ross is a clever light comedian, and gives a catchy rendering of a very humorous song. Queenie May is a dainty chorus vocalist. tier songs are Pleasingly rendered and evidence artistic taste in choice. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

Next week Lew Lake and his company will present the farcical absurdity " The Bloomsbury Burglars " and the comedy drama "My pal Jerry." Other items will be : Minerva Troupe of Football Cycliste, including Eddie Hughes, ex-amateur champion ; Fred Wright, the flippy comic ; Monty Walker, the Lancashire comic ; Edie Veno, eccentric comedienne ; The Buster Browns, the real rag-timers ; Emily Millard, comedienne ; Irving and Jasmine, enter- tail els ; Ethel Stephenson, mezzo contralto.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE. " Hindle Wakes."

Enterprise usually meets with its due reward and the proprietors of the loyal Court Theatre must feel gratified at the public appreciation which has been shown at the introduction of " Hindle Wakes " the play of the moment —to Warrington. People have literally flocked to the Theatre since Wednesday and even the two performances nightly have scarcely sufficed to satisfy the demand for seats. By arrange- ment with Miss Horniman, lr E T Heys' Company was persuaded to visit Warrington to fill a vacant date—a fortunate circumstance for Warrington playgoers. Mr Stanley Houghton's play has been discussed the length and breadth of England, and its salient features will continue to provoke argument and criticism for a long time to come. " Hindle Wakes "is a Lancashire play "pure and simple" though objection may readily be taken to the appro- priateness of the adjectives. The theme is as old as the world, but the story is so true to life, the characters move before us in such a life-like way, that one hesitates in passing condemnation even cn the naked realism in which questionable phases of the relationships between a man and a maid are pre- sented. Mr Houghton's play is wonderfully clever,while his dialogue has extraordinary brilliance and fresh nese. The human drama is so virile that it appeals to all sorts of people, and maintains an interest which is positively absorbing. The one big query of the play is : Should Fanny Hawthorn have married Alan Jeffcote? 'I bey had spent the " Wakes" weekend together at Llandudno, and the problem of righting the wrong done the weaver's lass by the mill-owner's son is handled with distinct force and originality. Indeed the denouncement is a tribute to the highly developed dramatic instinct of the youngauthor.A mst admirable representation of the play is given by Mr Hey's Company, indeed we cannot recall any rerform- ance of recent date in which so high a standard of maintained. As Fanny Hawthorne, the pretty, wilful mill-girl, Miss B Fairfax was absolutely ideal. Her naturalness was only equalled by her capacity for emotional expression, and she scores heavily in every scene, whether with her own parents or those of her week-end lover. The duel betwet n her and Nathaniel Jeffcote was a fine contest of wills, leaving the mill- owner admittedly " beat " for the first time in his life. Equally good was the work of Miss Nellie Scott as Mrs Hawthorne. In fact, it would be no exaggeration to credit her with the cleverest characterisa- tion in the play. She was a type itself and not a copy. Mr John Cecil makes la fine Nathaniel Jeffcote brusque, determined, straight,and grimly humorous. We have all met him. As his wife Miss. Daisy England gave a finished piesentment.of the Wculd-be lady—but a woman of human sympethiei for all that. The difficult and far from heroic role of Alan Jeff cote was well played by Mr Bernard Copping, and a word of praise is due of Miss Dorathy Day as Beatrice Farrar. Her trying scene with Alan, in which she puts love aside and breaks off her engagement, was admirahly done. Mr F Howard was most humourously effective as ir Timothy Farrar, and the mild-mannered" decent" operative, Christopher Hawthorn, was presented with convincing accuracy by Mr W H Dewhurst. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME. Next week a drama "The Woman Pays" will be presented twice nightly, with matinee on Thursday.

LOCAL WEDDING. Browne---Welch.

The wedding of Mr. Reginald L. Browne, scn of Mrs. T. Danville, of tt suminerhill," Walton, to Florence, only daughter of Mr. R. W, Welch, of 50, Dudley-street, Warrington, took place at St. Ann's Church, Warrington, on Thursday.—The bride, who was given away by her father, was charmingly attired in grey silk, Bnd carried a handsome bouquet of orchids and lilies. The bridesmaids. Miss, Edith Doraville and Miss• Ada Speakman, were dressed in blue silk, and carried shower bouquets. The officiating clergyman was the Rev. J. Davies, vicar of St. Ann's, and Mr. Thomas Welch, the brother ot the bride, acted as best man. Mr, Milner presided at the organ. The bridegroom presented the bride with a sable muff and furs. The bride gave the groom a gold scarf pin, and the bridesmaids were presented with gold brooches by the bridegroom. The happy couple were the recipients of numerous useful and valuable presents. Following the wedding a reception was held at Atkinson's Cafe,Bridge-street, a large number of guests being present up to a late hour. tinder the presidency of Mr and Mrs T Domville,a most enjoyable evening,enlivened with song ' and dance, was spent. The young couple received the ihearty congratulations of all their friends.

STOCKTON HEATH SHOPKEEPER'S AFFAIRS.

At the Bankruptcy Court, yesterday, Sarah Ann Welaby, formerly carrying on business at the house and shop. of 72, Lonclow.road, Stockton Heath, aPParecl for her public examination in tankruptcy. Debtor stated that she commenced business in 1902 without any capital. Her father and mother having previously carried' on the business. She became aware of her insolvency in June last, and attributed her failure to lael, of capital, loss of business through compulsory removal and bad debts. The unsecured liabilities amounted to £126 6s sd, and the assets £l2.—The examination was closed.

FIRE AT WHITECROSS WORKS. Another serious works fire—the second within a week—broke out early on Thursday morning at the works of the Whitecross Wire Company Ltd. and the damage is estimated at about .£.2,000. A brick building with a wooden rcof, used as a Warehouse for storing wire netting, caught fire about half-past five and before the arrival of the brigades of Messrs Crosfields and the Corporation the premises were blazing furiously . Once at work the firemen soon got the upper hand and the flames were con- trolled after an hour's work, but not before the roof had teen destroyed and the building practically ruined.

Several thousand rolls of wire netting awaiting shipa eat for Australia were damaged.

BO TELE R GRAMMAR SCHOOL—AN EXAMINATION will be held at the School ' SATURDAY, December Mb, at. 9.30 a.m. for the pnrpoae of ELECTING one or two BOTELER SCHOLARS. Candidates who must have been in attend- ance at one of the Public Elementary Schools in the County Borough of Warrington during the last three years at least must be under the age of 12 on the day of the Examination. The subjects for examination will be Arithmetic, English, Gammar, History and Geography. HOCKEY. LATCH FORD. The First Team (minus Knight. and Travis) lost 3-1 against Swinton Park on a frost bound and terribly bumpy ground at Swinton. Swinton got two goals early on which gave them every encouragement and they adapted themselves to the conditions. The proceedings were however somewhat faroial and considering the bad state of the turf and the frost, the match should never have been played. Some of the visitors got nasty. knocks which prevented them playing their usual game. The Second Eleven also lost 3-1 at 14Ome. The visitors scored early and hanged the ball about dangerously, considering the hard ground, and there was no proper hockey. The game was the reverse of enjoyable, as the visitors were too talkative, apparently losing their heads over their early success. Latchford had them penned down for three parts of the game but bad no luck with their shots at goal. The Swinton

with which he had had to deal, he did not know of one which bad been approved by the Local Government Board with the works in such close proximity to dwellings, unless it was impossible to obtain a site else- where. The erection of works on the site in question would depreciate the value of surrounding property enormously. To deal with the three parishes separately would cost much more for land alone (even including Lord Barrymore's gift of two acres) than the larger scheme would necessitate. It would also demand throe pumping schemes, with the attendance of men night and day, and altogether the cost would be thousands of pounds in excess of the combined scheme. He defied any engineer to prove otherwise. the inq Airy was adjourned until Tussday at 10.30 a.m

over rocky, broken grouno. in the darkness he was half conscious of his body being covered with the hardening crust of coagulated blodd, whilst his feet had lost all sense of feeling. At last he could go no further, and again begged to be left to die. "No death can be worse than this," he gasped. "Leave me, and look after yourself." "No, no; there are only a few more yards and we are at our hiding-place. Here, take another drink, and I'll carry you." • • • I . • • . . The Hill Farm was rented and •managed by Miss McKennisome, a young lady of some fifty summers and fifteen stones. She ruled her domestics with a brush-handle instead of the proverbial rod of iron, and she knew her busi- ness. Miss McKennisome shirked nothing, and what is more, she allowed nobody else to do it. The morning following the escape just nar- rated she rose before sunrise, and after dragging her tired servants out of bed, walked out to look after her poultry. Daylight was just beginning to break; but she rubbed her sharp eyes and again looked at her fence, as if wondering whether the morning mist was deceiving her. i She knew every point and every flaw n that fence; but it looked strange in the dim morning light. so she walked nearer. "Well, I'll be blest!" was the unladylike expression. Perhaps it could be excused, for along the fence, on every alternate point, was hung a masculine garment. There was a silk hat on the first, a frock coat on the next, and so on down the row, the line finishing with sundry small- wares like socks and boots. Miss McKennisome took a deep breath, placed her hands on her hips, and pursed tier lips together. Then she noticed that the yard dog was straining at its chain. "tins! WARRINGTON' CO-OPERATIVE SOCTETY.—The Educa- tion Committee of the above society gave a grand free concert in the Wesleyan Schoolroom on Tuesday even - ing. The entertainment commenced at 7.30 and Mr E Realm (of the Education Committee) presided. The Society are noted for their entertainments and Tues- day's arrangements were no exception to the rules. Songs were given by Miss Carrie Hastings,Madam Fair- hurst. Mr J Willett and Mr J Poole, the humorist being Mr Farrington, who caused roars of laughter. Miss Perdue was the accompanist. As is 'usual on such occa- sions the schoolroom was crowded. WESLEYAN BIBLE CLASS ANNUAL SOCIAL—This took place on Saturday, November 30th in the Wesleyan schoolroom, when a fair number were present consider- ing the counter attractions on a Saturday evening. The arrangements were in the hands of a capable committee Mr CE Parker, who has been the leader of the class for many years and is held in high esteem by the members, gave the company a most hearty welcome. A good programme was arranged members and friends giving of their best in the way of songs, pianoforte solos, recitation, &c., to the delights ot the audience. After the company had partaken of refreshments, games were indulged in. Mr G Carman taking charge, and it goes without saying that the company spent a most enjoy- able evening. GUILD MUSICAL EVENING.—Many expressions of appreciation were made by the members of the Guild and friends for the excellent programme kindly arranged by Mrs T A Ambler and Mrs Dodds, held in the Wesleyan Schoolroom, on Wednesday evening. Mr J G Withinshaw presided. The programme greatly delighted a large audience. It included songs by Miss Black- burn and Mr Walter Clay, recitations by Mrs Long- bottom, violin solos by Miss Parker, pianoforte selections by Miss Clarice Newell, and humorous recitals by Mr H M Ashton. The Chairman, at the close, expressed the best thanks of the audience to the ladies for arranging such an excellent entertainment and also to the artistes who had so kindly given their Services for the evening's enjoyment. PENKETII LIBERAL CLUB.—One of the most success- ful dances and whist drives was held on Friday of last , week at the St Mary's Sunday School, Great Sankey, in I connection with the Penketh Liberal Club, when members and friends gathered together to the number of about 160. The evening's entertainment commenced at 8 p.m and continued till 2 am. Tables to the number of 18 were supplied for whist players and the remainder entered with zest into the dancing. The M C's for dancing were Messrs H Plumpton and W Dwerryhoure, and Mr H Davies for the whist drive. Musical items were very eificientlylgiven by Messrs F and J Hall. The prize winners were as follows Ladies: 1, Mrs F White ; 2, Miss A Davies ; consola- tion. Gents : 1, Mr S Kilshaw, jun ; 2, 1' Rigby ; consolation, J Ainsworth. The refreshment; were sup- plied by Mrs George Clarke in her usual capable style I
